## IV A Few Days’ Rest in Billets / IV 在宿营地休整的几天

===Original===
After the strafing we had given Fritz on the raid, he behaved himself reasonably well for quite a while. It was the first raid that had been made on that sector for a long time, and we had no doubt caught the Germans off their guard.

Anyhow for quite a spell afterwards they were very “windy” and would send up the “Very” lights on the slightest provocation and start the “typewriters” a-rattling. Fritz was right on the job with his eye peeled all the time.

In fact he was so keen that another raid that was attempted ten days later failed completely because of a rapidly concentrated and heavy machine-gun fire, and in another, a day or two later, our men never got beyond our own wire and had thirty-eight casualties out of fifty men engaged.

But so far as anything but defensive work was concerned, Fritz was very meek. He sent over very few “minnies” or rifle grenades, and there was hardly any shelling of the sector.

Directly after the raid, we who were in the party had a couple of days “on our own” at the little village of Bully-Grenay, less than three miles behind the lines. This is directly opposite Lens, the better known town which figures so often in the dispatches.

Bully-Grenay had been a place of perhaps one thousand people. It had been fought over and through and around early in the war, and was pretty well battered up. There were a few houses left unhit and the town hall and several shops. The rest of the place was ruins, but about two hundred of the inhabitants still stuck to their old homes. For some reason the Germans did not shell Bully-Grenay, that is, not often. Once in a while they would lob one in just to let the people know they were not forgotten.

===Chinese===
我们突袭时给了弗里茨（Fritz，德军士兵的绰号）一顿狠揍，此后好一阵子他都相当老实。那片防区已经很久没搞过突袭，毫无疑问，我们打了德国人一个措手不及。

反正之后好长一段时间，他们都“风声鹤唳”（windy）得很，稍有风吹草动就放“维里信号弹”（Very lights），把“打字机”（typewriters，即机枪）打得咔嗒咔嗒响。弗里茨时刻瞪大眼睛守着，半点不敢松懈。

事实上他警惕得出奇：十天之后再试的一次突袭，就因为德军迅速集中起来的猛烈机枪火力而彻底告吹；一两天后又搞了一次，我们的人连自己的铁丝网都没能出得去，出战的五十人伤亡了三十八个。

不过，除了守势以外，弗里茨温顺得很。他很少把“明妮”（minnies，德军重迫击炮弹）或者枪榴弹丢过来，这一防区几乎谈不上有什么炮击。

突袭一结束，我们这些参加行动的人就得了两天“自由活动”，去了战线后方不到三英里的小村子布利-格勒奈（Bully-Grenay）。村子正对着朗斯（Lens）——那个在战报里频频露脸、名气大得多的城市。

布利-格勒奈原本是个千把人口的地方。战争初期，这里里里外外被打了个遍，早已破烂不堪。全村只剩下几栋没挨过炮的房子，外加镇公所和几家铺子，其余尽是废墟。可仍有大约两百个居民守着老家不肯走。不知出于什么缘故，德国人不炮击布利-格勒奈——至少不常打。他们偶尔丢一发进来，好让老百姓知道：我们可没忘了你们。

===Original===
There was a suspicion that there were spies in the town and that that accounted for the Germans laying off, but whatever was the cause the place was safer than most villages so near the lines.

Those two days in repose at Bully-Grenay were a good deal of a farce. We were entirely “on our own,” it is true, no parade, no duty of any kind﻿—but the quarters﻿—oof! We were billeted in the cellars of the battered-down houses. They weren’t shellproof. That didn’t matter much, as there wasn’t any shelling, but there might have been. The cellars were dangerous enough without, what with tottering walls and overhanging chunks of masonry.

Moreover they were a long way from waterproof. Imagine trying to find a place to sleep in an old ruin half full of rainwater. The dry places were piled up with brick and mortar, but we managed to clean up some half-sheltered spots for “kip” and we lived through it.

The worst feature of these billets was the rats. They were the biggest I ever saw, great, filthy, evil-smelling, grayish-red fellows, as big as a good-sized cat. They would hop out of the walls and scuttle across your face with their wet, cold feet, and it was enough to drive you insane. One chap in our party had a natural horror of rats, and he nearly went crazy. We had to “kip” with our greatcoats pulled up over our heads, and then the beggars would go down and nibble at our boots.

===Chinese===
有人疑心镇上藏着间谍，德国人才因此收了手。但不管缘故为何，这地方总比大多数离战线这么近的村子安全。

在布利-格勒奈休整的那两天，在很大程度上就是一出闹剧。确实完全是“自由活动”：不出操，什么勤务都没有——可住处嘛——唉！我们的宿营地（billets）就是坍塌房屋的地窖。地窖挡不住炮弹。好在没什么炮击，这倒不打紧——可万一来了呢。就算没有炮弹，这地窖也够危险的了：四壁摇摇欲坠，头顶上悬着大块大块的砖石。

再说，它们离“不透水”也差得远。请想象一下，在一座积了半屋子雨水的废墟里找个睡觉的地方是什么滋味。干的地方堆满了砖头灰浆，不过我们还是清理出几处勉强遮身的角落用来“睡觉”（kip），好歹熬了过来。

这种宿营地最要命的毛病是耗子。那是我平生所见最大的耗子：肥硕、肮脏、恶臭扑鼻、灰里透红，个头抵得上一只体面的大猫。它们会从墙缝里蹿出来，拖着湿漉漉、凉冰冰的脚爪从你脸上跑过去，能把人的神经磨到发疯。我们队里有个小伙子天生怕耗子，差点真被逼疯了。我们“睡觉”时只好把军大衣拉上来蒙住脑袋，结果这帮混账又跑下去啃我们的靴子。

===Original===
The first day somebody found a fox terrier, evidently lost and probably the pet of some officer. We weren’t allowed to carry mascots, although we had a kitten that we smuggled along for a long time. This terrier was a well-bred little fellow, and we grabbed him. We spent a good part of both mornings digging out rats for him and staged some of the grandest fights ever.

Most of the day we spent at a little estaminet across the way from our so-called billets. There was a pretty mademoiselle there who served the rotten French beer and *vin blanc*, and the Tommies tried their French on her. They might as well have talked Choctaw. I speak the language a little and tried to monopolize the lady, and did, which didn’t increase my popularity any.

“I say, Yank,” someone would call, “don’t be a blinkin’ ’og. Give somebody else a chawnce.”

Whereupon I would pursue my conquest all the more ardently. I was making a large hit, as I thought, when in came an officer. After that I was ignored, to the huge delight of the Tommies, who joshed me unmercifully. They discovered that my middle name was Derby, and they christened me “Darby the Yank.” Darby I remained as long as I was with them.

===Chinese===
第一天，有人捡到一条猎狐梗，显然是走失的，多半是哪位军官的爱犬。按规定不许带吉祥物，不过我们先前也偷偷养过一只小猫，带了好一阵子。这条猎狐梗是个血统端正的小家伙，我们一把就捞了过来。两个上午的大半工夫，我们都用来给它掏耗子，还组织了几场有史以来最精彩的大战。

白天的大部分时间，我们都泡在我们那所谓宿营地马路对面的一家小酒馆（estaminet）里。馆里有位标致的姑娘，端上劣质的法国啤酒和“白葡萄酒”（vin blanc），汤米们（Tommy，英国兵的通称）轮番对她卖弄法语——那还不如讲乔克托语（Choctaw，北美印第安人的语言）管用。我多少会说一点，便想独占这位姑娘，也真办到了，只是我的人缘因此一落千丈。

“我说，美国佬（Yank），”总有人喊，“别他妈学头猪独霸食槽，也给别人留点儿机会呗。”

于是我把攻势催得愈发火热。正当我自以为大获全胜，进来了一位军官。打那以后，姑娘就再没正眼瞧过我。汤米们乐不可支，毫不留情地拿我取笑。他们打听到我的中名叫德比，便给我行了洗礼，命名“美国佬达比”（Darby the Yank）。此后只要还跟他们在一起，我就一直叫达比。

===Original===
Some of the questions the men asked about the States were certainly funny. One chap asked what language we spoke over here. I thought he was spoofing, but he actually meant it. He thought we spoke something like Italian, he said. I couldn’t resist the temptation, and filled him up with a line of ghost stories about wild Indians just outside Boston. I told him I left because of a raid in which the redskins scalped people on Boston Common. After that he used to pester the life out of me for Wild West yarns with the scenes laid in New England.

One chap was amazed and, I think, a little incredulous because I didn’t know a man named Fisk in Des Moines.

We went back to the trenches again and were there five days. I was out one night on barbed wire work, which is dangerous at any time, and was especially so with Fritz in his condition of jumpy nerves. You have to do most of the work lying on your back in the mud, and if you jingle the wire, Fritz traverses No Man’s Land with his rapid-firers with a fair chance of bagging something.

I also had one night on patrol, which later became my favorite game. I will tell more about it in another chapter.

===Chinese===
弟兄们问起美国的事，有些问题实在可乐。有个小伙子问我们那边的人说什么语言。我以为他存心耍宝，谁知他是当真的。他说，他原以为我们说的话跟意大利语差不多。我实在没经住诱惑，给他灌了一整套鬼话，说什么波士顿城外不远处就是野人印第安人的地界。我还说，我是因为波士顿公园（Boston Common）上发生了一场剥头皮的袭击才离开美国的。打那以后，他天天缠着我要“蛮荒西部”的故事，还非得把场景安在新英格兰不可。

还有一位老兄又惊又疑——因为我不认得得梅因（Des Moines）一个叫菲斯克的人。

之后我们重回战壕，又待了五天。有一夜我出去干铁丝网作业。这活儿什么时候都危险，赶上弗里茨那阵子神经紧张，更是险上加险。活儿大半得仰面躺在泥水里干，铁丝只要叮当一响，弗里茨就用速射枪朝“无人地带”（No Man’s Land）来回扫射，多半能捞着点什么。

我还巡逻过一夜——巡逻后来成了我最上瘾的把戏。容我在别的章节里细讲。

===Original===
At the end of the five days the whole battalion was pulled out for rest. We marched a few miles to the rear and came to the village of Petite-Saens. This town had been fought through, but for some reason had suffered little. Few of the houses had been damaged, and we had real billets.

My section, ten men besides myself, drew a big attic in a clean house. There was loads of room and the roof was tight and there were no rats. It was oriental luxury after Bully-Grenay and the trenches, and for a wonder nobody had a word of “grousing” over “kipping” on the bare floor.

The house was occupied by a very old peasant woman and a very little girl, three years old, and as pretty as a picture. The old woman looked ill and sad and very lonesome. One night as we sat in her kitchen drinking black coffee and cognac, I persuaded her to tell her story. It was, on the whole, rather a cruel thing to ask, I am afraid. It is only one of many such that I heard over there. France has, indeed, suffered. I set down here, as nearly as I can translate, what the old woman said:

“Monsieur, I am very, very old now, almost eighty, but I am a patriot and I love my France. I do not complain that I have lost everything in this war. I do not care now, for I am old and it is for my country; but there is much sadness for me to remember, and it is with great bitterness that I think of the pig *Allemand*﻿—beast that he is.

===Chinese===
五天期满，全营撤下来休整。我们朝后方行军几英里，来到佩蒂特-桑（Petite-Saens）村。这村子也遭过战火，可不知怎的损失轻微，没几栋房子受损，我们住上了货真价实的宿营地。

我这个班——连我在内十一个人——分到一栋干净房子里的大阁楼。地方宽敞得很，屋顶严实，又没有耗子。比起布利-格勒奈和战壕，这简直是东方帝王般的奢侈。稀奇的是，睡光地板居然没有一个人“发牢骚”（grousing）。

房里住着一位年迈的农妇和一个小不点儿的小姑娘，才三岁，漂亮得像画里的一样。老太太看起来有病，愁容满面，又非常孤单。一天晚上，我们坐在她家厨房里喝黑咖啡和白兰地，我劝她讲讲自己的身世。如今想来，这要求实在有点残忍。我在那边听过的这类故事不止一件，这只是其中之一。法兰西确实受了大苦。这里我尽力按原话，把老太太讲的译在下面：

“先生，我如今很老很老了，快八十啦，可我是个爱国者，我爱我的法兰西。这场战争叫我失去了一切，我并不抱怨。如今我无所谓了，我老了，这又是为了我的国家；只是有太多伤心事要记，一想到德国佬（Allemand）那头猪——那样一头畜生——我心里就苦透了。

===Original===
“Two years ago I lived in this house, happy with my daughter and her husband and the little baby, and my husband, who worked in the mines. He was too old to fight, but when the great war came he tried to enlist, but they would not listen to him, and he returned to work, that the country should not be without coal.

“The *beau-fils* (son-in-law), he enlisted and said goodbye and went to the service.

“By and by the Boche come and in a great battle not far from this very house the *beau-fils* is wounded very badly and is brought to the house by comrades to die.

“The Boche come into the village, but the *beau-fils* is too weak to go. The Boche come into the house, seize my daughter, and there﻿—they﻿—oh, monsieur﻿—the things one may not say﻿—and we so helpless.

===Chinese===
“两年前我住在这栋房子里，同我的女儿、女婿和吃奶的小外孙女，还有我那下井挖煤的丈夫一起，日子过得快活。他太老，不能打仗了，可大战一来他还是去要求入伍，人家不听他的，他便回去下井，好叫国家不至于没有煤烧。

“女婿（beau-fils）呢，他报了名，道了别，从军去了。

“后来德国佬（Boche）打过来了。在离这栋房子不远的一场大战里，女婿身负重伤，被战友们抬回家来等死。

“德国佬进了村子，可女婿虚弱得走不动。德国佬闯进屋来，抓住我的女儿，接着——他们——啊，先生——那种事说不出口啊——而我们毫无办法。

===Original===
“Her father tries to protect her, but he is knocked down. I try, but they hold my feet over the fire until the very flesh cooks. See for yourselves the burns on my feet still.

“My husband dies from the blow he gets, for he is very old, over ninety. Just then *mon beau-fils* sees a revolver that hangs by the side of the German officer, and putting all his strength together he leaps forward and grabs the revolver. And there he shoots the officer﻿—and my poor little daughter﻿—and then he says goodbye and through the head sends a bullet.

“The Germans did not touch me but once after that, and then they knocked me to the floor when they came after the pig officer. By and by come you English, and all is well for dear France once more; but I am very desolate now. I am alone but for the *petite-fille* (granddaughter), but I love the English, for they save my home and my dear country.”

I heard a good many stories of this kind off and on, but this particular one, I think, brought home, to me at least, the general beastliness of the Hun closer than ever before. We all loved our little kiddie very much, and when we saw the evidence of the terrible cruelties the poor old woman had suffered we saw red. Most of us cried a little. I think that that one story made each of us that heard it a mean, vicious fighter for the rest of our service. I know it did me.

===Chinese===
“她父亲想护住她，被一拳打倒。我也上了，他们就把我的双脚按在火上，烤得皮肉都熟了。你们自己看，我脚上的伤疤还在呢。

“我丈夫挨了那一下就死了，他实在太老，九十开外了。就在那时，我女婿瞥见德国军官腰侧挂着的一把左轮手枪，便攒足全身的力气扑上前去，一把夺了下来。就在那儿，他一枪打死了那军官——又打死了我不幸的小女儿——然后道了声别，朝自己脑袋里送进一颗子弹。

“那以后，德国人只碰过我一次——他们回来抬那头猪猡军官时，把我掀翻在地板上。再后来，你们英国人来了，亲爱的法兰西又太平了一回；可我如今孤苦伶仃。身边只剩个小外孙女（petite-fille），可我爱英国人，因为他们救了我的家，救了我亲爱的祖国。”

这类故事我陆陆续续听过不少，可我想，恰恰是这一件，至少对我而言，把德国佬（Hun）整体的兽性前所未有地拉到了眼前。我们都非常疼我们那个小姑娘，待亲眼见到这位可怜的老太太惨遭酷刑的证据，我们全都红了眼，大多数人都掉了泪。我想，单是这一个故事，就让听到它的每一个人在往后服役的日子里都成了心黑手辣的斗士。我知道，它对我是这样。

===Original===
One of the first things a British soldier learns is to keep himself clean. He can’t do it, and he’s as filthy as a pig all the time he is in the trenches, but he tries. He is always shaving, even under fire, and show him running water and he goes to it like a duck.

More than once I have shaved in a periscope mirror pegged into the side of a trench, with the bullets snapping overhead, and rubbed my face with wet tea leaves afterward to freshen up.

Back in billets the very first thing that comes off is the big cleanup. Uniforms are brushed up, and equipment put in order. Then comes the bath, the most thorough possible under the conditions. After that comes the “cootie carnival,” better known as the “shirt hunt.” The cootie is the soldier’s worst enemy. He’s worse than the Hun. You can’t get rid of him wherever you are, in the trenches or in billets, and he sticks closer than a brother. The cootie is a good deal of an acrobat. His policy of attack is to hang on to the shirt and to nibble at the occupant. Pull off the shirt and he comes with it. Hence the shirt hunt. Tommy gets out in the open somewhere so as not to shed his little companions indoors﻿—there’s always enough there anyhow﻿—and he peels. Then he systematically runs down each seam﻿—the cootie’s favorite hiding place﻿—catches the game, and ends his career by cracking him between the thumb nails.

For some obscure psychological reason, Tommy seems to like company on one of these hunts. Perhaps it is because misery loves company, or it may be that he likes to compare notes on the catch. Anyhow, it is a common thing to see from a dozen to twenty soldiers with their shirts off, hunting cooties.

===Chinese===
英国兵最早要学会的事情之一，就是保持干净。他做不到——人在战壕里的整个工夫，他跟猪一样脏——但他一直在努力。他总在刮脸，哪怕炮火底下也刮；只要让他看见流动的活水，他就像鸭子见了水一样扑过去。

我就不止一次对着插在壕壁上的潜望镜刮脸，头顶上子弹噼啪作响，刮完再用湿茶叶搓一把脸，提提精神。

一回到宿营地，头一桩事就是大扫除。军装刷净，装备归置停当。接着是洗澡，条件许可之下洗得越彻底越好。再往后便是“虱子狂欢节”（cootie carnival）了，它更广为人知的名字叫“猎衫行动”（shirt hunt）。虱子（cootie）是士兵的头号大敌，比德国佬还坏。不管你身在战壕还是宿营地，都休想摆脱他，他比亲兄弟贴得还紧。虱子还挺是个杂技演员。他的进攻方略是扒住衬衫不放，啃食里面的房客。衬衫一脱，他就跟着下来了——“猎衫”由此得名。汤米会到外头找个露天的地方，免得把小朋友抖落在屋里——反正屋里从来也不缺——然后把衣服剥个精光，再顺着每条线缝系统地搜查——那是虱子最爱的藏身处——逮住猎物，用两个大拇指指甲一挤，终结他的生涯。

出于某种说不清的心理原因，汤米干这路营生时似乎爱有人作伴。也许是同病相怜，也许是要比比收成。反正，十几二十个士兵光着膀子集体猎虱子，是司空见惯的场面。

===Original===
“Hi sye, ’Arry,” you’ll hear someone sing out. “Look ’ere. Strike me bloomin’ well pink but this one ’ere’s got a black stripe along ’is back.”

Or, “If this don’t look like the one I showed ye ’fore we went into the blinkin’ line. ’Ow’d ’e git loose?”

And then, as likely as not, a little farther away, behind the officers’ quarters, you’ll hear one say:

“I say, old chap, it’s deucedly peculiar I should have so many of the beastly things after putting on the Harrisons mothaw sent in the lawst parcel.”

===Chinese===
“俺说，阿利，”你会听见有人亮开嗓门嚷嚷。“你瞅这儿。真是活见鬼了，这只背上还带道黑杠呢。”

要不然就是：“这可不就是进这条该死的战线之前，俺给你瞧过的那只吗。它咋又跑出来了？”

然后，十有八九，在稍远一点的地方、军官宿舍后头，你会听见有人这么讲：

“我说，老伙计，实在邪门得很——敝人抹了母亲大人上回包裹里寄来的哈里森（Harrisons）之后，居然还有这么多可恶的小东西。”

===Original===
The cootie isn’t at all fastidious. He will bite the British aristocrat as soon as anybody else. He finds his way into all branches of the service, and I have even seen a dignified colonel wiggle his shoulders anxiously.

Some of the cootie stories have become classical, like this one which was told from the North Sea to the Swiss border. It might have happened at that.

A soldier was going over the top when one of his cootie friends bit him on the calf. The soldier reached down and captured the biter. Just as he stooped, a shell whizzed over where his head would have been if he had not gone after the cootie. Holding the captive between thumb and finger, he said:

“Old feller, I cawn’t give yer the Victoria Cross﻿—but I can put yer back.”

And he did.

The worst thing about the cootie is that there is no remedy for him. The shirt hunt is the only effective way for the soldier to get rid of his bosom friends. The various dopes and patent preparations guaranteed as “good for cooties” are just that. They give ’em an appetite.

===Chinese===
虱子一点也不挑嘴。咬起英国贵族来，跟咬谁都一样利索。全军各个兵种里都有他的踪迹，我甚至见过一位仪表威严的上校焦躁地扭动肩膀。

有些虱子故事已经成了经典，比如下面这一个，从北海一直讲到瑞士边境。没准儿还真有其事。

有个士兵正翻出壕顶冲锋（going over the top）时，一位虱子朋友咬了他的小腿肚。士兵伸手下去，逮住了咬人的家伙。就在他弯腰的那一瞬，一发炮弹呼啸而过，正好从他脑袋原本待着的地方掠过——假如他没去抓虱子的话。他把俘虏捏在拇指和食指之间，说道：

“老伙计，俺没法发给你维多利亚十字勋章——不过俺可以放你回去。”

他真放了。

虱子最糟的一点是：无药可治。“猎衫行动”是士兵摆脱这些心腹之交的唯一有效法子。那些打包票“除虱特效”的各色药水、专利制剂，倒真名副其实——它们确实对虱子大有好处：胃口大开。

## III A Trench Raid / 三、战壕突袭

===Original===
In the morning the members of the raiding party were taken back a mile or so to the rear and were given instruction and rehearsal. This was the first raid that “Batt” had ever tried, and the staff was anxious to have it a success. There were fifty in the party, and Blofeld, who had organized the raid, beat our instructions into us until we knew them by heart.

The object of a raid is to get into the enemy’s trenches by stealth if possible, kill as many as possible, take prisoners if practicable, do a lot of damage, and get away with a whole hide.

We got back to the front trenches just before dark. I noticed a lot of metal cylinders arranged along the parapet. They were about as big as a stovepipe and four feet long, painted brown. They were the gas containers. They were arranged about four or five to a traverse, and were connected up by tubes and were covered with sandbags. This was the poison gas ready for release over the top through tubes.

===Chinese===
早上，突袭队的成员被带到一英里开外的后方，接受讲课和演练。这还是咱们营（“Batt”）头一回搞突袭，营部上下都盼着能一炮打响。全队五十人，组织这次突袭的布洛菲尔德（Blofeld）把行动要领掰开揉碎往我们脑子里灌，直到人人背得滚瓜烂熟。

突袭的目的，是尽可能神不知鬼不觉地摸进敌方战壕，尽量多撂倒几个，条件许可就抓俘虏，大搞一场破坏，然后一根汗毛不少地全身而退。

天擦黑之前，我们回到了前线战壕。我注意到胸墙边排着许多金属圆筒，粗细跟火炉烟囱管差不多，四英尺长，漆成棕色。那是毒气罐。每个隔段（traverse，挡墙分隔的壕段）摆上四五个，用管子连起来，再拿沙袋盖住。这就是毒气——只等时候一到，就从管子里放出壕顶。

===Original===
The time set for our stunt was eleven p.m. Eleven o’clock was “zero.” The system on the Western Front, and, in fact, all fronts, is to indicate the time fixed for any event as zero. Anything before or after is spoken of as plus or minus zero.

Around five o’clock we were taken back to Mechanics trench and fed﻿—a regular meal with plenty of everything, and all good. It looked rather like giving a condemned man a hearty meal, but grub is always acceptable to a soldier.

After that we blacked our faces. This is always done to prevent the whiteness of the skin from showing under the flare lights. Also to distinguish your own men when you get to the Boche trench.

===Chinese===
我们这场“好戏”定在晚上十一点。十一点就是“零时”（zero）。西线的规矩——其实各条战线都一样——是随便什么行动，定下的时刻一律叫零时，凡在零时之前或之后的时刻，则说成“负零”或“正零”。

五点钟前后，我们被带回技工壕（Mechanics trench）开饭——一顿正儿八经的饭食，样样管够，样样可口。那样子倒有点像给死囚临刑前的一顿饱饭，不过对当兵的来说，有得吃总是好的。

饭后我们把脸涂黑。这一手是雷打不动的：一来免得皮肤的白在照明弹光下显眼，二来摸进德国佬（Boche）战壕以后，也好分清自己人和敌人。

===Original===
Then we wrote letters and gave up our identification discs and were served with persuader sticks or knuckle knives, and with “Mills” bombs.

The persuader is a short, heavy bludgeon with a nail-studded head. You thump Fritz on the head with it. Very handy at close quarters. The knuckle knife is a short dagger with a heavy brass hilt that covers the hand. Also very good for close work, as you can either strike or stab with it.

We moved up to the front trenches at about half-past ten. At zero minus ten, that is, ten minutes of eleven, our artillery opened up. It was the first bombardment I had ever been under, and it seemed as though all the guns in the world were banging away. Afterwards I found that it was comparatively light, but it didn’t seem so then.

===Chinese===
接着我们写了家信，交出身份牌，领了“说服棍”（persuader stick）或指节匕首（knuckle knife），还有“米尔斯”（Mills）手雷。

说服棍是一根短粗的棍棒，顶头钉满铁钉，专门用来敲弗里茨（Fritz，指德国兵）的脑袋，近身肉搏时趁手得很。指节匕首是一柄短刀，粗重的黄铜护手把整只手罩住，同样适合贴身近战——抡、捅两便。

十点半左右，我们开进前沿战壕。零前十分钟——也就是差十分十一点——我方炮兵开火了。那是我平生头一回置身炮击之下，只觉得全世界的火炮都在一齐轰鸣。后来我才知道，那阵炮火算得上轻的，可当时全然不觉得。

===Original===
The guns were hardly started when there was a sound like escaping steam. Jerry leaned over and shouted in my ear: “There goes the gas. May it finish the blighters.”

Blofeld came dashing up just then, very much excited because he found we had not put on our masks, through some slip-up in the orders. We got into them quick. But as it turned out there was no need. There was a fifteen-mile wind blowing, which carried the gas away from us very rapidly. In fact it blew it across the Boche trenches so fast that it didn’t bother them either.

The barrage fire kept up right up to zero, as per schedule. At thirty seconds of eleven I looked at my watch and the din was at its height. At exactly eleven it stopped short. Fritz was still sending some over, but comparatively there was silence. After the earsplitting racket it was almost still enough to hurt.

===Chinese===
炮声刚起，就响起一阵像蒸汽泄出似的嘶声。杰里（Jerry）俯过身来，冲我耳朵喊：“毒气放出去了。愿它把那帮家伙统统放倒。”

正在这时，布洛菲尔德飞奔而来，急得不行——他发现由于命令传达出了岔子，我们全都还没戴防毒面具。我们手忙脚乱赶紧戴上，结果证明纯属多余。当时正刮着十五英里时速的大风，很快就把毒气从我们头顶上刮跑了。实际上，毒气掠过德国佬战壕的速度也太快，压根没给人家添什么堵。

按照预定计划，弹幕射击一直压到零时。差三十秒十一点时我看了一眼表，喧嚣正到顶点。十一点整，炮声戛然而止。弗里茨还在零星地打几发过来，但相比之下，四下里一片寂静。经过那阵撕耳朵的巨响，这份安静几乎静得叫人发疼。

===Original===
And in that silence over the top we went.

Lanes had been cut through our wire, and we got through them quickly. The trenches were about one hundred twenty yards apart and we still had nearly one hundred to go. We dropped and started to crawl. I skinned both my knees on something, probably old wire, and both hands. I could feel the blood running into my puttees, and my rifle bothered me as I was afraid of jabbing Jerry, who was just ahead of me as first bayonet man.

They say a drowning man or a man in great danger reviews his past. I didn’t. I spent those few minutes wondering when the machine-gun fire would come.

I had the same “gone” feeling in the pit of the stomach that you have when you drop fast in an elevator. The skin on my face felt tight, and I remember that I wanted to pucker my nose and pull my upper lip down over my teeth.

===Chinese===
就在这片寂静里，我们翻过壕顶，冲了上去。

我方铁丝网上早已开辟出通道，我们很快钻了过去。两军战壕相距约一百二十码，我们还有将近一百码要爬。我们卧倒在地，开始匍匐前进。不知被什么东西——多半是旧铁丝——把两个膝盖和两只手全蹭破了皮。我能觉出血正往绑腿里渗。步枪也碍事：杰里就在我前头充当先头刺刀手，我生怕一不留神捅到他身上。

都说人快淹死、或者大难临头的时候，一生往事会在脑子里闪回。我没有。那几分钟里，我净在琢磨机枪火力什么时候会来。

胃底那股空落落的感觉，跟电梯急坠时一模一样。我脸上的皮发紧，记得自己一个劲儿想皱鼻子，想把上嘴唇抿下来盖住牙齿。

===Original===
We got clean up to their wire before they spotted us. Their entanglements had been flattened by our barrage fire, but we had to get up to pick our way through, and they saw us.

Instantly the “Very” lights began to go up in scores, and hell broke loose. They must have turned twenty machine guns on us, or at us, but their aim evidently was high, for they only “clicked” two out of our immediate party. We had started with ten men, the other fifty being divided into three more parties farther down the line.

When the machine guns started, we charged. Jerry and I were ahead as bayonet men, with the rest of the party following with buckets of “Mills” bombs and “Stokeses.”

It was pretty light, there were so many flares going up from both sides. When I jumped on the parapet, there was a whaling big Boche looking up at me with his rifle resting on the sandbags. I was almost on the point of his bayonet.

===Chinese===
我们一直摸到他们铁丝网跟前，才被他们发现。他们的铁丝障碍早被我们的弹幕轰平了，可我们总得站起来挑路穿行，就这么暴露了。

刹那间，维里（Very）信号弹成串升起，跟着就天翻地覆。他们准是把二十挺机枪都压到我们身上——或者说朝我们这边招呼上了——不过瞄得显然偏高，我们身边这一小队只被“点”掉了两个。我们出发时是十个人，另外那五十人则分成三队，在战线更远的地段行动。

机枪一响，我们便发起冲锋。我和杰里作为刺刀手冲在最前，其余的人提着一桶桶“米尔斯”手雷和“斯托克斯”（Stokes）迫击炮弹跟在后头。

双方升起的照明弹那么多，四下里亮堂堂的。我跳上胸墙时，一个块头大得吓人的德国佬正仰脸瞧着我，步枪架在沙袋上。我差不多已经顶上他的刺刀尖了。

===Original===
For an instant I stood with a kind of paralyzed sensation, and there flashed through my mind the instructions of the manual for such a situation, only I didn’t apply those instructions to this emergency.

Instead I thought﻿—if such a flash could be called thinking﻿—how I, as an instructor, would have told a rookie to act, working on a dummy. I had a sort of detached feeling as though this was a silly dream.

Probably this hesitation didn’t last more than a second.

Then, out of the corner of my eye, I saw Jerry lunge, and I lunged too. Why that Boche did not fire I don’t know. Perhaps he did and missed. Anyhow I went down and in on him, and the bayonet went through his throat.

===Chinese===
有那么一瞬间，我僵在原地，浑身发麻，脑子里闪过操典上针对这种情形的动作指示——只是我压根没能把那些指示用到眼前这个紧急关头上。

我想的却是——若那一闪也算得上想的话——换个场合，我当教官时会怎么指点一个新兵蛋子对着假人演练这一手。我有一种置身事外的恍惚，仿佛这一切是场荒唐的梦。

这阵犹豫多半没超过一秒钟。

紧接着，我用眼角余光瞥见杰里突刺出去，我也跟着刺了出去。那个德国佬为什么没开枪，我不知道。也许开了，没打中。反正我压低枪身朝他扑进去，刺刀穿透了他的喉咙。

===Original===
Jerry had done his man in and all hands piled into the trench.

Then we started to race along the traverses. We found a machine gun and put an eleven-pound high-explosive “Stokes” under it. Three or four Germans appeared, running down communication trenches, and the bombers sent a few Millses after them. Then we came to a dugout door﻿—in fact, several, as Fritz, like a woodchuck, always has more than one entrance to his burrow. We broke these in in jig time and looked down a thirty-foot hole on a dugout full of graybacks. There must have been a lot of them. I could plainly see four or five faces looking up with surprised expressions.

Blofeld chucked in two or three Millses and away we went.

A little farther along we came to the entrance of a mine shaft, a kind of incline running toward our lines. Blofeld went in it a little way and flashed his light. He thought it was about forty yards long. We put several of our remaining Stokeses in that and wrecked it.

===Chinese===
杰里那边已经结果了他的对手，大伙儿一拥跳进战壕。

接着，我们沿着一个个隔段飞速推进。发现一挺机枪，就在它底下塞了一枚十一磅的“斯托克斯”高爆炮弹。三四个德国兵顺着交通壕跑过来，投弹手便朝他们送了几颗米尔斯手雷。随后我们来到一扇掩蔽部门前——其实是好几扇，因为弗里茨跟土拨鼠一个德性，洞穴向来不止一个口。我们三下五除二把门砸开，往一个三十英尺深的洞里张望：底下满坑满谷都是灰军装德国兵。人肯定不少，我清清楚楚看见四五张仰起来的脸，一脸的错愕。

布洛菲尔德往里甩了两三颗米尔斯手雷，我们转身就走。

再往前一段，我们到了一条坑道的入口——一道朝我方防线方向延伸的斜坡。布洛菲尔德进去几步，晃了晃手电。他估计这坑道约莫四十码长。我们把剩下的斯托克斯炮弹塞进去几枚，把它炸了个稀烂。

===Original===
Turning the corner of the next traverse, I saw Jerry drop his rifle and unlimber his persuader on a huge German who had just rounded the corner of the “bay.” He made a good job of it, getting him in the face, and must have simply caved him in, but not before he had thrown a bomb. I had broken my bayonet prying the dugout door off and had my gun upended﻿—clubbed.

When I saw that bomb coming, I bunted at it like Ty Cobb trying to sacrifice. It was the only thing to do. I choked my bat and poked at the bomb instinctively, and by sheer good luck fouled the thing over the parapet. It exploded on the other side.

“Blimme eyes,” says Jerry, “that’s cool work. You saved us the wooden cross that time.”

===Chinese===
转过下一个隔段的拐角，我看见杰里撂下步枪，抡起说服棍，招呼上一个大个子德国兵——那家伙刚从这截壕湾的拐角绕出来。这一下干得漂亮，正中脸面，八成把他整张脸都砸塌了——只可惜动手慢了半拍，那家伙抢先扔出了一颗手雷。我先前撬掩蔽部门的时候把刺刀撬断了，这会儿正倒提着枪——当棍子使。

眼见手雷飞来，我像泰·科布（Ty Cobb）打牺牲触击似的迎面捅了过去。不这么办也没别的招。我攥紧手里这根“球棒”，本能地朝手雷一戳，纯属走了大运，竟把这玩意儿擦过胸墙打出了“界外”。它在墙那头炸响了。

“我的老天爷，”杰里说，“真有你的，够冷静。这一下，你可把木十字架从大伙儿头上挡掉了。”

===Original===
We had found two more machine guns and were planting Stokeses under them when we heard the Lewises giving the recall signal. A good gunner gets so he can play a tune on a Lewis, and the device is frequently used for signals. This time he thumped out the old one﻿—“All policemen have big feet.” *Rat-a-tat-tat﻿—tat, tat.*

It didn’t come any too soon.

As we scrambled over the parapet we saw a big party of Germans coming up from the second trenches. They were out of the communication trenches and were coming across lots. There must have been fifty of them, outnumbering us five or six to one.

===Chinese===
我们又找到两挺机枪，正往底下塞斯托克斯炮弹，忽然听见刘易斯机枪打出了收队信号。好的机枪手能把刘易斯机枪弹出调子来，这本事常被拿去传信号。这回他敲的是那支老调子——“警察个个大脚板”。*哒哒哒——哒、哒。*

这信号来得正及时，一点也不算早。

我们手脚并用翻过胸墙时，只见一大群德国兵正从第二线战壕压上来。他们已经出了交通壕，正从开阔地上直扑过来。足有五十人，兵力五六倍于我们。

===Original===
We were out of bombs, Jerry had lost his rifle, and mine had no “ammo.” Blofeld fired the last shot from his revolver and, believe me, we hooked it for home.

We had been in their trenches just three and a half minutes.

Just as we were going through their wire a bomb exploded near and got Jerry in the head. We dragged him in and also the two men that had been clicked on the first fire. Jerry got Blighty on his wound, but was back in two months. The second time he wasn’t so lucky. He lies now somewhere in France with a wooden cross over his head.

===Chinese===
我们的手雷打光了，杰里的步枪丢了，我那支也没了“弹药”。布洛菲尔德用左轮枪打出最后一发，然后——信我一句——我们撒开丫子往家跑。

我们在他们的战壕里，统共只待了三分半钟。

刚穿过他们的铁丝网，一颗手雷在近旁炸开，击中了杰里的头。我们把他拖了回来，还带回了最初挨机枪时倒下的那两个弟兄。杰里这处伤，够他领一张回“布莱蒂”（Blighty，英国兵对本土的叫法）的通行证——负伤回国。可两个月后他又回来了。第二次他没这么走运。如今他躺在法兰西的某个角落，头顶上立着一副木十字架。

===Original===
Did that muddy old trench look good when we tumbled in? Oh, Boy! The staff was tickled to pieces and complimented us all. We were sent out of the lines that night and in billets got hot food, high-grade “fags,” a real bath, a good stiff rum ration, and letters from home.

Next morning we heard the results of the raid. One party of twelve never returned. Besides that we lost seven men killed. The German loss was estimated at about one hundred casualties, six machine guns and several dugouts destroyed, and one mine shaft put out of business. We also brought back documents of value found by one party in an officer’s dugout.

Blofeld got the military cross for the night’s work, and several of the enlisted men got the D.C.M.

Altogether it was a successful raid. The best part of it was getting back.

===Chinese===
连滚带爬跌回那条泥乎乎的旧战壕时，它瞧着有多亲切？嘿，美得很！指挥部乐开了花，把我们挨个夸了一遍。当夜我们就撤出了战线，在宿营地里吃上了热乎饭，领了上等“香烟”（fags）、洗了个货真价实的澡、分到足足一份烈性朗姆酒，还有家书。

第二天早上，突袭的战果传来了。一支十二人的小队再也没能回来。此外我们还阵亡了七个人。德国人的损失估计约一百人伤亡，六挺机枪和好几处掩蔽部被毁，一条坑道报废。我们还带回了一批有价值的文件——一支小队在一个军官的掩蔽部里搜出来的。

布洛菲尔德凭这一夜的功劳得了军功十字勋章（Military Cross），几名士兵得了杰出行为奖章（D.C.M.）。

总的说来，这是一场成功的突袭。最妙的部分，是活着回来了。

## XIII Back on the Somme Again / 第十三章 重回索姆河

===Original===

When I rejoined the battalion they were just going into the Somme again after a two weeks’ rest. They didn’t like it a bit.

“Gawd lumme,” says Wellsie, “ ’ave we got to fight th’ ’ole blinkin’ war. Is it right? I awsks yer. Is it?”

It was all wrong. We had been told after High Wood that we would not have to go into action again in that part of the line but that we would have a month of rest and after that would be sent up to the Ypres sector. “Wipers” hadn’t been any garden of roses early in the war, but it was paradise now compared with the Somme.

It was a sad lot of men when we swung out on the road again back to the Somme, and there was less singing than usual. That first night we remained at Mametz Wood. We figured that we would get to kip while the kipping was good. There were some old Boche dugouts in fair condition, and we were in a fair way to get comfortable. No luck!

We were hardly down to a good sleep when C company was called to fall in without equipment, and we knew that meant fatigue of some sort. I have often admired the unknown who invented that word “fatigue” as applied in a military term. He used it as a disguise for just plain hard work. It means anything whatever in the way of duty that does not have to do directly with the manning of the trenches.

This time we clicked a burial fatigue. It was my first. I never want another. I took a party of ten men and we set out, armed with picks and shovels, and, of course, rifles and bandoliers (cloth pockets containing fifty rounds of ammo).

===Chinese===

我归队的时候，营里刚休整完两个星期，正要再度开进索姆河（Somme）。弟兄们一点儿也不乐意。

“老天爷哟，”威尔西（Wellsie）说，“咱是不是非得把这场混账仗从头打到尾啊？这讲理吗？我问你。讲理吗？”

确实不讲理。海伍德（High Wood）打完之后，上头明明说我们不必再回这段防线作战，可以休整一个月，之后再调往伊普尔（Ypres）防区。大战刚开始那阵子，“歪泼斯”（Wipers，大兵们对伊普尔的讹音）也算不上什么玫瑰园，可如今跟索姆河一比，简直就是天堂。

于是，再度迈步上路、开回索姆河去的时候，一帮人个个蔫头耷脑，歌声也比往常稀落。头一夜我们宿在马梅茨树林（Mametz Wood）。大伙儿盘算着，趁能睡就赶紧睡个够。林子里有几处德国佬（Boche）留下的旧掩蔽部，状况还算凑合，眼看就能安安逸逸歇下来。没门儿！

我们还没睡出个囫囵觉，C 连就奉命空手集合，大家心里有数：这准是又摊上什么“杂役”（fatigue）了。那位头一个把这个词挪作军语的无名氏，我一向佩服得很——他拿它给不折不扣的苦力活儿做了块遮羞布。凡是跟直接驻守战壕沾不上边的差事，一概都归这个词管。

这回我们摊上的是掩埋尸体的杂役。我头一遭干这个，一辈子不想再干第二回。我带上十个弟兄出发，扛着镐头铁锹，当然还有步枪和弹药背带（一种布制弹袋，装五十发子弹）。

===Original===

We hiked three miles up to High Wood and in the early morning began the job of getting some of the dead under ground. We were almost exactly in the same place from which we had gone over after the tanks. I kept expecting all the time to run across the bodies of some of our own men. It was a most unpleasant feeling.

Some cleaning up had already been done, so the place was not so bad as it had been, but it was bad enough. The advance had gone forward so far that we were practically out of shell range, and we were safe working.

The burial method was to dig a pit four feet deep and big enough to hold six men. Then we packed them in. The worst part of it was that most of the bodies were pretty far gone and in the falling away stage. It was hard to move them. I had to put on my gas mask to endure the stench and so did some of the other men. Some who had done this work before rather seemed to like it.

I would search a body for identification marks and jot down the data found on a piece of paper. When the man was buried under, I would stick a rifle up over him and tuck the record into the trap in the butt of the gun where the oil bottle is carried.

When the pioneers came up, they would remove the rifle and substitute a little wooden cross with the name painted on it. The indifference with which the men soon came to regard this burial fatigue was amazing. I remember one incident of that first morning, a thing that didn’t seem at all shocking at the time, but which, looking back upon it, illustrates the matter-of-factness of the soldier’s viewpoint on death.

===Chinese===

我们徒步三英里上到海伍德，大清早开始干活，把一些死者送入土。那地方几乎正是我们当初跟着坦克翻出壕顶冲出去的出发位置。我一路提心吊胆，就怕撞上自己人的尸体。那滋味实在不好受。

之前已经清理过一轮，地方没有原先那么惨，可也够呛。前线推进得太远，我们实际上已在炮火射程之外，干起活来倒还安全。

掩埋的办法是挖一个四英尺深、大小装得下六个人的坑，然后把人码进去。最难熬的是，多数尸体都已经烂得差不多了，正处在皮肉溃脱的阶段，搬动起来格外费劲。那股恶臭逼得我只好戴上防毒面具，另外几个弟兄也一样。倒有个别从前干过这活的，看上去还干得挺来劲。

我负责在尸体上搜寻身份标识，把查到的信息草草记在一张纸片上。等死者入土，我就把一支步枪插在他坟头上，把那张记录塞进枪托里放油壶的小舱口。

等先遣工兵上来，他们会拔走步枪，换上一支漆着姓名的小木十字架。弟兄们没过多久就把这掩埋杂役当成家常便饭，那份无所谓真叫人吃惊。我记得头一天上午的一件小事，当时丝毫不觉得骇人，如今回头再看，却把士兵看待死亡的那份平常心刻画得入木三分。

===Original===

“Hi sye, Darby,” sang out one fellow. “Hi got a blighter ’ere wif only one leg. Wot’ll Hi do wif ’im?”

“Put him under with only one, you blinking idiot,” said I.

Presently he called out again, this time with a little note of satisfaction and triumph in his voice.

“Darby, Hi sye. I got a leg for that bleeder. Fits ’im perfect.”

Well, I went over and took a look and to my horror found that the fool had stuck a German leg on the body, high boot and all. I wouldn’t stand for that and had it out again. I wasn’t going to send a poor fellow on his last pilgrimage with any Boche leg, and said so. Later I heard this undertaking genius of a Tommy grousing and muttering to himself.

===Chinese===

“我说，达比（Darby），”一个家伙扯着嗓子喊，“这儿有个家伙只剩一条腿。拿他咋办？”

“一条腿也就一条腿，埋了，你这个该死的蠢货，”我说。

没过多会儿他又喊上了，这回嗓音里带了点得意和凯旋的味道。

“达比，我说。我给那家伙配上一条腿了。配他正合适。”

我走过去一看，吓了一跳：这蠢货竟往尸体上接了一条德国人的腿，连长筒靴都一块儿带着。这我可不依，让他又拆了下来。我不能让一个可怜人拖着条德国佬的腿踏上最后的旅程，这话我也说出了口。后来我就听见这位堪称殡葬天才的汤米（Tommy，英国兵的通称）一边发牢骚一边自言自语。

===Original===

“Cawn’t please Darby,” says he, “no matter wot. Fawncy the blighter’d feel better wif two legs, if one was Boche. It’s a fair crime sendin’ ’im hover the river wif only one.”

I was sure thankful when that burial fatigue was over, and early in the forenoon we started back to rest.

Rest, did I say? Not that trip. We were hardly back to Mametz and down to breakfast when along came an order to fall in for a carrying party. All that day we carried boxes of Millses up to the dump that was by High Wood, three long miles over hard going. Being a corporal had its compensations at this game, as I had no carrying to do; but inasmuch as the bombs were moved two boxes to a man, I got my share of the hard work helping men out of holes and lending a hand when they were mired.

Millses are packed with the bombs and detonators separate in the box, and the men are very careful in the handling of them. So the moving of material of this kind is wearing.

Another line of man-killers that we had to move were “toffy apples.” This quaint toy is a huge bomb, perfectly round and weighing sixty pounds, with a long rod or pipe which inserts into the mortar. Toffy apples are about the awkwardest thing imaginable to carry.

This carrying stunt went on for eight long days and nights. We worked on an average sixteen hours a day. It rained nearly all the time, and we never got dried out. The food was awful, as the advance had been so fast that it was almost impossible to get up the supplies, and the men in the front trenches had the first pick of the grub. It was also up to us to get the water up to the front. The method on this was to use the five-gallon gasoline cans. Sometimes they were washed out, oftener they weren’t. Always the water tasted of gas. We got the same thing, and several times I became sick drinking the stuff.

===Chinese===

“没法儿讨达比的好，”他说，“不管干成啥样。你想想，这老兄有两条腿总归舒坦些，哪怕一条是德国佬的。叫他只带一条腿就送他过河，简直是作孽。”

掩埋杂役收了工，我真是谢天谢地，上半天还没过完，我们就动身回去休整。

休整？我刚才是说休整？那趟可没指望。我们刚回到马梅茨坐下吃早饭，命令就到了：集合，出搬运队。那一整天，我们扛着一箱箱米尔斯手雷（Millses），送到海伍德旁的弹药堆积所——三英里长路，步步难走。干这活儿，下士的身份也有它的好处：轮不着我扛箱子；不过既然弹药是一人两箱地搬，我的那份苦工也没躲掉——拉弟兄们出坑，谁陷进泥里就搭谁一把。

米尔斯手雷装箱时弹体和引信分开摆放，弟兄们搬运时格外小心。所以倒腾这类物资最磨人。

我们还得搬运的另一路杀人玩意儿叫“太妃苹果”（toffy apples）。这件别致的玩具是种巨型炸弹，浑圆滚圆，重六十磅，带着一根长杆或长管，插进迫击炮里发射。天底下数一数二难扛的东西，大概就是太妃苹果了。

这桩搬运的营生一干就是八天八夜，我们平均每天干十六个钟头。差不多天天下雨，身上就没干爽过。伙食糟透了——推进太快，补给几乎送不上来，吃食尽让前面战壕里的弟兄先挑。往前线送水也是我们的差事。办法是拿五加仑的汽油桶装水，有时候涮一涮，多数时候不涮。那水永远带着汽油味儿。我们喝的也是这路货，有几回我喝下去直犯恶心。

===Original===

When that eight days of carrying was over, we were so fed up that we didn’t care whether we clicked or not. Maybe it was good mental preparation for what was to come, for on top of it all it turned out that we were to go over the top in another big attack.

When we got that news, I got Dinky out and scolded him. Maybe I’d better tell you all about Dinky before I go any farther. Soldiers are rather prone to superstitions. Relieved of all responsibility and with most of their thinking done for them, they revert surprisingly quick to a state of more or less savage mentality. Perhaps it would be better to call the state childlike. At any rate they accumulate a lot of fool superstitions and hang to them. The height of folly and the superlative invitation to bad luck is lighting three fags on one match. When that happens one of the three is sure to click it soon.

As one out of any group of three anywhere stands a fair chance of “getting his,” fag or no fag, the thing is reasonably sure to work out according to the popular belief. Most every man has his unlucky day in the trenches. One of mine was Monday. The others were Tuesday, Wednesday, Thursday, Friday, Saturday, and Sunday.

Practically every soldier carries some kind of mascot or charm. A good many are crucifixes and religious tokens. Some are coins. Corporal Wells had a sea shell with three little black spots on it. He considered three his lucky number. Thirteen was mine. My mascot was the aforesaid and much revered Dinky. Dinky was and is a small black cat made of velvet. He’s entirely flat except his head, which is becomingly round with yellow glass eyes. I carried Dinky inside my tunic always and felt safer with him there. He hangs at the head of my bed now and I feel better with him there. I realize perfectly that all this sounds like tommyrot, and that superstition may be a relic of barbarism and ignorance. Never mind! Wellsie sized the situation up one day when we were talking about this very thing.

“Maybe my shell ayen’t doin’ me no good,” says Wells. “Maybe Dinky ayen’t doin’ you no good. But ’e ayen’t doin’ ye no ’arm. So ’ang on to ’im.”

I figure that if there’s anything in war that “ayen’t doin’ ye no ’arm,” it is pretty good policy to “ ’ang on to it.”

===Chinese===

八天搬运干下来，我们腻烦透顶，连摊不摊上倒霉事儿都懒得计较了。也许这倒是对接下来那场大戏挺到位的心理预习，因为压轴的消息到底还是来了：我们要在又一次大攻势里翻出壕顶冲锋（going over the top）。

得到这个消息，我把丁奇（Dinky）掏出来数落了一顿。趁话头还没往下走，我最好先把丁奇的底细向你交代清楚。当兵的这号人特别容易迷信。他们卸下了全部责任，大部分思考又有人代劳，于是飞快地退回一种多少带点原始的心智状态——或许叫孩子气更贴切。反正他们会攒下一大堆傻气十足的迷信，还死抱着不撒手。其中登峰造极的蠢事、顶呱呱的招灾法门，就是一根火柴点三支烟。这么一点，三个人里准有一个没多久就要去见阎王。

不过话说回来，天底下随便哪三个人凑成一堆，总有一个有相当的把握“交待掉”，点不点烟都一样，所以照大伙儿的信法，这预言灵验得合情合理。战壕里几乎人人都有自己的倒霉日子。我的一个倒霉日子是星期一，另外几个倒霉日子是星期二、星期三、星期四、星期五、星期六，还有星期日。

差不多每个兵身上都带着点儿吉祥物或护身符。十字架和宗教信物占一大半，也有的是钱币。威尔斯（Wells）下士有只带三个小黑点的贝壳，认定三就是他的幸运数。我的幸运数是十三。我的吉祥物，就是前面说的那位备受敬仰的丁奇。丁奇从前是、如今也是一只黑丝绒做的小猫：除了脑袋，浑身扁扁的；脑袋倒圆得体面，还镶着两只黄玻璃眼睛。当年我总把丁奇揣在军装里层，有他在就觉得稳妥些。他如今挂在我床头，有他挂在那儿我就觉得舒坦些。我完全清楚这一切听着像十足的昏话，迷信也许是野蛮与愚昧的残余。那又何妨！有一天我们正聊起这件事，威尔西就把局面一语道破。

“兴许我这贝壳没给我带来啥好处，”威尔斯说，“兴许丁奇也没给你带来啥好处。可他没害你一根毫毛。所以你就揣好他吧。”

依我看，打仗这行当里，凡是“没害你一根毫毛”的东西，“揣好不放”就是上策。

===Original===

It was Sunday the eighth day of October that the order came to move into what was called the “O.G.I.,” that is, the old German first line. You will understand that this was the line the Boches had occupied a few days before and out of which they had been driven in the Big Push. In front of this trench was Eaucort Abbaye, which had been razed with the aid of the tanks.

We had watched this battle from the rear from the slight elevation of High Wood, and it had been a wonderful sight to see other men go out over the top without having ourselves to think about. They had poured out, wave after wave, a large part of them Scotch with their kilted rumps swinging in perfect time, a smashing barrage going on ahead, and the tanks lumbering along with a kind of clumsy majesty. When they hit the objective, the tanks crawled in and made short work of it.

The infantry had hard work of it after the positions were taken, as there were numerous underground caverns and passages which had to be mopped out. This was done by dropping smoke bombs in the entrances and smoking the Boches out like bees.

When we came up, we inherited these underground shelters, and they were mighty comfortable after the kipping in the muck. There were a lot of souvenirs to be picked up, and almost everybody annexed helmets and other truck that had been left behind by the Germans.

Sometimes it was dangerous to go after souvenirs too greedily. The inventive Hun had a habit of fixing up a body with a bomb under it and a tempting wrist watch on the hand. If you started to take the watch, the bomb went off, and after that you didn’t care what time it was.

I accumulated a number of very fine razors, and one of the sawtooth bayonets the Boche pioneers use. This is a perfectly hellish weapon that slips in easily and mangles terribly when it is withdrawn. I had thought that I would have a nice collection of souvenirs to take to Blighty if I ever got leave. I got the leave all right, and shortly, but the collection stayed behind.

===Chinese===

十月八日，星期天，命令下来了：开进叫做“O.G.I.”的地方，也就是旧德军第一线。你要知道，这条防线是德国佬几天前还占着的，是在大攻势（Big Push）里被轰出去的。这道战壕前面是奥库尔修道院（Eaucort Abbaye），已经靠着坦克帮衬夷成了平地。

这场仗我们是站在后方、踩着海伍德那点高坡上看的，看着别人翻出壕顶往外冲、自己一点儿心思不用操，真是一幅好景致。他们一波接一波地涌出去，里头好大一部分是苏格兰部队，穿褶裙的屁股一板一眼摆得整整齐齐；前头一道猛烈弹幕压着炸，坦克迈着某种笨拙的威严隆隆推进。他们一扑上目标，坦克就爬进去，三下五除二收拾干净。

阵地到手之后，步兵的硬仗还在后头：地下洞穴和坑道多得数不清，得挨个清剿。法子是把烟雾弹丢进洞口，像熏蜜蜂似的把德国佬熏出来。

等我们上来，这些地下掩蔽部就归我们继承了；在烂泥里睡惯了之后，这里简直舒服到家。四下里有的是纪念品可捡，几乎人人都顺手收缴了德国人丢下的钢盔和别的破烂玩意儿。

有时候，贪着劲儿去捡纪念品是会送命的。这个鬼点子多多的德国佬有个惯技：布置一具尸体，身子底下压着炸弹，手腕上戴块撩人的手表。你一伸手拿表，炸弹就响，打那以后，几点钟你也就不在乎了。

我攒下了一批上好的剃刀，还有一把德国佬先遣工兵用的锯齿刺刀。这家伙是件彻头彻尾的凶器：捅进去顺顺溜溜，拔出来能把人绞得稀烂。我原打算，要是能批下假来，就把这套漂亮收藏带回布莱蒂（Blighty，英国本土）。假倒是批了，没多久就批了，可那批收藏没能跟着走。

===Original===

The dugout that Number 10 drew was built of concrete and was big enough to accommodate the entire platoon. We were well within the Boche range and early in the day had several casualties, one of them a chap named Stransfield, a young Yorkshireman who was a very good friend of mine. Stransie was sitting on the top step cleaning his rifle and was blown to pieces by a falling shell. After that we kept to cover all day and slept all the time. We needed it after the exhausting work of the past eight days.

It was along about dark when I was awakened by a runner from headquarters, which was in a dugout a little way up the line, with word that the platoon commanders were wanted. I happened to be in command of the platoon, as Mr. Blofeld was acting second in command of the company, Sergeant Page was away in Havre as instructor for a month, and I was next senior.

I thought that probably this was merely another detail for some fatigue, so I asked Wells if he would go. He did and in about half an hour came back with a face as long as my arm. I was sitting on the fire step cleaning my rifle and Wellsie sank dejectedly down beside me.

“Darby,” he sighed hopelessly, “wot th’ blinkin’ ’ell do you think is up now?”

I hadn’t the faintest idea and said so. I had, however, as the educated Bones used to say “a premonition of impending disaster.” As a premonitor I was a success. Disaster was right.

Wellsie sighed again and spilled the news.

===Chinese===

10 号排抽到的掩蔽部是混凝土造的，大得容得下全排。我们稳稳处在德国佬的射程之内，天一放亮就折了几个人，其中一个叫斯特兰斯菲尔德（Stransfield），是个年轻的约克郡人，跟我是很要好的朋友。斯特兰西（Stransie）正坐在最上头一级台阶上擦枪，一发落弹把他炸得粉碎。打那以后，我们一整天都猫在掩体里，睡了又睡。前八天的活儿把人榨干了，正缺觉呢。

天擦黑的时候，一个传令兵把我叫醒，他从指挥部——设在防线上头不远的一个掩蔽部里——带来了口信：各排排长立刻过去。当时恰好由我掌着这个排：布洛菲尔德先生（Mr. Blofeld）正代行连副之职，佩奇军士（Sergeant Page）去勒阿弗尔（Havre）当教官，一去一个月，我便顺位顶了上来。

我琢磨着多半又是派什么杂役，就问威尔斯能不能替我跑一趟。他去了，约莫半个钟头回来，脸拉得有我胳膊那么长。我正坐在射击踏台上擦枪，威尔西垂头丧气地在我身边坐了下来。

“达比，”他万念俱灰地叹了口气，“你猜这回又见什么鬼了？”

我半点儿数也没有，也照实说了。不过，正像那些有学问的家伙常说的，我有了“大祸将临的预感”。作为一名预报员，我干得相当成功。大祸果真应验。

威尔西又叹了口气，把消息抖了出来。

===Original===

“We’re goin’ over th’ bleedin’ top at nine. We don’t ’ave to carry no tools. We’re in the first bloomin’ wave.”

Going without tools was supposed to be a sort of consolation for being in the first wave. The other three waves carry either picks or shovels. They consolidate the trenches after they have been taken by the first wave. That is, they turn the trench around, facing the other way, to be ready for a counter attack. It is a miserable job. The tools are heavy and awkward, and the last waves get the cream of the artillery fire, as the Boche naturally does not want to take the chance of shelling the first wave for fear of getting his own men. However, the first wave gets the machine-gun fire and gets it good. At that the first wave is the preference. I have heard hundreds of men say so. Probably the reason is that a bullet, unless it is explosive, makes a relatively clean wound, while a shell fragment may mangle fearfully.

Wells and I were talking over the infernal injustice of the situation when another runner arrived from the Sergeant Major’s, ordering us up for the rum issue. I went up for the rum and left Wells to break the news about going over.

I got an extra large supply, as the Sergeant Major was good humored. It was the last rum he ever served. I got enough for the full platoon and then some, which was a lot, as the platoon was well down in numbers owing to casualties. I went among the boys with a spoon and the rum in a mess tin and served out two tots instead of the customary one. After that all hands felt a little better, but not much. They were all fagged out after the week’s hard work. I don’t think I ever saw a more discouraged lot getting ready to go over. For myself I didn’t seem to care much, I was in such rotten condition physically. I rather hoped it would be my last time.

===Chinese===

“九点钟咱们就翻那该死的壕顶。用不着扛工具。咱们编在头一个混账波次里。”

不用扛工具，据说算是对编进第一波的一点抚慰。后面三个波次不是扛镐就是扛锹，第一波把战壕夺下来之后，由他们负责巩固——就是把战壕调个头，朝另一边设防，好迎击反扑。那是份苦不堪言的差事。工具又沉又碍事，而且炮火的精华尽落在后面几波头上，因为德国佬自然不想冒炮击第一波的风险，怕炸着自己人。不过，第一波挨的是机枪火力，而且挨得瓷实。话虽这么说，大家还是宁挑第一波。几百号人都这么对我说过。道理大概是：子弹除非是爆炸弹头，创口还算利落，而炮弹的碎片能把人绞得怕人。

我和威尔斯正痛陈这桩安排何其混账、天理何在，又一个传令兵从军士长那儿跑来，传我们上去领朗姆酒配给。我上去领酒，把宣布冲锋消息的差事留给了威尔斯。

军士长那天脾气好，给我的分量格外足。那是他这辈子配出的最后一回朗姆酒。我领到的量够全排人喝还有富余，这可不算小数，因为排里叫伤亡折得没剩几个人了。我拿一把勺子、一饭盒朗姆酒，走到弟兄们中间，一人发了两份，而不是照例的一份。之后大伙儿心里都好受了点儿，但也有限。经过一个礼拜的苦干，人人都累脱了形。要论准备冲锋的队伍，我想我从没见过比这更没精打采的一帮人。至于我自己，倒似乎不怎么在乎——身子骨已经糟透了，我反倒巴不得这是最后一回。

## XV Bits of Blighty / 十五、布莱蒂点滴

===Original===
Blighty meant life﻿—life and happiness and physical comfort. What we had left behind over there was death and mutilation and bodily and mental suffering. Up from the depths of hell we came and reached out our hands with pathetic eagerness to the good things that Blighty had for us.

I never saw a finer sight than the faces of those boys, glowing with love, as they strained their eyes for the first sight of the homeland. Those in the bunks below, unable to move, begged those on deck to come down at the first land raise and tell them how it all looked.

A lump swelled in my throat, and I prayed that I might never go back to the trenches. And I prayed, too, that the brave boys still over there might soon be out of it.

===Chinese===
布莱蒂（Blighty，英国兵对本土的叫法）意味着生——生，加上幸福，加上身体的舒坦。我们留在那边的，是死亡，是残缺，是身心双重的煎熬。我们从地狱的深处爬上来，带着一副可怜巴巴的热切，朝布莱蒂为我们备下的种种好处伸出手去。

我这辈子没见过比这更好看的景象——小伙子们的脸膛放着热爱的光彩，一个个睁大眼睛，巴望着望见故土的第一眼。下层铺位里动弹不得的伤员央求甲板上的人：陆地一冒头，就下来讲给他们听，那一切都是什么模样。

我喉咙里哽上一团东西，暗暗祷告，求老天让我再也别回战壕。我也祷告，愿那些还留在那边的勇敢的小伙子们，早日脱身。

===Original===
We steamed into the harbor of Southampton early in the afternoon. Within an hour all of those that could walk had gone ashore. As we got into the waiting trains the civilian populace cheered. I, like everybody else I suppose, had dreamed often of coming back sometime as a hero and being greeted as a hero. But the cheering, though it came straight from the hearts of a grateful people, seemed, after all, rather hollow. I wanted to get somewhere and rest.

It seemed good to look out of the windows and see the signs printed in English. That made it all seem less like a dream.

I was taken first to the Clearing Hospital at Eastleigh. As we got off the train there the people cheered again, and among the civilians were many wounded men who had just recently come back. They knew how we felt.

===Chinese===
午后不久，我们的轮船驶进了南安普敦（Southampton）港。不出一个钟头，凡是走得动的都上了岸。我们钻进候着的列车时，平民百姓齐声欢呼。我估摸着跟旁人一样，从前常做这样的梦：有朝一日英雄般归来，受英雄般的迎接。可这欢呼声虽说发自感激的人们一颗颗真心，听来终究有些空落落。我只想找个地方歇一歇。

从车窗望出去，看见招牌全用英文印着，心里真舒坦。这样一来，一切才不那么像场梦。

我先被送到伊斯特利（Eastleigh）的后送医院。在那儿下火车时，人们又欢呼起来，人群里还有许多刚回国的伤兵。他们懂我们心里是什么滋味。

===Original===
The first thing at the hospital was a real honest-to-God bath. *In a tub. With hot water!* Heavens, how I wallowed. The orderly helped me and had to drag me out. I’d have stayed in that tub all night if he would have let me.

Out of the tub I had clean things straight through, with a neat blue uniform, and for once was free of the cooties. The old uniform, bloodstained and ragged, went to the baking and disinfecting plant.

That night all of us newly arrived men who could went to the Y.M.C.A. to a concert given in our honor. The chaplain came around and cheered us up and gave us good fags.

===Chinese===
进医院头一件事，是洗一个不折不扣、如假包换的澡。*在浴缸里。用的还是热水！* 老天爷，我在里头那个扑腾哟。护理员过来帮我，末了是硬把我拽出来的。他要是肯依我，我能在那缸里泡上一整夜。

出了浴缸，从里到外换上干净衣物，还有一身挺括的蓝制服，总算头一回身上没了虱子（cooties，军中对体虱的叫法）。那身旧军装，血迹斑斑、破破烂烂，送去烘烤消毒了。

当天晚上，新来乍到、走得动的人都去了基督教青年会（Y.M.C.A.），那儿专为我们办了一场音乐会。随军牧师挨个儿过来给我们鼓劲，还发了上好的“香烟”（fags）。

===Original===
Next morning I went around to the M.O. He looked my arm over and calmly said that it would have to come off as gangrene had set in. For a moment I wished that piece of shrapnel had gone through my head. I pictured myself going around with only one arm, and the prospect didn’t look good.

However, the doctor dressed the arm with the greatest care and told me I could go to a London hospital as I had asked, for I wanted to be near my people at Southall. These were the friends I had made before leaving Blighty and who had sent me weekly parcels and letters.

I arrived in London on Tuesday and was taken in a big Red Cross motor loaned by Sir Charles Dickerson to the Fulham Hospital in Hammersmith. I was overjoyed, as the hospital was very near Southall, and Mr. and Mrs. Puttee were both there to meet me.

===Chinese===
第二天早上，我去找军医官（M.O.）。他把我的胳膊端详了一遍，不动声色地说：坏疽已经发了，这条胳膊保不住，得锯掉。有那么一瞬，我真希望当初那块弹片干脆穿了我的脑袋。我想象自己独臂到处走动的光景——前景可不妙。

不过，医生还是极其细心地替我包扎了胳膊，并告诉我，可以照我的请求转去伦敦的医院，因为我想离绍索尔（Southall）的亲友近些。那是我上次离开布莱蒂之前结识的朋友，他们每星期给我寄包裹、写信。

星期二我到了伦敦，坐着一辆查尔斯·迪克森爵士（Sir Charles Dickerson）出借的大型红十字汽车，被送往哈默史密斯（Hammersmith）的富勒姆医院。我喜出望外——医院离绍索尔很近，而且普蒂先生和普蒂太太（Mr. and Mrs. Puttee）都在那儿迎我。

===Original===
The Sister in charge of my ward, Miss Malin, is one of the finest women I have met. I owe it to her care and skill that I still have my good right arm. She has since married and the lucky man has one of the best of wives. Miss Malin advised me right at the beginning not to submit to an amputation.

My next few weeks were pretty awful. I was in constant pain, and after the old arm began to come around under Miss Malin’s treatment one of the doctors discovered that my left hand was queer. It had been somewhat swollen, but not really bad. The doctor insisted upon an X-ray and found a bit of shrapnel imbedded. He was all for an operation. Operations seemed to be the long suit of most of those doctors. I imagine they couldn’t resist the temptation to get some practice with so much cheap material all about. I consented this time, and went down for the pictures on Lord Mayor’s Day. Going to the pictures is Tommy’s expression for undergoing an anesthetic.

I was under ether two hours and a half, and when I came out of it the left hand was all to the bad and has been ever since. There followed weeks of agonizing massage treatments. Between treatments though, I had it cushy.

===Chinese===
负责我病房的护士长马琳小姐（Miss Malin），是我平生遇见的最好的女人之一。我这条好右臂至今还在，全亏她的照料和本事。她后来嫁了人，那位幸运儿娶到了一等一的好妻子。马琳小姐打一开始就劝我：别同意截肢。

接下来的几个星期相当难熬。我疼个不停。等这条老胳膊在马琳小姐的治疗下开始见好，又有一位医生发现我的左手不对劲。它肿了一阵子，但算不上真坏。医生非要做X光，查出里头嵌着一小块弹片。他一门心思主张开刀。动刀子似乎是那些医生多数人的拿手好戏。我琢磨，眼前到处是廉价的活教材，他们实在挡不住练练手的诱惑。这一回我同意了，便于市长就职日（Lord Mayor’s Day）那天下去“看片子”。“看片子”是汤米（Tommy，英国兵的通称）给上麻醉起的诨名。

乙醚麻醉了两个半钟头，等我醒过来，左手彻底完了，从此一直如此。接下来是几个星期痛得钻心的按摩治疗。不过治疗的间隙，日子过得倒挺“舒坦”（cushy，军中俚语）。

===Original===
My friends were very good to me, and several Americans entertained me a good deal. I had a permanent walking-out pass good from nine in the morning until nine at night. I saw almost every show in the city, and heard a special performance of the Messiah at Westminster Abbey. Also I enjoyed a good deal of restaurant life.

London is good to the wounded men. There is entertainment for all of them. A good many of these slightly wounded complain because they cannot get anything to drink, but undoubtedly it is the best thing for them. It is against the law to serve men in the blue uniform of the wounded. Men in khaki can buy all the liquor they want, the public houses being open from noon to two thirty and from six p.m. to nine thirty. Treating is not allowed. Altogether it works out very well and there is little drunkenness among the soldiers.

I eventually brought up in a Convalescent Hospital in Brentford, Middlesex, and was there for three weeks. At the end of that time I was placed in category C 3.

===Chinese===
朋友们待我极好，几位美国同胞也常常带我出去消遣。我有一张长期外出证，从早上九点管用到晚上九点。城里几乎每一场戏我都看了，还在威斯敏斯特教堂（Westminster Abbey）听了《弥赛亚》（Messiah）的一场专场演出。此外，我还着实过了一阵子下馆子的日子。

伦敦待伤兵不薄，人人都有消遣。不少轻伤员抱怨喝不着一口酒，可这无疑对他们最有好处。给穿伤兵蓝制服的人卖酒是违法的。穿卡其军装的想买多少酒都行，酒馆营业时间从中午到两点半、傍晚六点到九点半。请人喝酒也在禁止之列。总的说来效果很好，士兵里头几乎见不着醉鬼。

我最后辗转到来德尔塞克斯郡（Middlesex）布伦特福德（Brentford）的一所疗养医院，在那儿住了三个星期。期满时，我被列入 C 3 类。

===Original===
The system of marking the men in England is by categories, A, B, and C. A 1, 2, and 3 are for active service. A 4 is for the under-aged. B categories are for base service, and C is for home service. C 3 was for clerical duty, and as I was not likely to become efficient again as a soldier, it looked like some kind of bookkeeping for me for the duration of the war.

Unless one is all shot to pieces, literally with something gone, it is hard to get a discharge from the British army. Back in the early days of 1915, a leg off was about the only thing that would produce a discharge.

When I was put at clerical duty, I immediately began to furnish trouble for the British army, not intentionally, of course, but quite effectively. The first thing I did was to drop a typewriter and smash it. My hands had spells when they absolutely refused to work. Usually it was when I had something breakable in them. After I had done about two hundred dollars’ damage indoors they tried me out as bayonet instructor. I immediately dropped a rifle on a concrete walk and smashed it. They wanted me to pay for it, but the M.O. called attention to the fact that I shouldn’t have been put at the work under my category.

===Chinese===
英国给士兵评级的办法是分类：A、B、C 三等。A 1、2、3 级服现役，A 4 级是未达兵役年龄的。B 类在基地服役，C 类在本土服役。C 3 类干的是文案差事。我既然多半再也当不成一个像样的兵了，看来这场仗打完之前，我就得跟账本打交道了。

除非真给打得七零八落——实实在在缺了点儿什么——否则很难从英国军队里退伍。早先 1915 年那阵子，少一条腿差不多是唯一能换来退伍证的东西。

一被派上文案差事，我立刻开始给英国军队供应麻烦——当然不是故意的，可相当见效。头一件事，就是摔了一台打字机，摔了个稀碎。我这双手时不时闹罢工，死活不肯干活，偏偏总是赶在我手里捧着易碎品的时候。等我在屋里砸了大约两百美元的家当之后，他们改让我去试刺枪教官。我立刻把一支步枪摔在水泥地上，摔碎了。他们要我赔，军医官提请注意一个事实：按我的类别，本来就不该派我干这活儿。

===Original===
They then put me back at bookkeeping at Command Headquarters, Salisbury, but I couldn’t figure English money and had a bad habit of fainting and falling off the high stool. To cap the climax, I finally fell one day and knocked down the stovepipe, and nearly set the office afire. The M.O. then ordered me back to the depot at Winchester and recommended me for discharge. I guess he thought it would be the cheapest in the long run.

The adjutant at Winchester didn’t seem any too pleased to see me. He said I looked as healthy as a wolf, which I did, and that they would never let me out of the army. He seemed to think that my quite normal appearance would be looked upon as a personal insult by the medical board. I said that I was sorry I didn’t have a leg or two gone, but it couldn’t be helped.

While waiting for the Board, I was sent to the German Prison Camp at Winnal Downs as corporal of the permanent guard. I began to fear that at last they had found something that I could do without damaging anything, and my visions of the U.S.A. went a-glimmering. I was with the Fritzies for over a week, and they certainly have it soft and cushy.

===Chinese===
随后他们把我弄回索尔兹伯里（Salisbury）的司令部总部管记账，可我算不来英国钱，还有个晕过去、从高凳子上栽下来的坏毛病。最绝的是，有一天我终于摔了一跤，连火炉烟囱管都撞塌了，差点把办公室点着。军医官于是下令把我送回温彻斯特（Winchester）的兵站，并建议让我退伍。我估摸他是想：长痛不如短痛，这么着长远最省钱。

温彻斯特的副官见了我，似乎不怎么乐意。他说我壮得像头狼——我也确实像——还说军队永远不会放我走。他似乎认为，医务委员会见到我这副十分正常的尊容，会当成是对他们个人的侮辱。我说，很遗憾我没少一条腿两条腿，可这也没法子。

等候委员会传见期间，我被派到温纳尔丘（Winnal Downs）的德军战俘营，当常备警卫队的下士。我开始害怕：他们到底还是找到了一件我干起来砸不了东西的差事，我那合众国之梦眼看要化成泡影。我跟弗里茨们（Fritzies，德国兵的诨名）相处了一个多星期，他们的日子那叫一个安逸滋润。

===Original===
They have as good food as the Tommies. They are paid ninepence a day, and the work they do is a joke. They are well housed and kept clean and have their own canteens, where they can buy almost anything in the way of delicacies. They are decently treated by the English soldiers, who even buy them fags out of their own money. The nearest thing I ever saw to humiliation of a German was a few good-natured jokes at their expense by some of the wits in the guard. The English know how to play fair with an enemy when they have him down.

I had about given up hope of ever getting out of the army when I was summoned to appear before the Travelling Medical Board. You can wager I lost no time in appearing.

The board looked me over with a discouraging and cynical suspicion. I certainly did look as rugged as a navvy. When they gave me a going over, they found that my heart was out of place and that my left hand might never limber up again. They voted for a discharge in jig time. I had all I could do to keep from howling with joy.

===Chinese===
他们吃的跟汤米们一样好。每天领九便士工钱，干的活儿纯属玩笑。住得宽敞，收拾得干净，还有自己的小卖部，几乎什么讲究吃喝都买得着。英国士兵待他们规规矩矩，甚至自己掏腰包给他们买香烟。我见过的最接近羞辱德国人的事，不过是警卫里几个促狭鬼拿他们打趣，开几句善意的玩笑。把敌人打趴下之后还懂得公平相待——这一手，英国人是会的。

就在我差不多断了退伍念想的时候，传我到巡回医务鉴定委员会（Travelling Medical Board）应讯。你尽可打赌，我一秒钟也没耽搁就到场了。

委员会的人把我上下打量，那眼光又叫人泄气又世故，满含疑心。我确实看上去壮得像个挖土的苦力。可他们一上手检查，就发现我的心脏挪了位，左手也未必再灵便得起来。眨眼工夫，他们就表决通过了让我退伍。我拼命忍着，才没有乐得嚎出声来。

===Original===
It was some weeks before the final formalities were closed up. The pension board passed on my case, and I was given the magnificent sum of sixteen shillings and sixpence a week, or $3.75. I spent the next few weeks in visiting my friends and, eventually, at the 22nd Headquarters at Bermondsey, London, S.C., received the papers that once more made me a free man.

The papers read in part, “He is discharged in consequence of paragraph 392, King’s Rules and Regulations. No longer fit for service.” In another part of the book you will find a reproduction of the character discharge also given. The discharged man also receives a little silver badge bearing the inscription, “For King and Empire, Services Rendered.” I think that I value this badge more than any other possession.

Once free, I lost no time in getting my passport into shape and engaged a passage on the *St. Paul*, to sail on the second of June. Since my discharge is dated the twenty-eighth of May, you can see that I didn’t waste any time. My friends at Southall thought I was doing things in a good deal of a hurry. The fact is, I was fed up on war. I had had a plenty. And I was going to make my getaway before the British War Office changed its mind and got me back in uniform. Mrs. Puttee and her eldest son saw me off at Euston Station. Leaving them was the one wrench, as they had become very dear to me. But I had to go. If Blighty had looked good, the thought of the U.S.A. was better.

===Chinese===
又过了几个星期，最后的例行手续才办完。抚恤金委员会审了我的案子，发给我一笔了不起的巨款：每周十六先令六便士，合 3.75 美元。接下来几个星期我四处访友，最后在伦敦贝蒙德赛（Bermondsey）的第 22 总部，领到了那几纸文书——它们让我重新做回了自由人。

文书上写道：“兹依据《国王条例与规程》第 392 条令其退伍。不再适合服役。”本书另一处印有同时发给的品行退伍证明的复制件。退伍的人还会领到一枚小银章，上面镌着：“为国王与帝国，效劳已尽”（For King and Empire, Services Rendered）。我想，我珍视这枚徽章，胜过其他任何一件财物。

一获自由，我立刻把护照办妥，订了“圣保罗号”（St. Paul）六月二日启航的舱位。我的退伍令日期是五月二十八日——可见我半点儿工夫也没耽搁。绍索尔的朋友们觉得我办事未免太急。实情是，这场仗我受够了，实实在在受够了。我要赶在英国陆军部（War Office）变卦、把我重新塞回军装之前，溜之大吉。普蒂太太和她的大儿子到尤斯顿车站（Euston Station）送我。离开他们是最揪心的一下——他们早已成了我的至亲。可我非走不可。如果说布莱蒂看着已经够好，那么合众国的念想还要更好。

===Original===
My passage was uneventful. No submarines, no bad weather, nothing disagreeable. On the eighth day I looked out through a welter of fog and rain to the place where the Statue of Liberty should have been waving a greeting across New York harbor. The lady wasn’t visible, but I knew she was there. And even in a downpour equal to anything furnished by the choicest of Flanders rainstorms, little old New York looked better than anything I could imagine, except sober and staid old Boston.

That I am at home, safe and free of the horrors of war, is to me a strange thing. I think it comes into the experience of most of the men who have been over there and who have been invalided out of the service. Looking back on the awfulness of the trenches and the agonies of mind and body, the sacrifice seems to fade into insignificance beside the satisfaction of having done a bit in the great and just cause.

Now that our own men are going over, I find myself with a very deep regret that I cannot go too. I can only wish them the best of luck and rest in confidence that every man will do his uttermost.

===Chinese===
这趟航程风平浪静。没有潜艇，没有坏天气，没有任何不痛快的事。第八天，我透过弥漫的雾雨，朝自由女神像本该跨越纽约港口挥手致意的地方望出去。那位女士瞧不见，可我知道她在那儿。哪怕浇着一场不输法兰德斯上等暴雨的瓢泼大雨，小老纽约看上去仍比我所能想象的一切都美——除了稳重古板的老波士顿。

如今我人在家里，安然脱开了战争的恐怖——这在我感觉是桩怪事。我想，凡是去过那边、又因伤病退役的人，多半都有这段体验。回望战壕的可怖、身心的煎熬，那份牺牲，比起在伟大而正义的事业里尽过一份力的满足，似乎淡得不值一提了。

如今我们美国自己的小伙子们也要过去了，我却发觉自己心里有一份很深的遗憾：不能同去。我唯有祝他们鸿运当头，并满心笃定地相信：每一个人都会拼尽全力。

## Dedication / 献词

===Original===
To Marion A. Puttee, Southall, Middlesex, England, I dedicate this book as a token of appreciation for all the loving thoughts and deeds bestowed upon me when I was a stranger in a strange land

===Chinese===
谨以此书献给英格兰米德尔塞克斯郡绍索尔的马里恩·A·帕蒂（Marion A. Puttee），聊表感激——当我在异乡为客之时，她曾给予我种种关爱的牵挂与善举。

## VII Fascination of Patrol Work / 七、巡逻勤务的魅力

===Original===
I want to say a word right here about patrol work in general, because for some reason it fascinated me and was my favorite game.

If you should be fortunate﻿—or unfortunate enough, as the case might be﻿—to be squatting in a front-line trench this fine morning and looking through a periscope, you wouldn’t see much. Just over the top, not more than twenty feet away, would be your barbed-wire entanglements, a thick network of wire stretched on iron posts nearly waist high, and perhaps twelve or fifteen feet across. Then there would be an intervening stretch of from fifty to one hundred fifty yards of No Man’s Land, a tortured, torn expanse of muddy soil, pitted with shell craters, and, over beyond, the German wire and his parapet.

There would be nothing alive visible. There would probably be a few corpses lying about or hanging in the wire. Everything would be still except for the flutter of some rag of a dead man’s uniform. Perhaps not that. Daylight movements in No Man’s Land are somehow disconcerting. Once I was in a trench where a leg﻿—a booted German leg, stuck up stark and stiff out of the mud not twenty yards in front. Some idiotic joker on patrol hung a helmet on the foot, and all the next day that helmet dangled and swung in the breeze. It irritated the periscope watchers, and the next night it was taken down.

===Chinese===
我想在这儿专门就巡逻勤务说上几句，因为不知怎的，这差事对我有一种魅力，是我最爱玩的一把游戏。

假如你走运——或者说倒霉，那就得看情形了——在这个晴朗的早晨蹲在前沿战壕里，凑着潜望镜往外望，你见不着多少东西。刚出壕顶，不出二十英尺，就是你们的带刺铁丝网障碍：密匝匝一张铁丝网，绷在差不多齐腰高的铁桩上，纵深约莫十二到十五英尺。再往前，隔着五十至一百五十码不等的无人地带（No Man’s Land）——那是一片被糟蹋得皮开肉绽的泥泞地，满布弹坑；再过去，就是德国人的铁丝网和胸墙。

看不见一样活物。多半有几具尸体横在四下里，或者挂在铁丝网上。一切都静止不动，只有死人军装上的某片破布偶尔飘一下。也许连这个也没有。大白天里，无人地带上有什么动静，总是叫人心里发毛。有一回我在一条战壕里，正前方不出二十码的泥地里直挺挺戳着一条腿——一条穿着靴子的德国腿。哪个巡逻的二百五促狭鬼把一顶钢盔挂在那只脚上，于是整整第二天，那顶钢盔就在风里荡来荡去。看潜望镜的人被它惹得心烦，第二天夜里就有人把它摘了下来。


===Original===
Ordinarily, however, there is little movement between the wires, nor behind them. And yet you know that over yonder there are thousands of men lurking in the trenches and shelters.

After dark these men, or some of them, crawl out like hunted animals and prowl in the black mystery of No Man’s Land. They are the patrol.

The patrol goes out armed and equipped lightly. He has to move softly and at times very quickly. It is his duty to get as close to the enemy lines as possible and find out if they are repairing their wire or if any of their parties are out, and to get back word to the machine gunners, who immediately cut loose on the indicated spot.

===Chinese===
不过一般说来，两边铁丝网之间、铁丝网后面，都难得有什么动静。可你也知道，就在那边，成千上万的人正潜伏在战壕和掩蔽工事里。

天黑以后，这些人——或者说其中一部分——像被追猎的野兽一样爬出工事，到无人地带漆黑的神秘里潜行。他们就是巡逻兵。

巡逻兵出勤，轻装带枪。他得走得悄没声，有时又得快得出奇。他的职责是尽可能贴近敌方防线，查清对方是不是在修铁丝网、有没有小队出巢，然后把消息带回去报告机枪手——机枪手立马朝指定地点一顿猛扫。


===Original===
Sometimes he lies with his head to the ground over some suspected area, straining his ears for the faint *scrape, scrape* that means a German mining party is down there, getting ready to plant a ton or so of high explosive, or, it may be, is preparing to touch it off at that very moment.

Always the patrol is supposed to avoid encounter with enemy patrols. He carries two or three Mills bombs and a pistol, but not for use except in extreme emergency. Also a persuader stick or a trench knife, which he may use if he is near enough to do it silently.

The patrol stares constantly through the dark and gets so he can see almost as well as a cat. He must avoid being seen. When a Very light goes up, he lies still. If he happens to be standing, he stands still. Unless the light is behind him so that he is silhouetted, he is invisible to the enemy.

===Chinese===
有时候，他把脑袋贴在地上，趴在某段可疑的地皮上，竖起耳朵捕捉那一下下微弱的*嚓、嚓*声——那意味着一支德国坑道队就在底下，正准备埋上一吨上下的高爆炸药，说不定，正打算就在这一刻点火。

巡逻兵的铁律，是无论如何避开与敌方巡逻队照面。他随身带两三颗“米尔斯”（Mills）手雷和一支手枪，可不到万分危急绝不动用。还有一根说服棍（persuader stick）或一把战壕匕首（trench knife）——只要近到能悄没声地下手，倒可以拿来一用。

巡逻兵在黑暗里不停地睁眼瞪着，练到后来眼力几乎赶得上一只猫。他必须不被瞧见。维里（Very）信号弹一升空，他就趴着不动；碰巧正站着，那就站着不动。只要光不是打他背后照来、把他的影子勾出个轮廓，敌人就瞧不见他。


===Original===
Approaching a corpse, the patrol lies quiet and watches it for several minutes, unless it is one he has seen before and is acquainted with. Because sometimes the man isn’t dead, but a perfectly live Boche patrol lying “doggo.” You can’t be too careful.

If you happen to be pussyfooting forward erect and encounter a German patrol, it is policy to scuttle back unless you are near enough to get in one good lick with the persuader. He will retreat slowly himself, and you mustn’t follow him. Because: The British patrol usually goes out singly or at the most in pairs or threes.

The Germans, on the other hand, hunt in parties. One man leads. Two others follow to the rear, one to each side. And then two more, and two more, so that they form a V, like a flock of geese. Now if you follow up the lead man when he retreats, you are baited into a trap and find yourself surrounded, smothered by superior numbers, and taken prisoner. Then back to the Boche trench, where exceedingly unpleasant things are apt to happen.

===Chinese===
靠近一具尸体时，巡逻兵会先趴着静静盯上几分钟——除非那是他从前见过、打过照面的老相识。因为有时候那家伙压根没死，而是一个活生生的德国佬（Boche）巡逻兵在“装死潜伏”（lying doggo）。小心不为过，怎么谨慎都不过分。

假如你碰巧直着身子蹑手蹑脚往前摸，迎头撞上一支德国巡逻队，上策就是赶紧缩回去——除非你近到足以抡起说服棍，结结实实给他来一下。他自己也会缓缓后撤，而你千万别跟。因为：英国巡逻兵通常单枪匹马出去，至多两人一双、三人一伙。

德国人正相反，成群结伙地出来打猎。一个人领头，两个跟在后面，一左一右；再后面又是两个、两个，排成一个V字，像一群大雁。你要是见领头的退了就跟上去，便是自投罗网：转眼被团团围住，被优势兵力闷头按倒，成了俘虏。然后被押回德国佬的战壕——在那里，极不愉快的事情多半等着你。


===Original===
It is, in fact, most unwholesome for a British patrol to be captured. I recall a case in point which I witnessed and which is far enough in the past so that it can be told. It occurred, not at Vimy Ridge, but further down the line, nearer the Somme.

I was out one night with another man, prowling in the dark, when I encountered a Canadian sergeant who was alone. There was a Canadian battalion holding the next trench to us, and another farther down. He was from the farther one. We lay in the mud and compared notes. Once, when a light floated down near us, I saw his face, and he was a man I knew, though not by name.

After a while we separated, and he went back, as he was considerably off his patrol. An hour or so later the mist began to get gray, and it was evident that dawn was near. I was a couple of hundred yards down from our battalion, and my man and I made for the trenches opposite where we were. As we climbed into a sap head, I was greeted by a Canadian corporal. He invited me to a tin of “char,” and I sent my man up the line to our own position.

We sat on the fire step drinking, and I told the corporal about meeting the sergeant out in front. While we were at the “char” it kept getting lighter, and presently a pair of Lewises started to rattle a hundred yards or so away down the line. Then came a sudden commotion and a kind of low, growling shout. That is the best way I can describe it. We stood up, and below we saw men going over the top.

===Chinese===
事实上，英国巡逻兵一旦被俘，多半凶多吉少。我记得一个现成的例子，是我亲眼所见，好在年代隔得够远，如今可以讲了。事情不是发生在维米岭（Vimy Ridge），而是在战线更靠下的地方，离索姆河（Somme）更近。

一天夜里，我带着一个人在黑暗里潜行，遇上一个落单的加拿大军士。挨着我们的那条战壕由一个加拿大营防守，再往下还有一个营。他是那更下面一个营的。我们趴在泥地里交换见闻。有一回，一颗照明弹飘落到近旁，我看清了他的脸——是个认得的人，虽然叫不上名字。

过了一阵我们分了手，他回去了——他偏离自己的巡逻地段已经太远。约莫一个钟头后，雾开始泛灰，天眼看就要亮了。我这时离我们营有二百来码，便带着我的人朝我们所在位置对面的战壕走去。爬进一个坑道头的时候，一个加拿大下士跟我打了个招呼。他请我喝一罐“茶”（char，兵俚），我打发我的人沿战线回我们自己的阵地去。

我们坐在射击踏垛（fire step）上喝着，我把在前头遇上那位军士的事讲给下士听。茶还没喝完，天越来越亮，不多时，战线那头百来码外响起一对刘易斯（Lewis）机枪的嗒嗒声。跟着是一阵突然的骚动，还有一声低沉的、咆哮似的呐喊——我只能这样形容。我们站起身，看见下方有人正翻出壕顶冲锋。


===Original===
“What the dickens can this be?” stuttered the corporal. “There’s been no barrage. There’s no orders for a charge. What is it? What is it?”

Well, there they were, going over, as many as two hundred of them﻿—growling. The corporal and I climbed out of the trench at the rear, over the parados, and ran across lots down to a point opposite where the Canadians had gone over, and watched.

They swept across No Man’s Land and into the Boche trench. There was the deuce of a ruckus over there for maybe two minutes, and then back they came﻿—carrying something. Strangely enough there had been no machine-gun fire turned on them as they crossed, nor was there as they returned. They had cleaned that German trench! And they brought back the body of a man﻿—nailed to a rude crucifix. The thing was more like a T than a cross. It was made of planks, perhaps two by five, and the man was spiked on by his hands and feet. Across the abdomen he was riddled with bullets and again with another row a little higher up near his chest. The man was the sergeant I had talked to earlier in the night. What had happened was this. He had, no doubt, been taken by a German patrol. Probably he had refused to answer questions. Perhaps he had insulted an officer. They had crucified him and held him up above the parapet. With the first light his own comrades had naturally opened on the thing with the Lewises, not knowing what it was. When it got lighter, and they recognized the hellish thing that had been done to one of their men, they went over. Nothing in this world could have stopped them.

The M.O. who viewed the body said that without question the man had been crucified alive. Also it was said that the same thing had happened before.

===Chinese===
“这、这算怎么回事？”下士结结巴巴地说。“没有弹幕，也没有冲锋的命令。这是什么名堂？这是什么名堂？”

反正，他们出去了，出去的人足有两百来号——一个个低吼着。我和下士从战壕后头爬出去，翻过背墙（parados），穿过开阔地，跑到加拿大人翻出去的那段正对面，望过去。

他们横扫过无人地带，冲进德国佬的战壕。那边天翻地覆地闹腾了约莫两分钟，然后他们回来了——抬着一样东西。奇怪的是，他们冲过去时没有一挺机枪朝他们开火，回来时也没有。他们把那条德国战壕荡平了！抬回来的是一具尸体——钉在一座粗陋的十字架上。那玩意儿与其说像十字，不如说像个丁字。是木板拼的，约莫两英尺宽、五英尺长，那人被铁钉钉穿了双手双脚。腹部横着挨了一排枪眼，靠近胸口的稍高处又是一排。死者正是前半夜同我说过话的那个军士。事情是这样的：他毫无疑问是被一支德国巡逻队逮住了。多半是不肯答话，兴许还冲撞了哪位军官。他们把他钉上十字架，高高地举在胸墙上方。天刚透亮，他自家的弟兄自然朝着那东西开了刘易斯机枪——他们不知道那是什么。等天色更亮，认出那是加在他们一个人身上的魔鬼行径，他们便翻出壕顶冲了过去。这世上没有任何东西拦得住他们。

验尸的军医（M.O.）说，这人毫无疑问是被活着钉上去的。据说同样的事从前也发生过。


===Original===
I told Captain Green of the occurrence when I got back to our own trenches, and he ordered me to keep silent, which I did. It was feared that if the affair got about the men would be “windy” on patrol. However, the thing did get about and was pretty well talked over. Too many saw it.

The Canadians were reprimanded for going over without orders. But they were not punished. For their officers went with them﻿—led them.

Occasionally the temptation is too great. Once I was out on patrol alone, having sent my man back with a message, when I encountered a Heinie. I was lying down at the time. A flock of lights went up and showed this fellow standing about ten feet from me. He had frozen and stayed that way till the flares died, but I was close enough to see that he was a German. Also﻿—marvel of marvels﻿—he was alone.

===Chinese===
回到我们自己的战壕后，我把这件事报告了格林上尉（Captain Green），他吩咐我别声张，我也照办了。上头担心这事一旦传开，弟兄们出巡会“发怵”。不过事情还是传开了，而且议论得挺热闹。看见的人太多了。

加拿大人因无令擅出壕挨了申斥，但没有受罚。因为他们的军官跟他们一起出去了——还是领头去的。

有时候，诱惑实在太大。有一回，我打发我的人送信回去，独自一个出巡，撞上了一个“海尼”（Heinie，美国兵对德国兵的叫法）。当时我正趴着。一串照明弹升起来，照出这家伙站在离我十来英尺的地方。他僵在原地，就那么定住，直到照明弹熄灭；而我离得够近，看得出他是个德国兵。而且——奇中之奇——他是独自一个。


===Original===
When the darkness settled again, I got to my feet and jumped at him. He jumped at me﻿—another marvel. Going into the clinch I missed him with the persuader and lost my grip on it, leaving the weapon dangling by the leather loop on my wrist. He had struck at me with his automatic, which I think he must have dropped, though I’m not sure of that. Anyway we fell into each other’s arms and went at it barehanded. He was bigger than I. I got under the ribs and tried to squeeze the breath out of him, but he was too rugged.

At the same time I felt that he didn’t relish the clinch. I slipped my elbow up and got under his chin, forcing his head back. His breath smelled of beer and onions. I was choking him when he brought his knee up and got me in the stomach and again on the instep when he brought his heel down.

It broke my hold, and I staggered back groping for the persuader. He jumped back as far as I did. I felt somehow that he was glad. So was I. We stood for a minute, and I heard him gutter out something that sounded like “Verdamder swinehunt.” Then we both backed away.

It seemed to me to be the nicest way out of the situation. No doubt he felt the same.

===Chinese===
黑暗重新合拢时，我一跃而起扑向他。他也朝我扑来——又是一桩奇事。接上扭斗的一瞬，说服棍抡了个空，还脱了手，全凭腕上的皮带环吊着晃荡。他先前拿自动手枪朝我砸过一下，那枪我看八成是掉了，不过不敢肯定。反正我们俩抱作一团，赤手空拳干了起来。他块头比我大。我把胳膊勒进他肋下，想把他勒得背过气去，可这家伙太皮实。

同时我觉出他并不乐意这么搂着。我把肘往上滑，顶进他下巴底下，把他的头往后别。他满嘴啤酒拌洋葱的味儿。我正勒得他透不上气，他膝盖一抬，顶中我的肚子，紧跟着脚跟砸落，又踩了我的脚背。

这一下挣开了我的搂抱。我踉跄后退，伸手去摸说服棍。他蹦开的距离跟我一样远。不知怎么，我觉出他挺乐意，我也一样。我们站了片刻，我听见他从喉咙里咕噜出一句什么，听着像“Verdamder swinehunt”（德语骂人话：该死的猪猡）。然后我们俩各自倒退着走开了。

依我看，这是摆脱这个局面最体面的出路。无疑，他也是这么想的。


===Original===
I seem to have wandered far from the Quarries and the Grouse Spots. Let’s go back.

We were two days in the Grouse Spots and were then relieved, going back to the Quarries and taking the place of Number 9 in support. While lying there, I drew a patrol that was interesting because it was different.

The Souchez River flowed down from Abalaine and Souchez villages and through our lines to those of the Germans, and on to Lens. Spies, either in the army itself or in the villages, had been placing messages in bottles and floating them down the river to the Germans.

===Chinese===
我这是把话头扯得离采石场（Quarries）和松鸡窝（Grouse Spots）太远了。言归正传。

我们在松鸡窝守了两天，随后换防下来，回到采石场，接替“九号”担任预备队。在那儿待命的时候，我领到一回有趣的巡逻——有趣就有趣在与众不同。

苏谢河（Souchez River）从阿巴兰（Abalaine）和苏谢两个村子流下来，穿过我方防线，流向德军防线，一路奔朗斯（Lens）去。有密探——不是混在军队里，就是藏在村子里——一直把情报装进瓶子，顺水漂给德国人。


===Original===
Somebody found this out, and a net of chicken wire had been placed across the river in No Man’s Land. Someone had to go down there and fish for bottles twice nightly. I took this patrol alone. The lines were rather far apart along the river, owing to the swampy nature of the ground, which made livable trenches impossible.

I slipped out and down the slight incline, and presently found myself in a little valley. The grass was rank and high, sometimes nearly up to my chin, and the ground was slimy and treacherous. I slipped into several shell holes and was almost over my head in the stagnant, smelly water.

I made the river all right, but there was no bridge or net in sight. The river was not over ten feet wide and there was supposed to be a footbridge of two planks where the net was.

===Chinese===
这事叫人察觉了，于是有人在无人地带的河面上横拦了一道铁丝纱网。这就得有人每晚下去两趟，捞瓶子。这趟巡逻我一个人包了。沿河一带两军防线离得相当远，因为地面全是沼泽，根本挖不出能住人的战壕。

我溜出壕，顺着一道缓坡往下走，不多时进了一条小山谷。野草长得又密又高，有时快齐到我的下巴，地面又滑又坑人。我失足跌进好几个弹坑，泡在死水臭水里，差点没过头顶。

河倒是摸到了，可桥和网一概看不见。河宽不过十英尺，按说拦网的地方该有一座两块木板拼的便桥。


===Original===
I got back into the grass and made my way downstream. Sliding gently through the grass, I kept catching my feet in something hard that felt like roots; but there were no trees in the neighborhood. I reached down and groped in the grass and brought up a human rib. The place was full of them, and skulls. Stooping, I could see them, grinning up out of the dusk, hundreds of them. I learned afterwards that this was called the Valley of Death. Early in the war several thousand Zouaves had perished there, and no attempt had been made to bury them.

After getting out of the skeletons, I scouted along downstream and presently heard the low voices of Germans. Evidently they had found the net and planned to get the messages first. Creeping to the edge of the grass, I peeped out. I was opposite the bottle trap. I could dimly make out the forms of two men standing on the nearer end of the plank bridge. They were, I should judge, about ten yards away, and they hadn’t heard me. I got out a Mills, pulled the pin, and pitched it. The bomb exploded, perhaps five feet this side of the men. One dropped, and the other ran.

After a short wait I ran over to the German. I searched him for papers, found none, and rolled him into the river.

===Chinese===
我退回草丛，顺流而下。在草里轻轻滑行时，脚下总绊着什么硬邦邦的东西，手感像树根；可这一带根本没有树。我伸手往草里一摸，捞上来一根人的肋骨。这地方遍地都是这玩意儿，还有骷髅头。弯下腰，能看见它们从暮色里龇着牙朝上瞪，成百上千。后来我才得知，这地方叫“死亡谷”。战争初期，几千名朱阿夫兵（Zouaves，法军步兵）就丧命在这里，一直没人想着把他们掩埋。

穿出白骨堆，我继续沿岸往下游侦察，不多时听见几个德国人压低的说话声。他们显然发现了拦网，打算抢先把瓶子捞走。我爬到草丛边上，探头张望。我正对着那个拦瓶陷阱。隐约辨出两个人影，站在木板桥靠我这一头。我估摸他们离我约莫十码，而且没听见我的动静。我掏出一颗米尔斯手雷，拔了销，扔了过去。手雷在那两人靠我这边约五英尺处炸开。一个倒了，另一个跑了。

等了片刻，我跑到那个德国兵跟前。在他身上搜文件，一无所获，便把他滚进了河里。


===Original===
After a few days in the Quarries we were moved to what was known as the Warren, so called because the works resembled a rabbit warren. This was on the lower side and to the left end of Vimy Ridge, and was extra dangerous. It did seem as though each place was worse than the last. The Warren was a regular network of trenches, burrows, and funk holes, and we needed them all.
