The position was downhill from the Huns, and they kept sending over and down a continuous stream of “pip-squeaks,” “whiz-bangs,” and “minnies.” The “pip-squeak” is a shell that starts with a silly *pip*, goes on with a sillier *squeeeeee*, and goes off with a man’s-size bang.

The “whiz-bang” starts with a rough whirr like a flushing cock partridge, and goes off on contact with a tremendous bang. It is not as dangerous as it sounds, but bad enough.

The “minnie” is about the size of a two-gallon kerosene can, and comes somersaulting over in a high arc and is concentrated death and destruction when it lands. It has one virtue﻿—you can see it coming and dodge, and at night it most considerately leaves a trail of sparks.

===Chinese===
在采石场待了几天，我们移防到一处叫“兔子窝”（the Warren）的地方——得名是因为那片工事活脱脱一个兔子窝。它在维米岭的下坡一侧、靠左端，格外危险。每换一地都比上一地更糟，看来真是如此。兔子窝是战壕、地洞和藏身洞（funk hole）盘成的一整片网，而这网处处都用得着。

我们的阵地处在德国佬（Hun）的下坡，他们便不停地成串把“吱哇弹”（pip-squeak）、“嗖砰弹”（whiz-bang）和“明妮”（minnie）泼过来、砸下来。“吱哇弹”是一种炮弹，起手一声傻乎乎的*噗*，接着一声更傻的*吱————*，末了炸出一声配得上男子汉分量的巨响。

“嗖砰弹”起手是一声沙沙的啸响，像受惊腾起的公鹧鸪，一触地便轰然炸出巨响。它不像听上去那么危险，可也够呛。

“明妮”差不多有两加仑煤油罐那么大，翻着跟头划一道高高的弧线过来，一落地就是浓缩的死亡与毁灭。它有一条好处——你看得见它来，来得及闪避；夜里它还极其体贴地拖一条火花的尾巴。


===Original===
The Boche served us full portions of all three of these man-killers in the Warren and kept us ducking in and out pretty much all the time, night and day.

I was lucky enough after the first day to be put on sappers’ duty. The Sappers, or Engineers, are the men whose duty it is to run mines under No Man’s Land and plant huge quantities of explosives. There was a great amount of mining going on all the time at Vimy Ridge from both sides.

Sometimes Fritz would run a sap out reasonably near the surface, and we would counter with one lower down. Then he’d go us one better and go still deeper. Some of the mines went down and under hundreds of feet. The result of all this was that on our side at least, the Sappers were undermanned and a good many infantry were drafted into that service.

===Chinese===
在兔子窝，德国佬把这三样杀人利器足份足量地招待我们，害得我们几乎昼夜不停地缩头缩脑、钻进钻出。

头一天过后，我就够走运，被派去干工兵（Sappers）的差事。工兵，也就是工程兵，职责是在无人地带底下掘坑道、埋设大量炸药。维米岭一带，双方的坑道作业从早到晚没停过，规模大得很。

有时候弗里茨（Fritz，指德国兵）把支坑道掘得相当贴近地表，我们就挖一条更低的来应招；他便再高一招，挖得更深。有些坑道掘下去几百英尺，又从底下横掏过去。这一切的结果是，至少在我们这边，工兵人手不足，一大批步兵被抽去干这一行。


===Original===
I had charge of a gang and had to fill sandbags with the earth removed from the end of the sap and get it out and pile the bags on the parapets. We were well out toward the German lines and deep under the hill when we heard them digging below us. An engineer officer came in and listened for an hour and decided that they were getting in explosives and that it was up to us to beat them to it. Digging stopped at once and we began rushing in H.E. in fifty-pound boxes. I was ordered back into supports with my section.

Right here I began to have luck. Just see how this worked out. First a rushing party was organized whose duty it was to rush the crater made by the mine explosion and occupy it before the Germans got there. Sixty men were selected, a few from each company, and placed where they were supposedly safe, but where they could get up fast. This is the most dangerous duty an infantryman has to do, because both sides after a mine explosion shower in fifty-seven varieties of sudden death, including a perfect rain of machine-gun bullets. The chances of coming out of a rushing party with a whole hide are about one in five.

Well, for a wonder, I didn’t get drawn for this one, and I breathed one long, deep sigh of relief, put my hand inside my tunic and patted Dinky on the back. Dinky is my mascot. I’ll tell you about him later.

===Chinese===
我领着一队人，得把坑道头上挖出来的土装进沙袋，运出去，把沙袋堆上胸墙。我们已经深深掘进到山底下、直逼德国人防线的时候，听见他们就在我们下面挖。一名工兵军官进来听了整整一个钟头，断定对方正在往里装炸药，我们得抢到他们前头。挖掘当即停下，我们开始把五十磅一箱的高爆炸药（H.E.）往里抢运。我奉命带着我那班人退回预备队。

打这儿起，我的好运开了头。且看这事是怎么演变的。先说，一支突击队组了起来，任务是一等坑道炸药起爆就冲上炸出的弹坑，赶在德国人之前把它占住。六十人入选，每个连抽几个，安置在号称安全、又能一跃而起的地方。这是步兵要干的最凶险的差事，因为坑道一响，双方都会把五十七个品种的暴死劈头泼进弹坑，外加一场地地道道的机枪弹骤雨。从突击队里一根汗毛不少地全身而退的机会，大约五分之一。

结果呢，说来稀奇，这一回竟没抽到我。我长长地、深深地舒了一口气，把手伸进军上衣里，拍了拍丁奇（Dinky）的背。丁奇是我的吉祥物。回头我再讲他的故事。


===Original===
On top of that another bit of luck came along, though it didn’t seem like it at the moment. It was the custom for a ration party to go out each night and get up the grub. This party had to go over the duck walk and was under fire both going and coming. One of the corporals who had been out on rations two nights in succession began to “grouse.”

Of course Sergeant Page spotted me and detailed me to the “wangler’s” duty. I “groused” too, like a good fellow, but had to go.

“Garn,” says Wellsie. “Wot’s the diff if yer gets it ’ere or there. If ye clicks, I’ll draw yer fags from Blighty and say a prayer for yer soul. On yer way.”

===Chinese===
紧跟着又来了一桩好运，尽管当时瞧着不像。照惯例，每晚要派一支口粮队出去，把吃食运上来。这队人得走木板路（duck walk），来回都在炮火底下。有个下士连着两夜出去运口粮，开始“发牢骚”（grouse）了。

于是，佩奇军士（Sergeant Page）一眼相中了我，把我点到那桩“被滑头躲掉的差事”（wangler’s duty）上。我也像个好弟兄似的“发牢骚”，可还是得去。

“得了，”威尔西（Wellsie）说。“在这儿挨一下还是在那儿挨一下，有啥两样。你要是送了命，我就从布莱蒂（Blighty）替你把香烟领走，再替你的灵魂念句祷文。上路吧。”


===Original===
Cheerful beggar, Wellsie. He was doing me a favor and didn’t know it.

I did the three miles along the duck walk with the ration party, and there wasn’t a shell came our way. Queer! Nor on the way back. Queerer! When we were nearly back and were about five hundred yards from the base of the Pimple, a dead silence fell on the German side of the line. There wasn’t a gun nor a mortar nor even a rifle in action for a mile in either direction. There was, too, a kind of sympathetic letup on our side. There weren’t any lights going up. There was an electric tension in the very air. You could tell by the feel that something big was going to happen.

I halted the ration party at the end of the duck walk and waited. But not for long. Suddenly the “Very” lights went up from the German side, literally in hundreds, illuminating the top of the ridge and the sky behind with a thin greenish white flare. Then came a deep rumble that shook the ground, and a dull boom. A spurt of bloodred flame squirted up from the near side of the hill, and a rolling column of gray smoke.

===Chinese===
好一个乐观的家伙，这个威尔西。他这是帮了我一个大忙，自己还不知道。

我跟着口粮队沿木板路走了三英里，一路上竟没有一发炮弹朝我们来。怪事！回程也没有。更怪！快到的时候，我们离“疙瘩丘”（the Pimple）的山脚约五百码，德军那一边骤然死寂下来。前后一英里之内，没有一门炮、一门迫击炮、甚至一支步枪在响。我们这边也像是有默契似的歇了火。没有一发照明弹升空。空气里弥漫着一种触电般的紧张。凭那股气氛你就感觉得出来：要出大事了。

我让口粮队在木板路尽头停下等候。没等多久。忽然，德军那边的“维里”信号弹腾空而起，实实在在数以百计，用一种淡绿泛白的光照亮了岭顶和山后的天空。跟着是一声震得地面发颤的深沉轰响，一记沉闷的巨爆。血红的火焰从山的近侧喷起，腾起一根滚滚翻卷的灰烟柱。


===Original===
Then another rumble, and another, and then the whole side of the ridge seemed to open up and move slowly skyward with a world-wrecking, soul-paralyzing crash. A murky red glare lit up the smoke screen, and against it a mass of tossed-up debris, and for an instant I caught the black silhouette of a whole human body spreadeagled and spinning like a pinwheel.

Most of our party, even at the distance, were knocked down by the gigantic impact of the explosion. A shower of earth and rock chunks, some as big as a barrel, fell around us.

Then we heard a faraway cheering, and in the light of the flares we saw a newly made hill and our men swarming up it to the crater. Two mines had exploded, and the whole side of the Pimple had been torn away. Half of our rushing party were killed and we had sixty casualties from shock and wounds among men who were supposed to be at a safe distance from the mining operation. But we took and held the new crater positions.

The corporal whose place I had taken on the ration party was killed by falling stones. Inasmuch as he was where I would have been, I considered that I had had a narrow escape from “going west!” More luck!

===Chinese===
接着又是一声，再一声，随后整面山坡仿佛豁然裂开，缓缓朝天掀去，带着一声毁天灭地、叫人魂飞魄散的巨响。昏红的火光照亮烟幕，映出漫天抛起的碎土乱石；有一刹那，我瞥见一具完整人体的黑色剪影，四肢大张，像只纸风车似的打着转。

离得那么远，我们队里大多数人还是叫爆炸的巨大冲击掀翻在地。一阵泥土石块雨劈头盖脸砸下来，有的块头大得像只木桶，落在我们四周。

接着我们听见远处的欢呼声。照明弹的光里，我们看见一座新堆出来的小山，我们的人正蜂拥往上爬，直扑弹坑。两处坑道炸药都起了爆，疙瘩丘的整面山坡被生生掀掉。我们突击队一半人阵亡；那些本该待在坑道作业安全距离之外的人里，还有六十名不是震伤就是挂了彩。但新的弹坑阵地，我们夺下来守住了。

口粮队上那个叫我顶替的下士，被落下的石头砸死了。既然他站的是我本来该站的位置，我就算从“上西天”（going west）里擦边捡回了一条命。又是一桩好运！

## V Feeding the Tommies / 五、给汤米们开饭

===Original===
Food is a burning issue in the lives of all of us. It is the main consideration with the soldier. His life is simplified to two principal motives, i.e., keeping alive himself and killing the other fellow. The question uppermost in his mind every time and all of the time, is, “When do we eat?”

In the trenches the backbone of Tommy’s diet is bully beef, “Maconochie’s Ration,” cheese, bread or biscuit, jam, and tea. He may get some of this hot or he may eat it from the tin, all depending upon how badly Fritz is behaving.

In billets the diet is more varied. Here he gets some fresh meat, lots of bacon, and the bully and the Maconochie’s come along in the form of stew. Also there is fresh bread and some dried fruit and a certain amount of sweet stuff.

===Chinese===
吃饭，是我们每个人生活里头一桩火烧眉毛的大事，对当兵的来说更是头等要紧。他的日子被简化成了两大动机：一是让自己活下去，二是把对面那家伙干掉。他脑子里从早到晚、时时刻刻顶在最上面的那个问题只有一个：“什么时候开饭？”

在战壕里，汤米（Tommy，英国兵的通称）伙食的顶梁柱，是咸牛肉罐头（bully beef）、“马科诺基口粮”（Maconochie’s Ration）、干酪、面包或硬饼干、果酱，还有茶。这些东西有时能吃上热的，有时只能开罐冷吃，全看弗里茨（Fritz，指德国兵）闹腾得凶不凶。

到了宿营地，伙食花样就多了些。在这儿他能吃上点鲜肉、大量的咸肉，咸牛肉和马科诺基也改头换面，炖成一大锅。还有新鲜面包、一些干果，外加一定分量的甜食。

===Original===
It was this matter of grub that made my life a burden in the billets at Petite-Saens. I had been rather proud of being lance corporal. It was, to me, the first step along the road to being field marshal. I found, however, that a corporal is high enough to take responsibility and to get bawled out for anything that goes wrong. He’s not high enough to command any consideration from those higher up, and he is so close to the men that they take out their grievances on him as a matter of course. He is neither fish, flesh, nor fowl, and his life is a burden.

I had the job of issuing the rations of our platoon, and it nearly drove me mad. Every morning I would detail a couple of men from our platoon to be standing mess orderlies for the day. They would fetch the char and bacon from the field kitchen in the morning and clean up the “dixies” after breakfast. The “dixie,” by the way, is an iron box or pot, oblong in shape, capacity about four or five gallons. It fits into the field kitchen and is used for roasts, stews, char, or anything else. The cover serves to cook bacon in.

Field kitchens are drawn by horses and follow the battalion everywhere that it is safe to go, and to some places where it isn’t. Two men are detailed from each company to cook, and there is usually another man who gets the sergeants’ mess, besides the officers’ cook, who does not as a rule use the field kitchen, but prepares the food in the house taken as the officers’ mess.

===Chinese===
在佩蒂特-桑（Petite-Saens）的宿营地里，正是伙食这档子事，把我的日子搅成了苦役。当上代理下士（lance corporal），我原本还挺得意——在我看来，那可是通往陆军元帅宝座的第一级台阶。谁知我很快发现：下士这个衔，已经高到出了任何岔子都得担责任、挨臭骂；却又不够高，够不上叫上头的人高看一眼；而且他跟士兵们贴得太近，大伙儿一肚子怨气，自然而然全撒在他身上。他非鱼非肉又非鸡，不三不四，日子难熬。

我摊上的差事是给全排发口粮，这活儿差点把我逼疯。每天早上，我从排里指派两个人，当一整天的伙食值勤兵（mess orderly）。他们一早去野战厨房领茶（char，兵俚）和咸肉，早饭后再把“迪希锅”（dixie）刷洗干净。顺便说一句，迪希锅是一种铁皮箱或铁锅，长方形，容量约四五加仑，正好嵌进野战厨房，烤肉、炖菜、烧茶，干什么都行，锅盖还能拿来煎咸肉。

野战厨房由马拉着，营队开到哪儿，它跟到哪儿——凡是去得安全的地方都去，有些不安全的地方也照去不误。每个连抽两个人当厨子，通常还另有一人专管军士们的伙食；此外还有军官的厨子，他照例不用野战厨房，而是在被征作军官食堂的那栋房子里另行备膳。

===Original===
As far as possible, the company cooks are men who were cooks in civil life, but not always. We drew a plumber and a navvy (road builder)﻿—and the grub tasted of both trades. The way our company worked the kitchen problem was to have stew for two platoons one day and roast dinner for the others, and then reverse the order next day, so that we didn’t have stew all the time. There were not enough “dixies” for us all to have stew the same day.

Every afternoon I would take my mess orderlies and go to the quartermaster’s stores and get our allowance and carry it back to the billets in waterproof sheets. Then the stuff that was to be cooked in the kitchen went there, and the bread and that sort of material was issued direct to the men. That was where my trouble started.

The powers that were had an uncanny knack of issuing an odd number of articles to go among an even number of men, and vice versa. There would be eleven loaves of bread to go to a platoon of fifty men divided into four sections. Some of the sections would have ten men and some twelve or thirteen.

===Chinese===
连里的厨子，尽量挑平生就干过厨师的人来当，但也不尽然。我们连摊上一个管子工和一个修路工——饭菜里全是他俩本行的味道。我们连对付厨房难题的办法是：一天给两个排做炖菜，其余的排吃烤肉正餐，第二天再对调过来，免得顿顿都是炖菜。迪希锅不够数，没法让全连同一天都吃炖的。

每天下午，我带着伙食值勤兵上军需库领全排的配给，用雨布兜回宿营地。要下锅的东西送去厨房，面包之类的吃食则直接发到弟兄们手里。我的麻烦，就是从这儿开始的。

上头那些管事的有一手邪门本事：发下来的东西件数是单数，要分的人偏偏是双数，要不就倒过来。五十个人的一个排、分四个班，偏发给你十一条面包。有的班十个人，有的班十二三个。

===Original===
The British Tommy is a scrapper when it comes to his rations. He reminds me of an English sparrow. He’s always right in there wangling for his own. He will bully and browbeat if he can, and he will coax and cajole if he can’t. It would be “Hi sye, corporal. They’s ten men in Number 2 section and fourteen in ourn. An’ blimme if you hain’t guv ’em four loaves, same as ourn. Is it right, I arsks yer? Is it?” Or,

“Lookee! Do yer call that a loaf o’ bread? Looks like the A.S.C. (Army Service Corps) been using it fer a piller. Gimme another, will yer, corporal?”

When it comes to splitting seven onions nine ways, I defy anyone to keep peace in the family, and every doggoned Tommy would hold out for his onion whether he liked ’em or not. Same way with a bottle of pickles to go among eleven men or a handful of raisins or apricots. Or jam or butter or anything, except bully beef or Maconochie. I never heard anyone “argue the toss” on either of those commodities.

===Chinese===
一沾口粮，英国汤米个个是能吵善斗的主儿。他让我想起英国麻雀：永远扎堆扑腾，钻营自己那一份。能唬人的时候，他就连吓带压；唬不成，他就连哄带磨。张嘴就是：“嘿我说，下士。二班拢共十个人，俺们班十四个。好家伙，你发给他们的面包居然跟俺们一样多，都是四个。俺问你，这公道吗？啊？这公道吗？”再不然就是——

“你瞅瞅！这也配叫面包？瞧着倒像是叫陆军勤务队（A.S.C.）的爷们儿枕着睡过觉。再给俺来一个行不行，下士？”

至于七个洋葱分九份的差事，我看谁来了也摆不平这一家子，而且每个死心眼的汤米都非咬定自己那份洋葱不可——管他爱不爱吃。一瓶腌菜分给十一个人、一小把葡萄干或杏干分给全排，全是一个道理。果酱、黄油，样样如此——唯独咸牛肉和马科诺基例外。这两样东西，我从没听谁“争过一句嘴”。

===Original===
Bully is high-grade corned beef in cans and is OK if you like it, but it does get tiresome.

Maconochie ration is put up a pound to the can and bears a label which assures the consumer that it is a scientifically prepared, well-balanced ration. Maybe so. It is my personal opinion that the inventor brought to his task an imperfect knowledge of cookery and a perverted imagination. Open a can of Maconochie and you find a gooey gob of grease, like rancid lard. Investigate and you find chunks of carrot and other unidentifiable material, and now and then a bit of mysterious meat. The first man who ate an oyster had courage, but the last man who ate Maconochie’s unheated had more. Tommy regards it as a very inferior grade of garbage. The label notwithstanding, he’s right.

Many people have asked me what to send our soldiers in the line of food. I’d say stick to sweets. Cookies of any durable kind﻿—I mean that will stand chance moisture﻿—the sweeter the better, and if possible those containing raisins or dried fruit. Figs, dates, etc., are good. And, of course, chocolate. Personally, I never did have enough chocolate. Candy is acceptable, if it is of the sort to stand more or less rough usage which it may get before it reaches the soldier. Chewing gum is always received gladly. The army issue of sweets is limited pretty much to jam, which gets to taste all alike.

===Chinese===
咸牛肉是罐装的上等腌碎牛肉，你要好这口，它没毛病，就是架不住天长日久，实在吃腻。

马科诺基口粮一罐一磅，罐子上贴着标签，向消费者郑重保证：这是一份科学配制、营养均衡的口粮。也许是吧。依我个人的看法，发明这东西的人，干这行靠的是半吊子的厨艺，外加一副长歪了的想象力。开一罐马科诺基，扑面就是一摊黏糊糊的油脂，像变了质的猪油。往里探究，能翻出成块的胡萝卜和别的些没法辨认的东西，时不时还冒出一小块来路不明的肉。头一个吃牡蛎的人有胆量，可最后一个吃冷马科诺基的人，胆量更大。在汤米眼里，这东西顶多算下等泔水。别管标签上吹什么，他说得对。

许多人问过我，该给前线的大兵们寄点什么吃的。我说，认准甜食寄。各种经得住放的曲奇——我是说不怕路上受潮的那种——越甜越好，最好里头带葡萄干或干果。无花果干、椰枣什么的都不错。当然，还有巧克力。就我个人而言，巧克力从来没有够过的时候。糖果也受欢迎，前提是经得起磕碰折腾——在送到大兵手里之前，路上少不得要挨些折腾。口香糖人人都乐意收。军队配给的甜食来来去去基本就是果酱，吃得全一个味儿。

===Original===
It is pathetic to see some of the messes Tommy gets together to fill his craving for dessert. The favorite is a slum composed of biscuit, water, condensed milk, raisins, and chocolate. If some of you folks at home would get one look at that concoction, let alone tasting it, you would dash out and spend your last dollar for a package to send to some lad “over there.”

After the excitement of dodging shells and bullets in the front trenches, life in billets seems dull. Tommy has too much time to get into mischief. It was at Petite-Saens that I first saw the Divisional Follies. This was a vaudeville show by ten men who had been actors in civil life, and who were detailed to amuse the soldiers. They charged a small admission fee and the profit went to the Red Cross.

There ought to be more recreation for the soldiers of all armies. The Y.M.C.A. is to take care of that with our boys.

===Chinese===
看看汤米为解甜瘾捣鼓出来的那些吃食，真叫人心酸。最受欢迎的是一种“杂拌糊”（slum），配料是饼干、清水、炼乳、葡萄干和巧克力。家里的诸位只要朝那锅糊糊瞅上一眼——都不用尝——保准立刻冲出门去，掏出兜里最后一块钱，买个包裹寄给“那边”的哪个小伙子。

在前沿战壕里躲够了炮弹子弹、受够了刺激之后，宿营地的日子就显得没劲透了。汤米闲工夫一多，就容易招灾惹祸。我头一回看“师部滑稽剧团”（Divisional Follies）演出，就是在佩蒂特-桑。那是个歌舞杂耍班子，十个从前当过演员的兵，专门抽调出来给大兵们逗乐。他们收一点门票钱，收益全捐给红十字会。

各国的士兵都该有更多的娱乐消遣。我们家小伙子们的这一摊，就交给基督教青年会（Y.M.C.A.）了。

===Original===
By the way, we had a Y.M.C.A. hut at Petite-Saens, and I cannot say enough for this great work. No one who has not been there can know what a blessing it is to be able to go into a clean, warm, dry place and sit down to reading or games and to hear good music. Personally I am a little bit sorry that the secretaries are to be in khaki. They weren’t when I left. And it sure did seem good to see a man in civilian’s clothes. You get after a while so you hate the sight of a uniform.

Another thing about the Y.M.C.A. I could wish that they would have more women in the huts. Not frilly, frivolous society girls, but women from thirty-five to fifty. A soldier likes kisses as well as the next. And he takes them when he finds them. And he finds too many. But what he really wants, though, is the chance to sit down and tell his troubles to some nice, sympathetic woman who is old enough to be levelheaded.

Nearly every soldier reverts more or less to a boyish point of view. He hankers for somebody to mother him. I should be glad to see many women of that type in the Y.M.C.A. work. It is one of the great needs of our army that the boys should be amused and kept clean mentally and morally. I don’t believe there is any organization better qualified to do this than the Y.M.C.A.

===Chinese===
顺带一说，佩蒂特-桑就有一座基督教青年会小屋，这份大功德，我怎么夸都夸不够。没在那种境地待过的人，体会不到能走进一间干净、暖和、干燥的屋子，坐下来看看书、打打牌、听听好音乐，是多大的福气。说句私心话，听说青年会的干事们往后要穿上卡其军装，我还有点遗憾。我离开的时候，他们还穿便装呢。能看见一个穿便服的人，那感觉真是没得说。日子一久，你见了军装就腻得慌。

关于青年会，还有一件事。我倒盼着小屋里能多些女工作人员。不要花里胡哨、轻浮虚荣的交际花，要三十五到五十岁的女人。当兵的跟旁人一样喜欢香吻，找得到机会也照亲不误。可他们找到的机会实在太多了。他真正想要的，其实是能坐下来，把心里的苦水倒给一位和善体贴、上了年纪、头脑冷静的女人听。

差不多每个当兵的都会或多或少退回孩子气的心思里去，眼巴巴盼着有个人来疼他、拿他当孩子哄。青年会的工作里要是能多些这样的女人，我求之不得。让小伙子们有得乐、思想上和品性上都干干净净，这是我们军队的一大需要。我看，没有哪个机构干这差事比基督教青年会更合格的了。

===Original===
Most of our chaps spent their time “on their own” either in the Y.M.C.A. hut or in the estaminets while we were in Petite-Saens. Our stop there was hardly typical of the rest in billets. Usually “rest” means that you are set to mending roads or some such fatigue duty. At Petite-Saens, however, we had it “cushy.”

The routine was about like this: Up at 6:30, we fell in for three-quarters of an hour physical drill or bayonet practice. Breakfast. Inspection of ammo and gas masks. One hour drill. After that, “on our own,” with nothing to do but smoke, read, and gamble.

Tommy is a great smoker. He gets a fag issue from the government, if he is lucky, of two packets or twenty a week. This lasts him with care about two days. After that he goes smokeless unless he has friends at home to send him a supply. I had friends in London who sent me about five hundred fags a week, and I was consequently popular while they lasted. This took off some of the curse of being a lance corporal.

===Chinese===
驻在佩蒂特-桑那阵子，我们大部分人一到“自由活动”时间，不是泡在青年会小屋，就是泡在小酒馆（estaminet）里。我们那趟驻扎，实在算不上宿营地“休整”的典型。平常所谓“休整”，多半是打发你去修路，或者干些诸如此类的杂役（fatigue）。不过在佩蒂特-桑，我们过得可“舒坦”（cushy）了。

一天的日程大致是这样：6 点 30 分起床，集合出三刻钟的操，练体操或拼刺刀。早饭。检查弹药和防毒面具。操练一个钟头。之后便是“自由活动”，除了抽烟、看书、赌钱，再没别的事可干。

汤米是天大的烟鬼。运气好的话，政府每周发他两包“香烟”（fag），二十支。省着抽，也就撑两天。往后就得干熬，除非家里有朋友源源不断寄烟来。我在伦敦有朋友，每星期给我寄五百来支烟，烟没断的那阵子，我人缘好得很。这多少冲掉了代理下士这差事的一些晦气。

===Original===
Tommy has his favorite in “fags” like anybody else. He likes above all Wild Woodbines. This cigarette is composed of glue, cheap paper, and a poor quality of hay. Next in his affection comes Goldflakes﻿—pretty near as bad.

People over here who have boys at the front mustn’t forget the cigarette supply. Send them along early and often. There’ll never be too many. Smoking is one of the soldier’s few comforts. Two bits’ worth of makin’s a week will help one lad make life endurable. It’s cheap at the price. Come through for the smoke fund whenever you get the chance.

Café life among us at Petite-Saens was mostly drinking and gambling. That is not half as bad as it sounds. The drinking was mostly confined to the slushy French beer and *vin blanc* and citron. Whiskey and absinthe were barred.

===Chinese===
抽烟，汤米跟旁人一样各有心头好。他顶爱的是“野伍德拜恩”（Wild Woodbines）。这种香烟的成分是：浆糊、贱纸，外加一等劣质的干草。感情排第二位的是“金箔”（Goldflakes）——恶劣程度不相上下。

家里有孩子在前线的诸位，千万别断了香烟这条补给线。要寄就趁早、勤寄。多少都不会嫌多。抽烟是当兵的为数不多的几样安慰之一。一星期两毛五分钱的烟丝（makin’s），就能帮一个小伙子把日子熬下去。这价钱买这效果，天大的便宜。但凡有机会，就为“烟草基金”出把力吧。

在佩蒂特-桑，我们的“咖啡馆生活”主要就是喝酒加赌钱。这可没有听上去一半那么糟。喝的无非是温吞寡淡的法国啤酒、白葡萄酒（vin blanc）和柠檬水（citron）。威士忌和苦艾酒是明令禁绝的。

===Original===
The gambling was on a small scale, necessarily, the British soldier not being at any time a bloated plutocrat. At the same time the games were continuous. “House” was the most popular. This is a game similar to the “lotto” we used to play as children. The backers distribute cards having fifteen numbers, forming what they call a school. Then numbered cardboard squares are drawn from a bag, the numbers being called out. When a number comes out which appears on your card, you cover it with a bit of match. If you get all your numbers covered, you call out “house,” winning the pot. If there are ten people in at a franc a head, the banker holds out two francs, and the winner gets eight.

It is really quite exciting, as you may get all but one number covered and be rooting for a certain number to come. Usually when you get as close as that and sweat over a number for ten minutes, somebody else gets his first. Corporal Wells described the game as one where the winner “ ’ollers ’ouse and the rest ’ollers ’ell!”

Some of the nicknames for the different numbers remind one of the slang of the crap shooter. For instance, “Kelly’s eye” means one. “Clickety click” is sixty-six. “Top of the house” is ninety. Other games are “crown and anchor,” which is a dice game, and “pontoon,” which is a card game similar to “twenty-one” or “seven and a half.” Most of these are mildly discouraged by the authorities, “house” being the exception. But in any estaminet in a billet town you’ll find one or all of them in progress all the time. The winner usually spends his winnings for beer, so the money all goes the same way, game or no game.

===Chinese===
赌钱自然是小米小来——英国大兵不管什么时候都当不成脑满肠肥的大财主。不过赌局是从不间断的。最流行的是“豪斯”（House）。这玩法跟咱们小时候玩的“洛托”（lotto）差不多。庄家发下印着十五个号码的卡片，凑成他们所谓的“一班人”。然后从袋子里摸出写有号码的纸板方片，唱一个号码。你卡片上的号码被唱到，就拿一截火柴把它盖上。要是卡上的号码全盖满了，你就喊一声“豪斯”，赢走彩池。十个人每人出一法郎入局，庄家抽留两法郎，赢家得八法郎。

这游戏当真刺激得很——你多半会只剩最后一个号码没盖到，眼巴巴望着某个号码蹦出来。而通常的情形是：你就差这么一步，为一个号码干熬了十分钟，结果别人先喊了“豪斯”。威尔斯下士（Wells）给这游戏下过一个定义：赢家嗷一嗓子“豪斯”，其余人嗷一嗓子“见鬼”！

各个号码还有些诨名，让人想起掷骰子赌徒的黑话。比如“凯利的眼睛”是一，“咔嗒咔哒”是六十六，“屋顶”是九十。别的赌戏还有“王冠与锚”，那是掷骰子的；还有“庞墩”（pontoon），一种纸牌戏，跟“二十一点”或“七点半”差不多。这些赌戏大多被当局不冷不热地劝止，唯独“豪斯”是个例外。可不管哪个宿营小镇的小酒馆，你随时都能撞见其中一样在开局，或者样样都在开局。赢家通常转头就把赢来的钱换成啤酒，所以不管赌不赌，钱最后都流进同一个去处。

===Original===
When there are no games on, there is usually a singsong going. We had a merry young nuisance in our platoon named Rolfe, who had a voice like a frog and who used to insist upon singing on all occasions. Rolfie would climb on the table in the estaminet and sing numerous unprintable verses of his own, entitled “Oh, What a Merry Plyce is Hengland.” The only redeeming feature of this song was the chorus, which everybody would roar out and which went like this:

> Cheer, ye beggars, cheer!
 

 Britannia rules the wave!
 

 ’Ard times, short times
 

 Never’ll come agyne.
 

 Shoutin’ out at th’ top o’ yer lungs:
 

 Damn the German army!
 

 Oh, wot a lovely plyce is Hengland!

Our ten days *en repos* at Petite-Saens came to an end all too soon.

===Chinese===
赌局不开的时候，通常就有人起头唱歌。我们排里有个讨人嫌又逗人乐的小年轻，名叫罗尔夫（Rolfe），嗓音像青蛙，还专爱逢场必唱。罗尔菲（Rolfie）会蹬上小酒馆的桌子，唱他自编的《哦，英格兰是个多快活的好地方》，歌词一长串，多到没法印出来。这首歌唯一可取之处是副歌，人人都能扯着嗓子吼上两句，词儿是这样的：

> 快活点，你们这帮叫花子，快活点！
 

 不列颠尼亚统御着波涛！
 

 苦日子、紧日子
 

 再也不会回来喽。
 

 扯开嗓门可劲儿吼：
 

 去他妈的德国军队！
 

 哦，英格兰这地方多可爱！

在佩蒂特-桑“休整”（en repos）的十天，转眼就到了头，快得叫人不甘心。

===Original===
On the last day we lined up for our official “bawth.”

Petite-Saens was a coal-mining town. The mines were still operated, but only at night﻿—this to avoid shelling from the Boche long-distance artillery, which are fully capable of sending shells and hitting the mark at eighteen miles. The water system of the town depended upon the pumping apparatus of the mines. Every morning early, before the pressure was off, all hands would turn out for a general “sluicing” under the hydrants. We were as clean as could be and fairly free of “cooties” at the end of a week, but official red tape demanded that we go through an authorized scouring.

On the last day we lined up for this at dawn before an old warehouse which had been fitted with crude showers. We were turned in twenty in a batch and were given four minutes to soap ourselves all over and rinse off. I was in the last lot and had just lathered up good and plenty when the water went dead. If you want to reach the acme of stickiness, try this stunt. I felt like the inside of a mucilage bottle for a week.

===Chinese===
最后一天，我们列队去接受官方安排的“洗澡”（“bawth”，伦敦佬口音）。

佩蒂特-桑是座煤矿小镇。矿井还在开工，但只在夜里开——为的是躲德国佬（Boche）远程炮兵的轰击，那种大炮完全有本事把炮弹打到十八英里开外，还正中目标。镇上的供水系统，全指望矿井的水泵设备。每天一大早，趁水压还没断，全体弟兄倾巢而出，在消防栓底下集体“冲个透”。一个礼拜下来，我们已经干净得没法再干净，虱子（cootie）也差不多绝了迹，可官方的繁文缛节规定，我们还必须履行一道经上级批准的正式搓洗。

最后一天，天刚破晓，我们列队来到一座旧仓库前，里面装了些简易淋浴。二十人一批往里送，给四分钟，从头到脚打肥皂再冲净。我排在最后一批，刚把肥皂沫子打得十足十，水就断了。你要是想体验黏糊糊的最高境界，不妨试试这一手。整整一个礼拜，我都觉得自己像浆糊瓶子的内壁。

===Original===
After the official purification we were given clean underwear. And then there was a howl. The fresh underthings had been boiled and sterilized, but the immortal cootie had come through unscathed and in all its vigor. Corporal Wells raised a pathetic wail:

“Blimme eyes, mytie! I got more’n two ’undred now an’ this supposed to be a bloomin’ clean shirt! Why, the blinkin’ thing’s as lousy as a cookoo now, an me just a-gittin’ rid o’ the bloomin’ chats on me old un. Strike me pink if it hain’t a bleedin’ crime! Someone ought to write to *John Bull* abaht it!”

*John Bull* is the English paper of that name published by Horatio Bottomley, which makes a specialty of publishing complaints from soldiers and generally criticising the conduct of army affairs.

Well, we got through the bath and the next day were on our way. This time it was up the line to another sector. My one taste of trench action had made me keen for more excitement, and in spite of the comfortable time at Petite-Saens, I was glad to go. I was yet to know the real horrors and hardships of modern warfare. There were many days in those to come when I looked back upon Petite-Saens as a sort of heaven.

===Chinese===
官方的净化仪式完毕，发下来干净的内衣裤。紧接着就炸了锅。这批新内衣明明煮过、消过毒，可那不死的老虱子毫发无伤、劲头十足地挺了过来。威尔斯下士发出一声悲怆的哀嚎：

“我的老天爷，乖乖！俺这会儿身上都两百多只了，这还他娘的算件‘干净衬衫’！你瞅瞅，这倒霉玩意儿这会儿虱子多得跟布谷鸟似的，俺才刚把旧的那件上的虱崽子拾掇利索！我指天发誓，这简直是他娘的一桩罪过！该有人为这事给《约翰牛》（John Bull）写信！”

《约翰牛》是霍雷肖·博顿利（Horatio Bottomley）在英国办的同名刊物，专爱刊登士兵们的投诉，对军中事务总体上指指点点、多有批评。

就这样，澡洗完了，第二天我们便上了路。这一回是开赴前线，去另一个防区。战壕实战我只尝过一回滋味，正馋着更刺激的，所以虽然在佩蒂特-桑过得舒坦，我还是乐意开拔。现代战争真正的恐怖和苦楚，我那时还一无所知。在后来的许多天里，我回首佩蒂特-桑，总当它是一处天堂。

## IX First Sight of the Tanks / 九、初见坦克

===Original===
Late in the summer I accumulated a nice little case of trench fever.

This disease is due to remaining for long periods in the wet and mud, to racked nerves, and, I am inclined to think, to sleeping in the foul air of the dugouts. The chief symptom is high temperature, and the patient aches a good deal. I was sent back to a place in the neighborhood of Arras and was there a week recuperating.

While I was there a woman spy whom I had known in Abalaine was brought to the village and shot. The frequency with which the duck walk at Abalaine had been shelled, especially when ration parties or troops were going over it, had attracted a good deal of attention.

There was a single house not far from the end of that duck walk west of Abalaine, occupied by a woman and two or three children. She had lived there for years and was, so far as anybody knew, a Frenchwoman in breeding and sympathies. She was in the habit of selling coffee to the soldiers, and, of course, gossiped with them and thus gained a good deal of information about troop movements.

===Chinese===
夏末时节，我给自己攒下了一份小小的“厚礼”——战壕热（trench fever）。

这病，一是长年累月泡在湿里泥里，二是神经绷到了极限，三是——我倾向于这么看——睡在掩蔽部的污浊空气里睡出来的。主要症状是发高烧，病人浑身酸痛。我被送往阿拉斯（Arras）附近的一个地方，在那儿休养了一个星期。

我在那儿的时候，一个我在阿巴兰（Abalaine）就认识的女间谍被押到村里枪决了。阿巴兰那条木板路（duck walk，铺在泥地上的木排便道）挨炮轰之勤——尤其是每当口粮队或队伍正打上面经过的时候——早就引起了上头不小的注意。

阿巴兰以西、离那条木板路一端不远，孤零零立着一座房子，住着一个女人和两三个孩子。她在那儿住了好些年，据大家所知，无论出身还是心气，都是个法国女人。她惯于卖咖啡给士兵们，当然也同他们闲话家常，就这样打探到不少部队调动的消息。

===Original===
She was not suspected for a long time. Then a gunner of a battery which was stationed near by noticed that certain children’s garments, a red shirt and a blue one and several white garments, were on the clothesline in certain arrangement on the days when troops were to be moved along the duck walk the following night. This soldier notified his officers, and evidence was accumulated that the woman was signalling to the Boche airplanes.

She was arrested, taken to the rear, and shot. I don’t like to think that this woman was really French. She was, no doubt, one of the myriad of spies who were planted in France by the Germans long before the war.

After getting over the fever, I rejoined my battalion in the early part of September in the Somme district at a place called Mill Street. This was in reality a series of dugouts along a road some little distance behind our second lines, but in the range of the German guns, which persistently tried for our artillery just beside us.

Within an hour of my arrival I was treated to a taste of one of the forms of German kultur which was new at the time. At least it was new to me﻿—tear gas. This delectable vapor came over in shells, comparatively harmless in themselves, but which loosed a gas, smelling at first a little like pineapple. When you got a good inhale you choked, and the eyes began to run. There was no controlling the tears, and the victim would fairly drip for a long time, leaving him wholly incapacitated.

===Chinese===
好久都没有人疑心她。后来，驻扎在附近的一个炮兵连里，有个炮手注意到：凡是第二天夜里要有队伍沿木板路调动的日子，晾衣绳上总会按某种固定的搭配挂出几件童衣——一件红衬衫、一件蓝的，外加几件白的。这士兵报告了军官，证据一点点攒实了：那女人是在朝德国佬（Boche）的飞机打信号。

她被捕了，解往后方，枪决了。我不愿意去想这女人真是个法国人。毫无疑问，她是德国人早在战前就密密栽进法国的千万间谍中的一个。

热退之后，九月初，我在索姆河（Somme）地区一个叫米尔街（Mill Street）的地方回营归队。那地方其实是沿公路排开的一溜掩蔽部，在我们第二线阵地后面不远，却仍在德军炮火射程之内——德军炮一门心思要敲掉的，就是我们身旁不远的我方炮兵。

到达后不出一个钟头，我就被请着品尝了一道德意志“文化”（kultur）的新花样——当时还新鲜，至少对我是新鲜的——催泪毒气。这股“可口”的气体是装在炮弹里过来的，炮弹本身倒比较无害，只是弹一炸便放出一股气，起初闻着有点像菠萝。狠狠吸上一大口，人就呛得透不过气，眼泪随即决堤，怎么也收不住，遭殃的人能足足滴上好半天，整个人彻底干不成事。

===Original===
Goggles provided for this gas were nearly useless, and we all resorted to the regular gas helmet. In this way we were able to stand the stuff.

The gas mask, by the way, was the bane of my existence in the trenches﻿—one of the banes. I found that almost invariably after I had had mine on for a few minutes I got faint. Very often I would keel over entirely. A good many of the men were affected the same way, either from the lack of air inside the mask or by the influence of the chemicals with which the protector is impregnated.

One of the closest calls I had in all my war experience was at Mills Street. And Fritz was not to blame.

Several of the men, including myself, were squatted around a brazier cooking char and getting warm, for the nights were cold, when there was a terrific explosion. Investigation proved that an unexploded bomb had been buried under the brazier, and that it had gone off as the heat penetrated the ground. It is a wonder there weren’t more of these accidents, as Tommy was forever throwing away his Millses.

===Chinese===
为这种毒气配发的护目镜几乎不顶事，于是大家全用上了制式的防毒头盔。靠它，我们才算扛住了这玩意儿。

顺便说一句，防毒面具在战壕里简直是我命中的一大克星——克星之一。我发现自己几乎回回如此：面具一上脸，几分钟人就发虚，还常常干脆整个栽倒。好多弟兄也中这个招，要么是面具里空气不够，要么是让浸在面具布料里的化学药剂给熏的。

我整个从军经历里最悬的几遭当中，有一遭就出在米尔街。而这一遭，还赖不着弗里茨（Fritz，指德国兵）。

一天夜里，天冷得很，我们几个人——包括我在内——正围着一只火盆煮茶（char，兵俚的“茶”）烤火，忽然一声惊天动地的爆炸。一查才明白，火盆底下埋着一颗未爆的炸弹，热量渗进土里，把它给焐炸了。这类事故居然没有出得更多，才算得上一桩奇迹——汤米（Tommy，英国兵的通称）扔起米尔斯（Mills）手雷来，可是从来不知心疼的。

===Original===
The Mills bomb fires by pulling out a pin which releases a lever which explodes the bomb after four seconds. Lots of men never really trust a bomb. If you have one in your pocket, you feel that the pin may somehow get out, and if it does you know that you’ll go to glory in small bits. I always had that feeling myself and used to throw away my Millses and scoop a hatful of dirt over them with my foot.

This particular bomb killed one man, wounded several, and shocked all of us. Two of the men managed to “swing” a “blighty” case out of it. I could have done the same if I had been wise enough.

I think I ought to say a word right here about the psychology of the Tommy in swinging a “blighty” case.

It is the one first, last, and always ambition of the Tommy to get back to Blighty. Usually he isn’t “out there” because he wants to be but because he has to be. He is a patriot all right. His love of Blighty shows that. He will fight like a bag of wildcats when he gets where the fighting is, but he isn’t going around looking for trouble. He knows that his officers will find that for him aplenty.

===Chinese===
米尔斯手雷的打法是：拔出保险销，放开一根击杆，四秒钟后炸弹爆炸。好多人对炸弹始终信不过。口袋里揣着一颗，你就觉得那根保险销保不齐会自己跑出来；真跑出来，你清楚自己就要碎成小块上天堂了。我自己就老犯这个嘀咕，总把米尔斯手雷扔得远远的，再用脚扒上一帽子的土盖住。

这一颗炸死了一个弟兄，炸伤了好几个，还把我们全体震得不轻。有两个弟兄设法从这场祸事里给自己“运作”出一份“布莱蒂”（Blighty，英国兵对本土的叫法）伤情。我本来也能照方抓药——只怪自己不够精明。

我想，正好在这儿说上一句，谈谈汤米在“运作”布莱蒂伤情这门学问上的心理。

回布莱蒂，是汤米从头到尾、始终不渝、压倒一切的抱负。通常他“到那边去”，不是想去，而是不得不去。他爱国，这没得说——他对布莱蒂的爱就是明证。真到了打仗的地方，他会像一袋子野猫似的拼命，可他不会到处找仗打。他知道，这种事军官们自会替他找来，管够。

===Original===
When he gets letters from home and knows that the wife or the “nippers” or the old mother is sick, he wants to go home. And so he puts in his time hoping for a wound that will be “cushy” enough not to discommode him much and that will be bad enough to swing Blighty on. Sometimes when he wants very much to get back he stretches his conscience to the limit﻿—and it is pretty elastic anyhow﻿—and he fakes all sorts of illness. The M.O. is usually a bit too clever for Tommy, however, and out and out fakes seldom get by. Sometimes they do, and in the most unexpected cases.

I had a man named Isadore Epstein in my section who was instrumental in getting Blighty for himself and one other. Issy was a tailor by trade. He was no fighting man and didn’t pretend to be, and he didn’t care who knew it. He was wild to get a “blighty one” or shell shock, or anything that would take him home.

One morning as we were preparing to go over the top, and the men were a little jumpy and nervous, I heard a shot behind me, and a bullet chugged into the sandbags beside my head. I whirled around, my first thought being that someone of our own men was trying to do me in. This is a thing that sometimes happens to unpopular officers and less frequently to the men. But not in this case.

It was Issy Epstein. He had been monkeying with his rifle and had shot himself in the hand. Of course, Issy was at once under suspicion of a self-inflicted wound, which is one of the worst crimes in the calendar. But the suspicion was removed instantly. Issy was hopping around, raising a terrific row.

===Chinese===
家里来了信，知道老婆、“小崽子们”（nippers）或是老母亲病了，他就想回家。于是他把日子都花在盼一枪上：那一枪要够“舒坦”（cushy），不至于让他太受罪；又要够重，重到能换来布莱蒂。有时实在想回去想得厉害，他就把良心抻到极限——反正那东西弹性十足——装出各式各样的病来。不过军医官（M.O.）通常比汤米棋高一着，彻头彻尾的装病很少能蒙混过关。偶尔也有漏网的，而且偏在最想不到的案子上。

我那个班里有个人叫伊萨多·爱泼斯坦（Isadore Epstein），他就成功地给自己和另外一个弟兄弄到了布莱蒂。伊西（Issy）本行是裁缝。他不是打仗的料，也从不冒充是，而且不在乎谁晓得这一点。他一门心思巴望挨上一记“布莱蒂伤”，或者得个弹震症（shell shock），或是任何能送他回家的东西。

一天早上，我们正准备翻出壕顶冲锋（going over the top），弟兄们都有点紧张发毛，忽然我背后响了一枪，一颗子弹“噗”地钻进我脑袋旁边的沙袋。我猛地转身，头一个念头是：自己人里有人要干掉我。这种事在人缘差的军官身上时有发生，落到士兵头上就少些。不过这一回不是。

是伊西·爱泼斯坦。他摆弄步枪摆弄走了火，打伤了自己的手。当然，伊西立刻背上了自伤的嫌疑——自伤是军法里数得着的重罪之一。可嫌疑转眼就烟消云散：伊西正满地蹦跳，嚎得惊天动地。

===Original===
“Oi, oi,” he wailed. “I’m ruint. I’m ruint. My thimble finger is gone. My thimble finger! I’m ruint. Oi, oi, oi, oi.”

The poor fellow was so sincerely desolated over the loss of his necessary finger that I couldn’t accuse him of shooting himself intentionally. I detailed a man named Bealer to take Issy back to a dressing station. Well, Bealer never came back.

Months later in England I met up with Epstein and asked about Bealer. It seems that after Issy had been fixed up, the surgeon turned to Bealer and said:

“What’s the matter with you?”

===Chinese===
“哎哟，哎哟，”他哭嚎着，“我完啦。我完啦。我的顶针指头没了。我的顶针指头哇！我完啦。哎哟，哎哟，哎哟，哎哟。”

这可怜虫为丢掉那根吃饭要紧的指头伤心得那样情真意切，我怎么也没法指控他是故意朝自己开枪。我派了一个叫比勒（Bealer）的弟兄，送伊西回包扎所去。得，比勒这一去就再没回来。

好几个月后，我在英格兰碰上爱泼斯坦，问起比勒的下落。据说，伊西处置完毕之后，外科医生转过身来，问比勒：

“你哪儿不舒服？”

===Original===
Bealer happened to be dreaming of something else and didn’t answer.

“I say,” barked the doctor, “speak up. What’s wrong?”

Bealer was startled and jumped and begun to stutter.

“Oh, I see,” said the surgeon. “Shell shock.”

===Chinese===
比勒正巧想着别的心事，走了神，没有答话。

“喂，”医生喝道，“说话。你哪儿不对劲？”

比勒吓了一跳，蹦起来，说起话来结结巴巴。

“哦，我明白了，”外科医生说。“弹震症。”

===Original===
Bealer was bright enough and quick enough after that to play it up and was tagged for Blighty. He had it thrust upon him. And you can bet he grabbed it and thanked his lucky stars.

We had been on Mill Street a day and a night when an order came for our company to move up to the second line and to be ready to go over the top the next day. At first there was the usual grousing, as there seemed to be no reason why our company should be picked from the whole battalion. We soon learned that all hands were going over, and after that we felt better.

We got our equipment on and started up to the second line. It was right here that I got my first dose of real honest-to-goodness modern war. The big push had been on all summer, and the whole of the Somme district was battered and smashed.

Going up from Mill Street there were no communication trenches. We were right out in the open, exposed to rifle and machine-gun fire and to shrapnel, and the Boches were fairly raining it in on the territory they had been pushed back from and of which they had the range to an inch. We went up under that steady fire for a full hour. The casualties were heavy, and the galling part of it was that we couldn’t hurry, it was so dark. Every time a shell burst overhead and the shrapnel pattered in the dirt all about, I kissed myself goodbye and thought of the baked beans at home. Men kept falling, and I wished I hadn’t enlisted.

===Chinese===
打那以后，比勒够机灵也够利索，顺水推舟把这出戏演足了，结果挂上了回布莱蒂的签。这好事简直是硬塞到他怀里的。你只管相信，他一把搂住，直念自己福星高照。

我们在米尔街待了一天一夜，命令来了：我们连开上第二线，准备第二天翻出壕顶冲锋。起初照例是一通发牢骚（grousing），因为全营这么多连，凭什么单单挑中我们连，看不出一点道理。很快我们便得知，全营都要上，打那以后，心里就好受多了。

我们披挂整齐，向第二线进发。恰恰就在这儿，我头一回尝到了地地道道、如假包换的现代战争的滋味。大攻势（Big Push）打了整整一个夏天，整个索姆河地区早已被打得稀烂。

从米尔街往上，根本没有交通壕。我们完全暴露在露天里，步枪、机枪和榴霰弹都招呼得到，而德国佬对他们被撵出来的那片地界，简直是拿炮弹往下浇——每一寸的射程都背得烂熟。我们顶着这样持续的火力走了足足一个钟头。伤亡惨重，更气人的是，天黑得厉害，想快也快不成。每逢头顶上一声炮响、榴霰弹的弹丸噼里啪啦砸进四周的泥土，我就跟自己吻别一回，想起家里的烤豆子。弟兄们不断地倒下，我直后悔当初不该入伍。

===Original===
When we finally got up to the trench, believe me, we didn’t need any orders to get in. We relieved the Black Watch, and they encouraged us by telling us they had lost over half their men in that trench, and that Fritz kept a constant fire on it. They didn’t need to tell us. The big boys were coming over all the time.

The dead here were enough to give you the horrors. I had never seen so many before and never saw so many afterwards in one place. They were all over the place, both Germans and our own men. And in all states of mutilation and decomposition.

There were arms and legs sticking out of the trench sides. You could tell their nationality by the uniforms. The Scotch predominated. And their dead lay in the trenches and outside and hanging over the edges. I think it was here that I first got the real meaning of that old quotation about the curse of a dead man’s eye. With so many lying about, there were always eyes staring at you.

Sometimes a particularly wide-staring corpse would seem to follow you with his gaze, like one of these posters with the pointing finger that they use to advertise Liberty Bonds. We would cover them up or turn them over. Here and there one would have a scornful death smile on his lips, as though he were laughing at the folly of the whole thing.

===Chinese===
等我们终于上到壕前——信我一句——用不着谁下命令，我们自己就跳了进去。我们换防下来的是黑色守卫团（Black Watch），他们给我们鼓劲的方式，是告诉我们：他们在这个战壕里折损了过半的人马，而且弗里茨对这里的炮火就没断过。这用不着他们说。那些大家伙——重炮弹——一刻不停从头顶上飞过。

这一带的死人，多得叫人魂飞魄散。我从没见过这么多，后来也再没在一个地方见过这么多。遍地都是，德国人和我们自己人都有，断肢残躯、腐烂发黑，什么惨状都有。

战壕两壁上戳出一条条胳膊、一条条腿，看军装就能认出是哪一国人。苏格兰人居多。他们的死者横在壕里、躺在壕外、挂在壕沿上。我想，正是在这儿，我头一回真正懂得了那句老话——死人眼睛的诅咒——究竟是什么意思。周围躺着这么多人，总有眼睛在瞪着你。

有时候，一具瞪得格外圆的尸首，目光仿佛会跟着你转，就像宣传自由公债（Liberty Bonds）的那种伸着一根手指头的招贴画。我们就把他们盖上，或者给他们翻个身。东一具西一具，有的嘴角挂着一丝冷蔑的死笑，仿佛在笑话这整件事愚不可及。

===Original===
The stench here was appalling. That frightful, sickening smell that strikes one in the face like something tangible. Ugh! I immediately grew dizzy and faint and had a mad desire to run. I think if I hadn’t been a noncom with a certain small amount of responsibility to live up to, I should have gone crazy.

I managed to pull myself together and placed my men as comfortably as possible. The Germans were five hundred yards away, and there was but little danger of an attack, so comparatively few had to “stand to.” The rest took to the shelters.

I found a little two-man shelter that everybody else had avoided and crawled in. I crowded up against a man in there and spoke to him. He didn’t answer and then suddenly I became aware of a stench more powerful than ordinary. I put out my hand and thrust it into a slimy, cold mess. I had found a dead German with a gaping, putrefying wound in his abdomen. I crawled out of that shelter, gagging and retching. This time I simply couldn’t smother my impulse to run, and run I did, into the next traverse, where I sank weak and faint on the fire step. I sat there the rest of the night, regardless of shells, my mind milling wildly on the problem of war and the reason thereof and cursing myself for a fool.

===Chinese===
这里的恶臭骇人听闻。那股可怕得令人作呕的气味，劈头盖脸打过来，简直像一件摸得着的实物。呸！我顿时头晕目眩、脚下发虚，疯了一样只想逃跑。我想，若不是我好歹是个军士，肩上还有那么一小份必须撑住的责任，我准得疯了。

我好歹把自己拢住，把弟兄们尽量安顿得舒服些。德国人远在五百码外，遭到进攻的危险不大，所以用不着几个人“站班警戒”（stand to）。其余的都钻进了掩蔽处。

我找到一处人人都避着不进的双人小掩体，爬了进去。我朝里面的一个人挤过去，同他搭话。他不答腔，紧接着我突然觉出一股异乎寻常的恶臭。我伸出手去，插进了一摊又黏又冷的烂东西。我摸到了一个死了的德国人，肚子上烂开一个大口子。我从那掩体里爬出来，干呕不止。这一回，我实在压不住想跑的冲动，也真跑了——一气跑进下一个隔段（traverse），浑身发虚、两眼发花地瘫坐在射击踏垛上。那一夜剩下的时光，我就坐在那儿，炮弹也顾不上了，脑子里疯狂地打着转，琢磨战争这桩难题和它究竟为的是什么，一面骂自己是个傻瓜。

===Original===
It was very early in the morning when Wells shook me up with, “Hi sye, Darby, wot the blinkin’ blazes is that noise?”

We listened, and away from the rear came a tremendous whirring, burring, rumbling buzz, like a swarm of giant bees. I thought of everything from a zeppelin to a donkey engine but couldn’t make it out. Blofeld ran around the corner of a traverse and told us to get the men out. He didn’t know what was coming and wasn’t taking any chances.

It was getting a little light though heavily misty. We waited, and then out of the gray blanket of fog waddled the great steel monsters that we were to know afterwards as the “tanks.” I shall never forget it.

In the half darkness they looked twice as big as they really were. They lurched forward, slow, clumsy but irresistible, nosing down into shell holes and out, crushing the unburied dead, sliding over mere trenches as though they did not exist.

===Chinese===
天蒙蒙亮的时候，威尔斯（Wells）把我摇醒了：“嗨我说，达比（Darby），那动静是啥见鬼的名堂？”

我们侧耳细听，后方远处传来老大一片嗡嗡隆隆、咕噜咕噜的声响，像一大群巨蜂。我把能想到的都想了个遍，从齐柏林飞艇到驴机（donkey engine，一种小蒸汽吊机），就是猜不出个名堂。布洛菲尔德（Blofeld）从隔段拐角跑出来，叫我们把弟兄们都弄出壕。他也不知道来的是什么，可他绝不冒这个险。

天色微亮，雾气却很重。我们等着，随后，从那床灰蒙蒙的雾毯底下，摇摇摆摆地拱出了巨大的钢铁怪物——后来我们才知道它们叫“坦克”。那一幕，我一辈子也忘不了。

在半明半暗里，它们看上去比真实尺寸还大一倍。它们磕磕绊绊地往前拱，又慢又笨，却势不可挡，一头扎进弹坑、又从坑里拱出来，把没掩埋的死者碾作两截，从普普通通的战壕上一滑而过，仿佛那些战壕压根不存在。

===Original===
There were five in all. One passed directly over us. We scuttled out of the way, and the men let go a cheer. For we knew that here was something that could and would win battles.

The tanks were an absolutely new thing to us. Their secret had been guarded so carefully even in our own army that our battalion had heard nothing of them.

But we didn’t need to be told that they would be effective. One look was enough to convince us. Later it convinced Fritzie.

===Chinese===
一共五辆。有一辆正好从我们头顶上开了过去。我们嗖地四散躲开，弟兄们放声欢呼。因为我们知道，眼前这东西能打胜仗，而且必定打胜仗。

坦克对我们来说是百分之百的新事物。它们的秘密保得那么严，连我们自己军队里都捂得密不透风，我们营事前竟连一点风声都没听到。

不过，这玩意儿管用不管用，用不着谁来告诉我们。看上一眼就够我们信服的了。后来，它把弗里茨也说服了。

## X Following the Tanks Into Battle / 十、跟着坦克上战场

===Original===
The tanks passed beyond us and halfway up to the first line and stopped. Trapdoors in the decks opened, and the crews poured out and began to pile sandbags in front of the machines so that when day broke fully and the mists lifted, the enemy could not see what had been brought up in the night.

Day dawned, and a frisky little breeze from the west scattered the fog and swept the sky clean. There wasn’t a cloud by eight o’clock. The sun shone bright, and we cursed it, for if it had been rainy the attack would not have been made.

We made the usual last preparations that morning, such as writing letters and delivering farewell messages; and the latest rooks made their wills in the little blanks provided for the purpose in the back of the pay books. We judged from the number of dead and the evident punishment other divisions had taken there that the chances of coming back would be slim. Around nine o’clock Captain Green gave us a little talk that confirmed our suspicions that the day was to be a hard one.

===Chinese===
坦克越过我们，朝第一线战壕开到半途便停了下来。顶甲上的舱门打开，乘员们涌出来，动手在车身前头堆起沙袋——这样一来，等天色大亮、雾气散尽，敌人就看不出夜里往前沿运上来的是什么家伙了。

天亮了，一股活泼的小西风把雾气扯散，把天空扫得干干净净。到八点钟，天上连一丝云也没有。太阳明晃晃地照着，我们直骂它——要是赶上下雨，这一仗压根就打不起来。

那天早上，我们照例做了最后一番准备：写家信，托付诀别的话；最新补进来的新兵蛋子（rooks，对新兵的谑称），则在薪饷手册背面专设的小空栏里写下了遗嘱。从尸首的数目和别的师在此地显然吃过的苦头判断，我们估摸着，活着回去的指望相当渺茫。九点钟前后，格林上尉（Captain Green）给我们讲了短短一席话，坐实了我们心头的疑团：这一天，怕是场硬仗。

===Original===
He said, as nearly as I can remember:

“Lads, I want to tell you that there is to be a most important battle﻿—one of the most important in the whole war. High Wood out there commands a view of the whole of this part of the Somme and is most valuable. There are estimated to be about ten thousand Germans in that wood and in the surrounding supports. The positions are mostly of concrete with hundreds of machine guns and field artillery. Our heavies have for some reason made no impression on them, and regiment after regiment has attempted to take the woods and failed with heavy losses. Now it is up to the 47th Division to do the seemingly impossible. Zero is at eleven. We go over then. The best of luck and God bless you.”

We were all feeling pretty sour on the world when the sky pilot came along and cheered us up.

===Chinese===
凭记忆，他大体是这么说的：

“小伙子们，我要告诉你们，今天要打一场极其重要的战役——整个大战里数得着的重要一仗。外头的海伍德（High Wood）居高临下，俯瞰索姆河（Somme）这一带的整个地面，价值极大。据估计，那片树林和周围支援阵地里的德国兵约有上万人，工事大半是混凝土浇筑的，配着几百挺机枪和野战炮。不知为什么，我们的重炮在它们面前毫无建树，一团又一团的部队试图夺下这片树林，都在惨重损失下败下阵来。如今，轮到第47师来干这桩看似不可能的事了。零时定在十一点。到时候我们就翻出壕顶冲锋。祝大伙儿走运，愿上帝保佑你们。”

我们个个正对这世界一肚子怨气，“天国领航员”（sky pilot，大兵对随军牧师的谑称）来了，给我们打气鼓劲。

===Original===
He was a good little man, that chaplain, brave as they make ’em. He always went over the top with us and was in the thick of the fighting, and he had the military cross for bravery. He passed down the line, giving us a slap on the back or a hand grip and started us singing. No gospel hymns either, but any old rollicking, good-natured song that he happened to think of that would loosen things up and relieve the tension.

Somehow he made you feel that you wouldn’t mind going to hell if he was along, and you knew that he’d be willing to come if he could do any good. A good little man! Peace to his ashes.

At ten o’clock things busted loose, and the most intense bombardment ever known in warfare up to that time began. Thousands of guns, both French and English, in fact every available gun within a radius of fifteen miles, poured it in. In the Bedlamitish din and roar it was impossible to hear the next man unless he put his mouth up close to your ear and yelled.

===Chinese===
那位随军牧师是个好样的小个子，天不怕地不怕。他回回跟我们一道翻出壕顶冲锋，哪里打得最凶哪里就有他，还得过军功十字勋章。他顺着队伍一路走过来，不是在这个背上拍一巴掌，就是握握那个的手，还领着我们唱起歌来。唱的也不是什么福音圣歌，全是随口想起的欢快和气的老段子，就为把气氛弄活络，把那股紧绷劲儿松一松。

不知怎么的，他能让你觉得，只要有他同行，下地狱也甘心；你也知道，只要能帮上一点忙，他保准乐意同去。一个多好的小个子！愿他安息。

十点钟，一切轰然炸开了锅，到那时为止战争史上最猛烈的一场炮击开始了。几千门火炮——法国的、英国的，其实是方圆十五英里内所有拉得出来的炮——一齐猛灌。在那疯人院似的喧嚣轰鸣里，身边的人说什么根本听不见，除非他把嘴贴到你耳朵上吼。

===Original===
My ear drums ached, and I thought I should go insane if the racket didn’t stop. I was frightfully nervous and scared, but tried not to show it. An officer or a noncom must conceal his nervousness, though he be dying with fright.

The faces of the men were hard-set and pale. Some of them looked positively green. They smoked fag after fag, lighting the new ones on the butts.

All through the bombardment Fritz was comparatively quiet. He was saving all his for the time when we should come over. Probably, too, he was holed up to a large extent in his concrete dugouts. I looked over the top once or twice and wondered if I, too, would be lying there unburied with the rats and maggots gnawing me into an unrecognizable mass. There were moments in that hour from ten to eleven when I was distinctly sorry for myself.

The time, strangely enough, went fast﻿—as it probably does with a condemned man in his last hour. At zero minus ten the word went down the line “Ten to go” and we got to the better positions of the trench and secured our footing on the side of the parapet to make our climb over when the signal came. Some of the men gave their bayonets a last fond rub, and I looked to my bolt action to see that it worked well. I had ten rounds in the magazine, and I didn’t intend to rely too much on the bayonet. At a few seconds of eleven I looked at my wrist watch and was afflicted again with that empty feeling in the solar plexus. Then the whistles shrilled; I blew mine, and over we went.

===Chinese===
我的耳膜疼得发慌，心想这动静再不停，我就要疯了。我紧张害怕得要命，却尽力不露形迹。当军官、当军士的，哪怕吓得半死，也得把慌张捂得严严实实。

弟兄们的脸绷得紧紧的，煞白，有几个简直泛出绿色。“香烟”（fags）一支接一支地抽，新的一支都用烟屁股点着。

炮击从头到尾，弗里茨（Fritz，指德国兵）都相当安静。他把自己的家伙什全攒着，单等我们冲过去时一股脑儿招呼上来；多半他还大半猫在混凝土掩蔽部里。我朝壕顶外张望了一两回，心里嘀咕：我是不是也会横在那儿无人收殓，任老鼠和蛆虫把我啃成一团认不出模样的东西。十点到十一点那一小时里，有那么几阵子，我确确实实地顾影自怜。

说来奇怪，时间过得飞快——多半死囚的最后一小时也是如此。零前十分钟，口令顺着战线传下来：“还剩十分钟。”我们占到战壕里位置较好的地段，在胸墙一侧踩稳脚跟，单等信号一出便翻墙而上。有几个弟兄把刺刀最后爱怜地擦了一遍，我则查看枪机，确认它动作顺滑。弹仓里压着十发子弹，我不打算太指望刺刀。差几秒十一点时，我看了看腕表，胃底那股空落落的感觉又袭了上来。随即哨音尖啸而起；我吹响自己的哨子，我们翻出壕顶，冲了上去。

===Original===
To a disinterested spectator who was far enough up in the air to be out of range it must have been a wonderful spectacle to see those thousands of men go over, wave after wave.

The terrain was level out to the point where the little hill of High Wood rose covered with the splintered poles of what had once been a forest. This position and the supports to the left and rear of it began to fairly belch machine-gun and shell fire. If Fritz had been quiet before, he gave us all he had now.

Our battalion went over from the second trench, and we got the cream of it.

The tanks were just ahead of us and lumbered along in an imposing row. They lurched down into deep craters and out again, tipped and reeled and listed, and sometimes seemed as though they must upset; but they came up each time and went on and on. And how slow they did seem to move! Lord, I thought we should never cover that five or six hundred yards.

===Chinese===
要是哪个不相干的看客飞得够高、出了炮火射程，眼看这几千人一波接一波地翻出壕顶，想必会觉得是一场叹为观止的奇观。

地势一路平坦，直到海伍德那座小山包拔地而起——山上盖满劈裂的木桩，那里原是一片树林。这座阵地连同它左翼与后方的各处支援阵地，开始简直像喷发一样往外吐机枪火力和炮火。如果说弗里茨先前还算安静，那此刻他是把压箱底的家当全招呼上来了。

我们营从第二线战壕翻出壕顶，炮火里最浓的那份“精华”全让我们赶上了。

坦克就在我们前头，摆出一排威风凛凛的阵势轰隆碾压。它们一头栽进深深的弹坑，又爬出来，歪歪斜斜、摇摇晃晃，有时眼看非翻不可；可每一回它们都稳住了，继续向前，一个劲儿向前。可它们挪得那个慢哟！老天，我以为那五六百码一辈子也走不完了。

===Original===
The tank machine guns were spitting fire over the heads of our first wave, and their Hotchkiss guns were rattling. A beautiful creeping barrage preceded us. Row after row of shells burst at just the right distance ahead, spewing gobs of smoke and flashes of flame, made thin by the bright sunlight. Half a dozen airplanes circled like dragonflies up there in the blue.

There was a tank just ahead of me. I got behind it. And marched there. Slow! God, how slow! Anyhow, it kept off the machine-gun bullets, but not the shrapnel. It was breaking over us in clouds. I felt the stunning patter of the fragments on my tin hat, cringed under it, and wondered vaguely why it didn’t do me in.

Men in the front wave were going down like tenpins. Off there diagonally to the right and forward I glimpsed a blinding burst, and as much as a whole platoon went down.

Around me men were dropping all the time﻿—men I knew. I saw Dolbsie clawing at his throat as he reeled forward, falling. I saw Vickers double up, drop his rifle, and somersault, hanging on to his abdomen.

===Chinese===
坦克上的机枪越过我们第一波的头顶喷吐火舌，哈奇开斯（Hotchkiss）机枪嗒嗒作响。一道漂亮的徐进弹幕在我们前头开路，一排接一排的炮弹在正前方恰到好处的距离上炸开，吐出一团团烟云、一道道火光，在明晃晃的日光里显得稀薄。半打飞机像蜻蜓一般，在蓝天上悠悠打着旋。

正好有一辆坦克在我前头。我跟在它屁股后面走。慢！天哪，真慢！不管怎么说，机枪子弹叫它挡住了，榴霰弹却挡不住。榴霰弹成团成团地在我们头顶炸开，弹片噼噼啪啪打在钢盔上，震得人发懵；我在盔底下缩着脖子，迷迷糊糊直纳闷：它怎么还没把我要了？

前一波的士兵像保龄球瓶一样接连倒下。右前方斜刺里，我瞥见一道晃眼的炸光，足足一整个排的人都倒了下去。

我身边随时有人倒下——都是我认识的人。我看见多布西（Dolbsie）踉跄着朝前栽倒，双手抠着自己的喉咙；看见维克斯（Vickers）弯下腰去，撂下步枪，整个人翻滚出去，双手死死捂着肚子。

===Original===
A hundred yards away, to the right, an officer walked backwards with an automatic pistol balanced on his finger, smiling, pulling his men along like a drum major. A shell or something hit him. He disappeared in a welter of blood and half a dozen of the front file fell with him.

I thought we must be nearly there and sneaked a look around the edge of the tank. A traversing machine gun raked the mud, throwing up handfuls, and I heard the gruff *row, row* of flattened bullets as they ricocheted off the steel armor. I ducked back, and on we went.

Slow! Slow! I found myself planning what I would do when I got to the front trenches﻿—if we ever did. There would be a grand rumpus, and I would click a dozen or more.

And then we arrived.

===Chinese===
右边一百码开外，一名军官倒退着走，手指上稳稳挑着一支自动手枪，满面笑容，像鼓乐队队长领着队伍似的把部下往前带。一发炮弹还是别的什么命中了他。他消失在一片血泊里，前排又有五六个人跟他一同栽倒。

我寻思咱们该快到了，便从坦克边缘偷偷张了一眼。一挺来回扫射的机枪刮着地面，掀起一把把泥土，我还听见打扁的子弹从钢甲上弹开时那低沉的*哐、哐*声。我把头缩回来，继续前进。

慢！真慢！我发现自己已经在盘算进前沿战壕以后要怎么干了——如果我们真能到得了的话。到时候准有一场天翻地覆的大乱战，我怎么说也能“点”掉一打还多。

然后，我们到了。

===Original===
I don’t suppose that trip across No Man’s Land behind the tanks took over five minutes, but it seemed like an hour.

At the end of it my participation in the battle of High Wood ended. No, I wasn’t wounded. But when we reached the Boche front trenches a strange thing happened. There was no fight worth mentioning. The tanks stopped over the trenches and blazed away right and left with their all-around traverse.

A few Boches ran out and threw silly little bombs at the monsters. The tanks, noses in air, moved slowly on. And then the Graybacks swarmed up out of shelters and dugouts, literally in hundreds, and held up their hands, whining “Mercy, *kamarad*.”

We took prisoners by platoons. Blofeld grabbed me and turned over a gang of thirty to me. We searched them rapidly, cut their suspenders and belts, and I started to the rear with them. They seemed glad to go. So was I.

===Chinese===
我估摸着，跟着坦克穿越无人地带（No Man’s Land）这一趟统共花不了五分钟，可当时竟像熬了一个钟头。

这一段路走完，我在海伍德之战里的戏份便到此为止了。不，我没负伤。只是我们抵达德国佬（Boche）前沿战壕时，怪事出现了：压根没有一场像样的战斗。坦克横骑在战壕上方，凭着周向旋转的火力朝左右两边猛轰。

有几个德国兵跑出来，朝这些钢铁怪物扔了几颗不顶用的小炸弹。坦克昂着鼻子，缓缓前行。接着，灰军装德国兵便从隐蔽处和掩蔽部里蜂拥而出——实打实的数以百计——高举双手，嘴里哀告：“饶命，*卡玛拉德*（kamarad，德语‘同志’，德兵投降时的喊话）。”

我们整排整排地收俘虏。布洛菲尔德（Blofeld）一把拽住我，把三十个俘虏交给我。我们火速搜了他们的身，割断背带和腰带，我便押着他们动身后撤。他们瞧着挺乐意走。我也乐意。

===Original===
As we hurried back over the five hundred yards that had been No Man’s Land and was now British ground, I looked back and saw the irresistible tanks smashing their way through the tree stumps of High Wood, still spitting death and destruction in three directions.

Going back we were under almost as heavy fire as we had been coming up. When we were about halfway across, shrapnel burst directly over our party and seven of the prisoners were killed and half a dozen wounded. I myself was unscratched. I stuck my hand inside my tunic and patted Dinky on the back, sent up a prayer for some more luck like that, and carried on.

After getting my prisoners back to the rear, I came up again but couldn’t find my battalion. I threw in with a battalion of Australians and was with them for twenty-four hours.

When I found my chaps again, the battle of High Wood was pretty well over. Our company for some reason had suffered very few casualties, less than twenty-nine. Company B, however, had been practically wiped out, losing all but thirteen men out of two hundred. The other two companies had less than one hundred casualties. We had lost about a third of our strength. It is a living wonder to me that any of us came through.

===Chinese===
我们匆匆折返，穿过那五百码地皮——前不久还是无人地带，如今已归英军。我回头望去，只见势不可当的坦克正在海伍德的树桩丛中碾开一条路，依旧朝三个方向喷吐着死亡与毁灭。

回程路上，我们头顶的炮火几乎同来时一样猛。走到半途，一发榴霰弹正在我们这一队人头顶炸开，七个俘虏丢了命，还有六个挂了彩。我自己倒毫发无伤。我把手伸进军上衣，拍了拍丁奇（Dinky）的背，暗暗祷告老天爷再多赏几回这样的运气，然后接着赶路。

把俘虏押到后方之后，我又折上前线，却怎么也找不着我们营。我便跟一个澳大利亚营搭了伙，随他们待了二十四小时。

等我再找到自己那帮弟兄，海伍德之战差不多已经收尾。不知什么缘故，我们连伤亡极少，不到二十九人。可B连几乎全军覆没，两百人里只活下十三个。另外两个连的伤亡都不到一百。我们损失了大约三分之一的兵力。我们这帮人里居然有人活着回来，在我看来简直是活生生的奇迹。

===Original===
I don’t believe any of us would have if it hadn’t been for the tanks.

The net result of the battle of High Wood was that our troops carried on for nearly two miles beyond the position to be taken. They had to fall back but held the wood and the heights. Three of the tanks were stalled in the farther edge of the woods﻿—out of fuel﻿—and remained there for three days unharmed under the fire of the German guns.

Eventually someone ventured out and got some juice into them, and they returned to our lines. The tanks had proved themselves, not only as effective fighting machines, but as destroyers of German morale.

===Chinese===
我敢说，若不是有坦克，我们当中一个也活不下来。

海伍德之战的最终战果是：我们的部队一鼓作气，越过预定夺取的阵地又推进了近两英里，虽然随后不得不后撤，却守住了树林和高地。有三辆坦克趴窝在林子远端的边上——燃料耗尽——在德军炮火底下停了三天，毫发未损。

最后总算有人壮着胆子摸出去，给它们灌足了油（juice，兵俚指燃料），它们便开回了我方防线。坦克就此证明了自己——不只是得力的战斗机器，更是德国人士气的克星。

## Foreword / 前言

===Original===
I have tried as an American in writing this book to give the public a complete view of the trenches and life on the Western Front as it appeared to me, and also my impression of conditions and men as I found them. It has been a pleasure to write it, and now that I have finished I am genuinely sorry that I cannot go further. On the lecture tour I find that people ask me questions, and I have tried in this book to give in detail many things about the quieter side of war that to an audience would seem too tame. I feel that the public want to know how the soldiers live when not in the trenches, for all the time out there is not spent in killing and carnage. As in the case of all men in the trenches, I heard things and stories that especially impressed me, so I have written them as hearsay, not taking to myself credit as their originator. I trust that the reader will find as much joy in the cockney character as I did and which I have tried to show the public; let me say now that no finer body of men than those Bermondsey boys of my battalion could be found.

I think it fair to say that in compiling the trench terms at the end of this book I have not copied any war book, but I have given in each case my own version of the words, though I will confess that the idea and necessity of having such a list sprang from reading Sergeant Empey’s *Over the Top*. It would be impossible to write a book that the people would understand without the aid of such a glossary.

It is my sincere wish that after reading this book the reader may have a clearer conception of what this great world war means and what our soldiers are contending with, and that it may awaken the American people to the danger of Prussianism so that when in the future there is a call for funds for Liberty Loans, Red Cross work, or Y.M.C.A., there will be no slacking, for they form the real triangular sign to a successful termination of this terrible conflict.

R. Derby Holmes.

===Chinese===
作为一个美国人，我在这本书里力图向公众完整呈现我亲眼所见的战壕与西线生活，以及我对那边的境况与人物的印象。写这本书是件乐事；如今写完了，我真心遗憾不能再往下写。巡回演讲的路上，我发现人们总爱向我提问，于是在这本书里，我尽量详细写进了战争的“平静一面”中许多事情——这些东西讲给台下的听众听，未免嫌太平淡。我觉得公众是想了解士兵们不在战壕里的时候怎样生活，因为在那边，时间并不全花在杀戮和伤亡上。同所有蹲过战壕的人一样，我也听到过一些格外打动我的事情和故事，所以我把它们当作听闻的掌故写了下来，不敢掠他人之美，冒认是它们的编创者。我相信读者会像我一样，从伦敦佬（cockney）的性格中获得乐趣——我也已尽力把他们展现给公众；现在就让我说一句：天底下再找不出比我营里那些伯蒙德赛（Bermondsey）小伙子更出色的一群人了。

我想应当公平地说明：汇编本书末尾的战壕术语时，我没有抄任何一本战争书籍，每一条给的都是我自己的讲法；不过我承认，编这么一份词表的念头与必要，是读了埃姆佩中士（Sergeant Empey）的《越过壕顶》（Over the Top）才有的。没有这样一份术语表帮忙，就不可能写出一本大家看得懂的书。

我诚挚地希望，读者读完这本书之后，能对这场世界大战意味着什么、我们的士兵正在同什么较量，有一个更清楚的认识；也希望它能唤醒美国人民，看清普鲁士主义（Prussianism）的危险，好叫将来再有为自由公债（Liberty Loans）、红十字（Red Cross）事业或基督教青年会（Y.M.C.A.）募款的时候，没有人再拖沓怠慢——因为这三样正是通往这场可怕冲突圆满终结的真正三角路标。

R. 德比·霍尔姆斯。

## Glossary of Army Slang / 军队俚语词汇表

===Original===
All around traverse - A machine gun placed on a swivel to turn in any direction.

===Chinese===
全向旋转机枪：架在旋转枪架上的机枪，可朝任何方向转动射击。

===Original===
Ammo - Ammunition. Usually for rifles, though occasionally used to indicate that for artillery.

===Chinese===
弹药：即 ammunition。通常指步枪弹药，偶尔也用来指炮弹。

===Original===
Argue the toss - Argue the point.

===Chinese===
抬杠：为一点事争辩不休。

===Original===
Back of the line - Anywhere to the rear and out of the danger zone.

===Chinese===
火线之后：战线后方的随便什么地方，只要出了危险地带就算。

===Original===
Barbed wire - Ordinary barbed wire used for entanglements. A thicker and heavier military wire is sometimes used.

===Chinese===
铁丝网：架设铁丝障碍用的普通带刺铁丝，有时也用更粗更重的军用铁丝。

===Original===
Barrage - Shells dropped simultaneously and in a row so as to form a curtain of fire. Literal translation “a barrier.”

===Chinese===
弹幕：炮弹同时落下、一排排铺开，织成一道火幕。直译即“屏障”。

===Original===
Bashed - Smashed.

===Chinese===
砸烂：打得稀烂。

===Original===
Big boys - Big guns or the shells they send over.

===Chinese===
大家伙：重炮，或是重炮打过来的炮弹。

===Original===
Big push - The battles of the Somme.

===Chinese===
大攻势：指索姆河战役。

===Original===
Billets - The quarters of the soldier when back of the line. Any place from a pigpen to a palace.

===Chinese===
宿营地：士兵撤到战线后方时的住处——从猪圈到宫殿，什么档次的都算。

===Original===
Bleeder or Blighter - Cockney slang for fellow. Roughly corresponding to American “guy.”

===Chinese===
家伙：伦敦佬俚语，指“人”，大致相当于美国话里的“家伙”（guy）。

===Original===
Blighty - England. East Indian derivation. The paradise looked forward to by all good soldiers﻿—and all bad ones too.

===Chinese===
布莱蒂：英国本土。源自东印度语。是所有好兵都一心盼着回去的天堂——坏兵也一样。

===Original===
Blighty one - A wound that will take the soldier to Blighty.

===Chinese===
布莱蒂伤：足以让士兵回布莱蒂（英国本土）养伤的伤。

===Original===
Bloody - The universal Cockney adjective. It is vaguely supposed to be highly obscene, though just why nobody seems to know.

===Chinese===
“他妈的”：伦敦佬人手一个的万能形容词。人们稀里糊涂地觉得这词下流透顶，至于为什么下流，似乎谁也说不上来。

===Original===
Blooming - A meaningless and greatly used adjective. Applied to anything and everything.

===Chinese===
“可恶的”：一个没什么实义、用得极凶的形容词，什么东西都能往上安。

===Original===
Bomb - A hand grenade.

===Chinese===
手雷：手榴弹。

===Original===
Bully beef - Corned beef, high grade and good of the kind, if you like the kind. It sets hard on the chest.

===Chinese===
咸牛肉罐头：腌碎牛肉。就这一类东西而论算得上等好货——前提是你好这一口。吃下去沉甸甸地坠在胸口。

===Original===
Carry on - To go ahead with the matter in hand.

===Chinese===
接着干：把手头的事继续做下去。

===Original===
Char - Tea. East Indian derivation.

===Chinese===
茶：茶水。源自东印度语。

===Original===
Chat - Officers’ term for cootie; supposed to be more delicate.

===Chinese===
chat：军官们对虱子（cootie）的叫法，据说更文雅些。

===Original===
Click - Variously used. To die. To be killed. To kill. To draw some disagreeable job, as: I clicked a burial fatigue.

===Chinese===
摊上：用法多样。死；阵亡；打死；摊上某件倒霉差事——例如：“我摊上了一桩掩埋尸首的杂役。”

===Original===
Communication trench - A trench leading up to the front trench.

===Chinese===
交通壕：通向前沿战壕的壕沟。

===Original===
Consolidate - To turn around and prepare for occupation a captured trench.

===Chinese===
巩固：把夺下的敌方战壕掉转方向、整备妥当，改由我方据守。

===Original===
Cootie - The common﻿—the too common﻿—body louse. Everybody has ’em.

===Chinese===
虱子：那种常见的——常见得过了头的——体虱。人人身上都有。

===Original===
Crater - A round pit made by an underground explosion or by a shell.

===Chinese===
爆坑：地下爆破或炮弹炸出的圆形大坑。

===Original===
Cushy - Easy. Soft.

===Chinese===
舒坦：轻松。安逸。

===Original===
Dixie - An oblong iron pot or box fitting into a field kitchen. Used for cooking anything and everything. Nobody seems to know why it is so called.

===Chinese===
迪希锅：一种长方形铁锅（铁匣），嵌进野战厨房里使用，煮什么都是它。为什么叫这么个名字，似乎没人说得清。

===Original===
Doggo - Still. Quiet. East Indian derivation.

===Chinese===
潜伏不动：一动不动，悄无声息。源自东印度语。

===Original===
Doing in - Killing.

===Chinese===
干掉：杀人；要人命。

===Original===
Doss - Sleep.

===Chinese===
睡觉：睡，睡一觉。

===Original===
Duck walk - A slatted wooden walk in soft ground.

===Chinese===
木板路：软泥地上用板条铺成的小道。

===Original===
Dud - An unexploded shell. A dangerous thing to fool with.

===Chinese===
哑弹：没有爆炸的炮弹。拿它摆弄着玩可是件危险事。

===Original===
Dug-out - A hole more or less deep in the side of a trench where soldiers are supposed to rest.

===Chinese===
掩蔽部：壕壁上挖出的或深或浅的洞，士兵们理应在里头歇息。

===Original===
Dump - A place where supplies are left for distribution.

===Chinese===
物资分放站：囤放物资、供各处前来领取分发的地方。

===Original===
Entrenching tool - A sort of small shovel for quick digging. Carried as part of equipment.

===Chinese===
工兵铲：一种便于快速掘土的小铲子，作为随身装备背着。

===Original===
Estaminet - A French saloon or café.

===Chinese===
小酒馆：法国的酒馆或咖啡馆。

===Original===
Fag - A cigarette.

===Chinese===
香烟：一支烟。

===Original===
Fatigue - Any kind of work except manning the trenches.

===Chinese===
杂役：除了守战壕之外的一切活计。

===Original===
Fed up - Tommy’s way of saying “too much is enough.”

===Chinese===
受够了：汤米式的说法，意思是“再多就受不了啦”。

===Original===
Firing step - A narrow ledge running along the parapet on which a soldier stands to look over the top.

===Chinese===
射击踏垛：沿胸墙砌出的一条窄台，士兵站上去，好朝壕顶外张望。

===Original===
Flare - A star light sent up from a pistol to light up out in front.

===Chinese===
照明弹：用手枪打上天空、照亮壕外正前方的星光弹。

===Original===
Fritz - An affectionate term for our friend the enemy.

===Chinese===
弗里茨：对我们那位朋友——敌人——的亲热叫法。

===Original===
Funk hole - A dugout.

===Chinese===
藏身洞：即掩蔽部。

===Original===
Gas - Any poisonous gas sent across when the wind is right. Used by both sides. Invented by the Germans.

===Chinese===
毒气：风向对头时施放过来的各种毒气。两边都用，德国人发明的。

===Original===
Goggles - A piece of equipment similar to that used by motorists, supposed to keep off tear gas. The rims are backed with strips of sponge which Tommy tears off and throws the goggle frame away.

===Chinese===
风镜：一件类似汽车司机护目镜的装备，据说能挡催泪毒气。镜框背面衬着一圈海绵条，汤米把海绵条一撕，把镜框扔了完事。

===Original===
Go west - To die.

===Chinese===
上西天：死。

===Original===
Grouse - Complain. Growl. Kick.

===Chinese===
发牢骚：抱怨，咕哝，提意见。

===Original===
Hun - A German.

===Chinese===
德国佬：一个德国兵。

===Original===
Identification disc - A fiber tablet bearing the soldier’s name, regiment, and rank. Worn around the neck on a string.

===Chinese===
身份牌：一块纤维小牌，写着士兵的姓名、团号和军衔，用绳子挂在脖子上。

===Original===
Iron rations - About two pounds of nonperishable rations to be used in an emergency.

===Chinese===
“铁份口粮”：约两磅不易变质的应急口粮。

===Original===
Knuckle knife - A short dagger with a studded hilt. Invented by the Germans.

===Chinese===
指节匕首：一种柄上带指节护箍的短匕首。德国人发明的。

===Original===
Lance Corporal - The lowest grade of noncommissioned officer.

===Chinese===
代理下士：军士中最低的一级。

===Original===
Lewis gun - A very light machine gun invented by one Lewis, an officer in the American army.

===Chinese===
刘易斯机枪：一种非常轻的机枪，发明者是美军一位姓刘易斯的军官。

===Original===
Light railway - A very narrow-gauge railway on which are pushed little hand cars.

===Chinese===
轻便铁路：一种轨距极窄的小铁路，道上一辆辆小推车靠人推着走。

===Original===
Listening post - One or more men go out in front, at night, of course, and listen for movements by the enemy.

===Chinese===
听音哨：一人或数人摸到壕外——当然是在夜里——监听敌人的动静。

===Original===
Maconochie - A scientifically compounded and well-balanced ration, so the authorities say. It looks, smells, and tastes like rancid lard.

===Chinese===
马科诺基：一种科学配制、营养均衡的口粮——官方是这么说的。可它看着、闻着、尝着都像哈喇了的猪油。

===Original===
M.O. - Medical Officer. A foxy cove who can’t be fooled with faked symptoms.

===Chinese===
军医官：即 Medical Officer。一个精明的家伙，装病糊弄不了他。

===Original===
Mess tin - A combination teapot, fry pan, and plate.

===Chinese===
饭盒：茶壶、煎锅加餐盘，三合一。

===Original===
Military cross - An officer’s decoration for bravery.

===Chinese===
军功十字勋章：授予军官的英勇勋章。

===Original===
Military medal - A decoration for bravery given to enlisted men.

===Chinese===
军功奖章：授予士兵的英勇勋章。

===Original===
Mills - The most commonly used hand grenade.

===Chinese===
米尔斯手雷：最常用的手榴弹。

===Original===
Minnies - German trench mortar projectiles.

===Chinese===
“明妮”：德军堑壕迫击炮打出的炮弹。

===Original===
Napper - The head.

===Chinese===
脑袋：头。

===Original===
Night 'ops - A much hated practice manoeuvre done at night.

===Chinese===
夜间演习：人人恨得牙痒的夜间演练。

===Original===
No Man's Land - The area between the trenches.

===Chinese===
无人地带：两军战壕之间的那片地。

===Original===
On your own - At liberty. Your time is your own.

===Chinese===
自由支配：得了空，时间归你自己。

===Original===
Out or over there - Somewhere in France.

===Chinese===
那边：法国的某个地方。

===Original===
Parados - The back wall of a trench.

===Chinese===
背墙：战壕靠己方一侧的壕墙。

===Original===
Parapet - The front wall of a trench.

===Chinese===
胸墙：战壕朝敌一侧的壕墙。

===Original===
Patrol - One or more men who go out in front and prowl in the dark, seeking information of the enemy.

===Chinese===
巡逻队：一人或数人摸到壕外，在黑暗中潜行，搜罗敌情。

===Original===
Periscope - A boxlike arrangement with two mirrors for looking over the top without exposing the napper.

===Chinese===
潜望镜：一个匣子样的装置，装着两面镜子，不用探出脑袋就能朝壕顶外张望。

===Original===
Persuader - A short club with a nail-studded head.

===Chinese===
说服棍：一根短棒，顶头钉满铁钉。

===Original===
Pip squeak - A German shell which makes that kind of noise when it comes over.

===Chinese===
吱哇弹：飞过来时吱哇乱叫的德国炮弹。

===Original===
Push up the daisies - To be killed and buried.

===Chinese===
进土里顶雏菊：阵亡，入土。

===Original===
Ration party - A party of men which goes to the rear and brings up rations for the front line.

===Chinese===
领粮差事：一队人到后方去，把口粮运回前线。

===Original===
Rest - Relief from trench service. Mostly one works constantly when “resting.”

===Chinese===
休整：撤出战壕轮休。不过“休整”的时候，多半也照样忙个不停。

===Original===
Ruddy - Same as bloody, but not quite so bad.

===Chinese===
“该死的”：跟“他妈的”一个用法，只是火气没那么冲。

===Original===
Sandbag - A bag which is filled with mud and used for building the parapet.

===Chinese===
沙袋：装满泥的袋子，用来垒胸墙。

===Original===
Sentry go - Time on guard in the front trench, or at rest at headquarters.

===Chinese===
站哨：在前沿战壕里警戒值班，或在总部驻地当值。

===Original===
Shell hole - A pit made by the explosion of a shell.

===Chinese===
弹坑：炮弹爆炸炸出的坑。

===Original===
Souvenir - Any kind of junk picked up for keepsakes. Also used as a begging word by the French children.

===Chinese===
纪念品：捡来留念的任何破烂玩意儿。法国小孩也拿这个词讨要东西。

===Original===
Stand to - Order for all men to stand ready in the trench in event of a surprise attack, usually at sundown and sunrise.

===Chinese===
站班警戒：命令全体士兵在壕内就位戒备、以防敌军偷袭，通常在日落和日出时分下达。

===Original===
Stand down - Countermanding “stand to.”

===Chinese===
解除警戒：撤销“站班警戒”。

===Original===
Stokes - A bomb weighing about eleven pounds usually thrown from a mortar, but sometimes used by hand.

===Chinese===
斯托克斯迫击炮弹：一种约十一磅重的炸弹，通常用迫击炮发射，偶尔也用手扔。

===Original===
Strafing - One of the few words Tommy has borrowed from Fritz. To punish.

===Chinese===
惩治：汤米从弗里茨那儿借来的为数不多的词之一。意为惩罚。

===Original===
Suicide club - The battalion bombers.

===Chinese===
“自杀俱乐部”：营投弹队。

===Original===
Tin hat - Steel helmet.

===Chinese===
钢盔：钢制头盔。

===Original===
Wave - A line of men going over the top.

===Chinese===
波次：翻出壕顶冲锋时排成一线的一队人。

===Original===
Whacked - Exhausted. Played out.

===Chinese===
累瘫：精疲力竭，累得散了架。

===Original===
Whiz-bang - A German shell that makes that sort of noise.

===Chinese===
嗖砰弹：飞过来时嗖——砰那样响的德国炮弹。

===Original===
Wind up or windy - Nervous. Jumpy. Temporary involuntary fear.

===Chinese===
发怵：紧张，心慌，一时按捺不住的恐惧；windy 即“胆怯的”。

===Original===
Wooden cross - The small wooden cross placed over a soldier’s grave.

===Chinese===
木十字架：立在士兵坟头的小小木十字架。

## II Going In / 二、开进前线

===Original===
The excitement of getting away from camp and the knowledge that we were soon to get into the thick of the big game pleased most of us. We were glad to go. At least we thought so.
Two hundred of us were loaded into side-door Pullmans, forty to the car. It was a kind of sardine or Boston Elevated effect, and by the time we reached Rouen, twenty-four hours later, we had kinks in our legs and corns on our elbows. Also we were hungry, having had nothing but bully beef and biscuits. We made “char,” which is trench slang for tea, in the station, and after two hours moved up the line again, this time in real coaches.
Next night we were billeted at Barlin﻿—don’t get that mixed up with Berlin, it’s not the same﻿—in an abandoned convent within range of the German guns. The roar of artillery was continuous and sounded pretty close.

===Chinese===
离开营地的兴奋劲儿，加上眼看就要一头扎进这场大猎局最激烈处的心知，让我们多数人乐不可支。上前线，我们都乐意。至少当时是这么想的。
我们两百号人被塞进侧开门的普尔曼（Pullman）车厢，每节车皮装四十人。那滋味有点像沙丁鱼罐头，又像挤波士顿高架铁路（Boston Elevated），等二十四小时后抵达鲁昂（Rouen），我们的腿全蜷得打了结，胳膊肘上快磨出鸡眼来了。肚子也饿——一路除了咸牛肉罐头（bully beef）就是饼干。我们在车站沏了“茶”（char，战壕俚语），两个钟头后再度沿线开进，这回总算坐上了正经客车。
第二天夜里，我们在巴兰（Barlin）宿营——别把这地方跟柏林（Berlin）弄混了，不是一码事——住的是一座废弃的女修道院，已经在德国人炮火够得着的范围里。炮声隆隆不断，听着怪近的。

===Original===
Now and again a shell would burst near by with a kind of hollow *spung*, but for some reason we didn’t seem to mind. I had expected to get the shivers at the first sound of the guns and was surprised when I woke up in the morning after a solid night’s sleep.
A message came down from the front trenches at daybreak that we were wanted and wanted quick. We slung together a dixie of char and some bacon and bread for breakfast, and marched around to the “quarters,” where they issued “tin hats,” extra “ammo,” and a second gas helmet. A good many of the men had been out before, and they did the customary “grousing” over the added load.
The British Tommy growls or grouses over anything and everything. He’s never happy unless he’s unhappy. He resents especially having anything officially added to his pack, and you can’t blame him, for in full equipment he certainly is all dressed up like a pack horse.

===Chinese===
不时有炮弹在近旁炸开，空空的一声*噗砰*，可不知怎么的，我们似乎并不当回事。我原以为头一回听见炮响就得打哆嗦，结果一夜睡得死沉，早上醒来自己都觉得意外。
天刚破晓，前线战壕传下话来：要用我们了，而且要得急。我们匆匆焖了一迪希锅（dixie，行军铁锅）的茶，配上培根面包当早饭，随后开到“军需处”，领了“钢盔”（tin hats）、加发的“弹药”，还有第二具防毒头盔（gas helmet）。弟兄里不少人先前上过前线，为这外加的分量照例“发牢骚”（grousing）了一通。
英国大兵汤米（Tommy，英国兵的通称）对什么都要哼哼两句、牢骚两句。他呀，非得有点儿不痛快，心里才算痛快。尤恨上头官方往他的背囊里添东西，这也怪不得他：全副披挂起来，他确确实实打扮得像匹驮马。

===Original===
After the issue we were split up into four lots for the four companies of the battalion, and after some “wangling” I got into Company C, where I stopped all the time I was in France. I was glad, because most of my chums were in that unit.
We got into our packs and started up the line immediately. As we neared the lines we were extended into artillery formation, that is, spread out so that a shell bursting in the road would inflict fewer casualties.
At Bully-Grenay, the point where we entered the communication trenches, guides met us and looked us over, commenting most frankly and freely on our appearance. They didn’t seem to think we would amount to much, and said so. They agreed that the “bloomin’ Yank” must be a “bloody fool” to come out there. There were times later when I agreed with them.

===Chinese===
发放完毕，我们分成四拨，补进本营的四个连。略使了点儿“滑头”（wangling）手段，我进了C连——此后我在法国的全部日子都待在这个连。我挺乐意，因为我的好哥们儿大半都在这个连里。
我们背起行装，立刻沿线开进。临近战线时，队伍展开成炮兵行军队形，也就是拉开间距散开走，这样一发炮弹落在路上，遭殃的人能少几个。
在布利-格勒奈（Bully-Grenay）——我们由此进入交通壕的地方——向导们迎上前来，把我们从头到脚打量一番，对我们的尊容评头论足，坦率得很，也刻薄得很。他们似乎觉得我们成不了什么大器，并且直说了。他们一致认定，那“美国佬”（Yank）准是个“天大的傻瓜”，不然不会跑到这种地方来。后来有些时候，我也同意他们的看法。

===Original===
It began to rain as we entered the communication trench, and I had my first taste of mud. That is literal, for with mud knee-deep in a trench just wide enough for two men to pass you get smeared from head to foot.
Incidentally, as we approached nearer the front, I got my first smell of the dead. It is something you never get away from in the trenches. So many dead have been buried so hastily and so lightly that they are constantly being uncovered by shell bursts. The acrid stench pervades everything, and is so thick you can fairly taste it. It makes nearly everybody deathly sick at first, but one becomes used to it as to anything else.
This communication trench was over two miles long, and it seemed like twenty. We finally landed in a support trench called “Mechanics” (every trench has a name, like a street), and from there into the first-line trench.

===Chinese===
进交通壕时下起了雨，我头一回尝到了烂泥的滋味。这话是字面意思：壕里的泥没膝深，壕宽刚够两个人错身，你从头到脚都得糊上一层。
顺带一提，越靠近前线，我头一回闻到了死人的气味。这股味道，在战壕里你永远躲不开。死人埋得那么多、那么仓促、那么浅，炮弹一炸，就不断把它们翻出地面。那股呛人的恶臭无孔不入，浓得几乎能嚼出味道来。起初几乎人人都要恶心得死去活来，可人对什么都能习惯，对它也一样。
这条交通壕足有两英里多长，走起来像有二十英里。最后我们到了一条名叫“技工壕”（Mechanics）的支援壕——每条战壕都有名字，跟街道似的——再从那里进了第一线战壕。

===Original===
I have to admit a feeling of disappointment in that first trench. I don’t know what I expected to see, but what I did see was just a long, crooked ditch with a low step running along one side, and with sandbags on top. Here and there was a muddy, bedraggled Tommy half asleep, nursing a dirty and muddy rifle on “sentry go.” Everything was very quiet at the moment﻿—no rifles popping, as I had expected, no bullets flying, and, as it happened, absolutely no shelling in the whole sector.
I forgot to say that we had come up by daylight. Ordinarily troops are moved at night, but the communication trench from Bully-Grenay was very deep and was protected at points by little hills, and it was possible to move men in the daytime.
Arrived in the front trench, the sergeant-major appeared, crawling out of his dugout﻿—the usual place for a sergeant-major﻿—and greeted us with,
“Keep your nappers down, you rooks. Don’t look over the top. It ayen’t ’ealthy.”

===Chinese===
我得承认，对这头一道战壕，心里有几分失望。我不知道自己原本指望看到什么，反正亲眼所见，不过是条又长又歪的沟，一侧沿着一道矮台阶，顶上码着沙袋。这儿那儿，立着些泥头土脑、邋里邋遢的汤米，半睡半醒，抱着脏兮兮的步枪“站哨”（sentry go）。这会儿四下里静得很——没有我料想中的步枪噼啪作响，没有子弹乱飞，而且碰巧，整个防区连一发炮弹都没有。
忘了说了，我们是白天开上来的。按惯例部队调动都在夜里，但从布利-格勒奈过来的这条交通壕很深，有些地段又有小山丘遮挡，白天运送兵员也使得。
到了前沿战壕，军士长从他的掩蔽部（dugout）里爬出来——那向来是军士长待的地方——招呼我们道：
“脑袋（nappers）压低点儿，你们这帮新兵（rooks）。别朝壕顶外头张望。那可伤身子骨。”

===Original===
It is the regular warning to new men. For some reason the first emotion of the rookie is an overpowering curiosity. He wants to take a peep into No Man’s Land. It feels safe enough when things are quiet. But there’s always a Fritzie over yonder with a telescope-sighted rifle, and it’s about ten to one he’ll get you if you stick the old “napper” up in daylight.
The Germans, by the way, have had the “edge” on the Allies in the matter of sniping, as in almost all lines of artillery and musketry practice. The Boche sniper is nearly always armed with a periscope-telescope rifle. This is a specially built super-accurate rifle mounted on a periscope frame. It is thrust up over the parapet and the image of the opposing parapet is cast on a little ground-glass screen on which are two crossed lines. At one hundred fifty yards or less the image is brought up to touching distance seemingly. Fritz simply trains his piece on some low place or anywhere that a head may be expected. When one appears on the screen, he pulls the trigger﻿—and you “click it” if you happen to be on the other or receiving end. The shooter never shows himself.
