I remember the first time I looked through a periscope I had no sooner thrust the thing up than a bullet crashed into the upper mirror, splintering it. Many times I have stuck up a cap on a stick and had it pierced.
The British sniper, on the other hand﻿—at least in my time﻿—had a plain telescope rifle and had to hide himself behind old masonry, tree trunks, or anything convenient, and camouflaged himself in all sorts of ways. At that he was constantly in danger.

===Chinese===
这是对新兵的一贯告诫。不知怎的，新兵的头一桩情绪，就是一股按捺不住的好奇心，总想朝无人地带（No Man’s Land）里偷瞧一眼。四下安静的时候，那感觉够安全了。可那头永远守着一个端望远瞄准步枪的弗里茨（Fritz，指德国兵），白天你只要把老“脑袋”探出去，十有八九就得栽在他手里。
顺便说一句，论狙击，德国人一直压协约国一头，在炮兵和步枪射击的几乎各个科目上也是如此。德国佬（Boche）狙击手几乎人人都配一支潜望镜式望远步枪。那是一种特制的高精度步枪，装在潜望镜架上，从胸墙上方伸出去，对面胸墙的影像便投在一小块毛玻璃屏上，屏上画着两根十字交叉线。在一百五十码或更近的距离上，那影像简直被拉到了触手可及的地方。弗里茨只消把枪口对准某处低洼，或任何可能冒出脑袋的地方，屏上一出现影子他就扣扳机——你要是碰巧站在对面，也就是收货的那一头，就算“送命”了。放枪的人自始至终不露分毫。
记得我头一回用潜望镜观察，那玩意儿刚一伸出去，一发子弹就砸在上面的镜子上，砸了个粉碎。后来我不止一次把军帽挑在棍子上伸出去，回回都被打穿。
英国狙击手呢——至少我在役那阵子——用的只是普通的望远瞄准步枪，人得躲进旧砖墙、树干或者随便什么顺手的东西后面，变着法儿伪装自己。饶是这样，他也时时悬着性命。

===Original===
I was assigned to Platoon 10 and found they were a good live bunch. Corporal Wells was the best of the lot, and we became fast friends. He helped me learn a lot of my new duties and the trench “lingo,” which is like a new language, especially to a Yank.
Wells started right in to make me feel at home and took me along with two others of the new men down to our “apartments,” a dugout built for about four, and housing ten.
My previous idea of a dugout had been a fairly roomy sort of cave, somewhat damp, but comparatively comfortable. Well, this hole was about four and a half feet high﻿—you had to get in doubled up on your hands and knees﻿—about five by six feet on the sides, and there was no floor, just muck. There was some sodden, dirty straw and a lot of old moldy sandbags. Seven men and their equipment were packed in here, and we made ten.

===Chinese===
我被分进第10排，发现这一排人是帮活蹦乱跳的好伙计。威尔斯（Wells）下士是其中顶拔尖的，我们成了莫逆之交。他帮着我熟悉了不少新差事，还有战壕“黑话”——那简直像一门新语言，尤其对美国佬来说。
威尔斯立马张罗着让我安下心来，带着我和另外两个新兵下到我们的“公寓”——一处按四人规格修的掩蔽部，塞了十个人。
先前我想象中的掩蔽部，是一种相当宽敞的洞穴，略有些潮，但还算舒适。结果呢，这个洞高约四英尺半——进去得蜷着身子手脚并用爬——两壁间五英尺乘六英尺，没有地板，只有烂泥。有几把浸透了的脏稻草，还有一堆发霉的旧沙袋。里面已经塞下七个人连同他们的装备，我们一进去，凑成了十个。

===Original===
There was a charcoal brazier going in the middle with two or three mess tins of char boiling away. Everybody was smoking, and the place stunk to high heaven, or it would have if there hadn’t been a bit of burlap over the door.
I crowded up into a corner with my back against the mud wall and my knees under my chin. The men didn’t seem overglad to see us, and groused a good deal about the extra crowding. They regarded me with extra disfavor because I was a lance corporal, and they disapproved of any young whippersnapper just out from Blighty with no trench experience pitchforked in with even a slight superior rank. I had thought up to then that a lance corporal was pretty near as important as a brigadier.
“We’ll soon tyke that stripe off ye, me bold lad,” said one big cockney.
They were a decent lot after all. Since we were just out from Blighty, they showered us with questions as to how things looked “t’ ’ome.” And then somebody asked what was the latest song. Right here was where I made my hit and got in right. I sing a bit, and I piped up with the newest thing from the music halls, “Tyke Me Back to Blighty.” Here it is:

===Chinese===
正当中一只炭火盆烧着，两三只饭盒里的茶咕嘟咕嘟滚个不停。人人都在抽烟，那地方臭气冲天——要不是门口挂着块麻袋布，怕是能一直臭到天上去。
我缩进一个角落，背抵着泥墙，膝盖顶着下巴。这帮人见我们来了似乎不怎么高兴，为这额外的拥挤发了一大通牢骚。他们对我格外看不顺眼，因为我是个代理下士（lance corporal），而凡是刚从布莱蒂（Blighty，英国兵对本土的叫法）出来、毫无战壕经历的小毛孩子，哪怕只带着一星半点的官衔被硬塞进来，他们都不待见。在那之前，我一直以为代理下士差不多跟准将一样了不得呢。
“用不了多久就叫你把那道杠扒下来，我的好汉小子，”一个大个子伦敦佬（cockney）说。
不过话说回来，这帮人心眼儿不坏。见我们刚打布莱蒂过来，他们拉着我们问长问短，打听“国内”如今是个什么光景。跟着有人问起眼下最新流行的歌是哪支。机会来了，正是我露一手、站稳脚跟的时候。我会唱上几句，便扯开嗓子唱起音乐厅里最新的玩意儿——《带我回布莱蒂》（Tyke Me Back to Blighty）。歌是这样唱的：

===Original===
> Tyke me back to dear old Blighty,
 

 Put me on the tryne for London town,
 

 Just tyke me over there
 

 And drop me anywhere,
 

 Manchester, Leeds, or Birmingham,
 

 I don’t care.
> I want to go see me best gal;
 

 Cuddlin’ up soon we’ll be,
 

 Hytey iddle de eyety.
 

 Tyke me back to Blighty,
 

 That’s the plyce for me.

===Chinese===
> 带我回亲爱的老布莱蒂，
 

 送我登上开往伦敦城的火车，
 

 只要把我带过去，
 

 随便撂在哪儿都行，
 

 曼彻斯特、利兹或伯明翰，
 

 我都无所谓。
> 我要去看我的心上人；
 

 咱俩很快就能依偎在一起，
 

 哼着嗨蒂德勒艾依蒂。
 

 带我回布莱蒂，
 

 那才是我的去处。

===Original===
It doesn’t look like much and I’m afraid my rendition of cockney dialect into print isn’t quite up to Kipling’s. But the song had a pretty little lilting melody, and it went big. They made me sing it about a dozen times and were all joining in at the end.
Then they got sentimental﻿—and gloomy.
“Gawd lumme!” says the big fellow who had threatened my beloved stripes. “Wot a life. Squattin’ ’ere in the bloody mud like a blinkin’ frog. Fightin’ fer wot? Wot, I arsks yer? Gawd lumme! I’d give me bloomin’ napper to stroll down the Strand agyne wif me swagger stick an’ drop in a private bar an’ ’ave me go of ’Aig an’ ’Aig.”
“Garn,” cuts in another Tommy. “Yer blinkin’ ’igh wif yer wants, ayen’t ye? An’ yer ’Aig an’ ’Aig. Drop me down in Great Lime Street (Liverpool) an’ it’s me fer the Golden Sheaf, and a pint of bitter, an’ me a ’oldin’ ’Arriet’s ’and over th’ bar. I’m a courtin’ ’er when,” etc., etc.
And then a fresh-faced lad chirps up: “T’ ’ell wif yer Lonnon an’ yer whuskey. Gimme a jug o’ cider on the sunny side of a ’ay rick in old Surrey. Gimme a happle tart to go wif it. Gawd, I’m fed up on bully beef.”

===Chinese===
这歌印出来瞧着不起眼，我怕自己把伦敦土腔搬上纸面的功夫，也远赶不上吉卜林（Kipling）。可这支歌有段轻快动听的小旋律，一唱就唱红了。他们拉着我唱了十来遍，到后来全都跟着一块儿唱。
随后他们就伤感起来——也灰暗下来。
“我的老天爷！”那个先前扬言要扒我宝贝军衔杠的大个子开了腔。“这过的叫什么日子！蹲在这该死的烂泥里，活像只倒霉的青蛙。打仗图个啥？我问你，图个啥？我的老天爷！只要能拄着小手杖再去斯特兰德街（the Strand）遛个弯儿，拐进一家雅座酒吧，来上一份‘黑格与黑格’（’Aig an’ ’Aig，即 Haig and Haig 威士忌），我情愿把这颗脑袋搭上去。”
“去你的，”另一个汤米插嘴。“你小子心气儿倒高，是吧？还‘黑格与黑格’呢。把我撂到大莱姆街（Great Lime Street，利物浦），我立马直奔‘金穗’（Golden Sheaf）酒馆，来上一品脱苦啤酒，隔着柜台攥住哈丽雅特（’Arriet）的手。我这正跟她处着对象呢，”如此等等，如此等等。
接着一个脸蛋儿还嫩着的小伙子尖声搭话：“让你的伦敦和你那威士忌统统见鬼去。给我一罐苹果酒，坐在老萨里郡（Surrey）向阳的干草垛边上，再来块苹果挞就着。天老爷，这咸牛肉我是真吃顶了。”

===Original===
And so it went. All about pubs and barmaids and the things they’d eat and drink, and all of it Blighty.
They were in the midst of a discussion of what part of the body was most desirable to part with for a permanent Blighty wound when a young officer pushed aside the burlap and wedged in. He was a lieutenant and was in command of our platoon. His name was Blofeld.
Blofeld was most democratic. He shook hands with the new men and said he hoped we’d be live wires, and then he told us what he wanted. There was to be a raid the next night and he was looking for volunteers.
Nobody spoke for a long minute, and then I offered.
I think I spoke more to break the embarrassing silence than anything else. I think, too, that I was led a little by a kind of youthful curiosity, and it may be that I wanted to appear brave in the eyes of these men who so evidently held me more or less in contempt as a newcomer.

===Chinese===
就这么你一言我一语。说的全是酒馆、酒吧女招待，还有他们想吃想喝的那些东西——桩桩件件，都是布莱蒂。
他们正讨论到热闹处——为了换一处能永远回布莱蒂的伤，身上哪个部位最舍得搭进去——这时一个年轻军官掀开麻袋布，挤了进来。他是位中尉，指挥我们排。名叫布洛菲尔德（Blofeld）。
布洛菲尔德一点儿架子没有。他跟新兵们挨个握手，说盼着我们个个是生龙活虎的机灵人，随后便道明来意：明晚要搞一次突袭，他正在招志愿者。
足足过了一分钟没人吭声，然后我报了名。
如今想来，我开口与其说是为了别的，不如说是为了打破那阵难堪的沉默。大概也有几分少年人的好奇在里头；说不定，我还想在这些明显多少拿我当外行看轻的汉子们眼前，显出一副勇敢的样子。

===Original===
Blofeld accepted me, and one of the other new men offered. He was taken too.
It turned out that all the older men were married and that they were not expected to volunteer. At least there was no disgrace attaching to a refusal.
After Blofeld left, Sergeant Page told us we’d better get down to “kip” while we could. “Kip” in this case meant closing our eyes and dozing. I sat humped up in my original position through the night. There wasn’t room to stretch out.
Along toward morning I began to itch, and found I had made the acquaintance of that gay and festive little soldier’s enemy, the “cootie.” The cootie, or the “chat” as he is called by the officers, is the common body louse. Common is right. I never got rid of mine until I left the service. Sometimes when I get to thinking about it, I believe I haven’t yet.

===Chinese===
布洛菲尔德收下了我，另一个新兵也报了名，同样被收下了。
后来才知道，老弟兄们都成了家，本就不指望他们志愿报名。至少，拒了也不算丢人。
布洛菲尔德走后，佩奇军士（Sergeant Page）对我们说，趁眼下能“睡觉”（kip）赶紧睡。这儿的“睡觉”，意思是闭上眼睛打个盹儿。我保持着原先的姿势蜷了一整夜——连伸开腿的地方都没有。
快天亮的时候，我开始浑身发痒，这才发觉自己结识了那位欢天喜地、最爱热闹的大兵小冤家——“虱子”（cootie）。虱子，军官们管它叫“chat”，其实就是普通的体虱。说普通真是普通。我那一份一直跟到我退伍才算甩脱。有时候细细一想，我疑心到今天还没甩脱呢。

## VI Hiking to Vimy Ridge / 六、行军维米岭

===Original===
We left Petite-Saens about nine o’clock Friday night and commenced our march for what we were told would be a short hike. It was pretty warm and muggy. There was a thin, low-lying mist over everything, but clear enough above, and there was a kind of poor moonlight. There was a good deal of delay in getting away, and we had begun to sweat before we started, as we were equipped as usual with about eighty pounds’ weight on the back and shoulders. That eighty pounds is theoretical weight.

As a matter of practice the pack nearly always runs ten and even twenty pounds over the official equipment, as Tommy is a great little accumulator of junk. I had acquired the souvenir craze early in the game, and was toting excess baggage in the form of a Boche helmet, a mess of shell noses, and a smashed German automatic. All this ran to weight.

I carried a lot of this kind of stuff all the time I was in the service, and was constantly thinning out my collection or adding to it.

When you consider that a soldier has to carry everything he owns on his person, you’d say that he would want to fly light; but he doesn’t. And that reminds me, before I forget it, I want to say something about sending boxes over there.

===Chinese===
星期五晚上九点来钟，我们离开了佩蒂特-桑（Petite-Saens），踏上行军路——上头说，这不过是趟短途行军。天气又闷又热。一层薄薄的低雾罩着万物，头顶上倒还算晴朗，月色淡淡的，有气无力。开拔前耽搁了老半天，还没动身我们先冒了汗，因为照例全副披挂，肩上背上压着八十来磅。这八十磅，是理论重量。

实际上，背囊几乎回回都要比制式装备多出十磅乃至二十磅，因为汤米（Tommy，英国兵的通称）攒破烂的本事实在一流。我入伙没多久就染上了搜集纪念品的瘾，于是多背了些超额行李：一顶德国佬（Boche）钢盔、一大把炮弹头，还有一支砸烂的德国自动手枪。这些玩意儿，都是压秤的。

服役期间，这类劳什子我身上一直背了不少，收藏也不断吐故纳新——不是清掉一批，就是再添几件。

细想起来，士兵的全部家当都得扛在自己身上，你准会说他该想轻装上路；可他偏不。这倒提醒了我——趁还没忘，我得说说往那边寄包裹的事。

===Original===
It is the policy of the British, and, I suppose, will be of the Americans, to move the troops about a good deal. This is done so that no one unit will become too much at home in any one line of trenches and so get careless. This moving about involves a good deal of hiking.

Now if some chap happens to get a twenty-pound box of good things just before he is shifted, he’s going to be in an embarrassing position. He’ll have to give it away or leave it. So﻿—send the boxes two or three pounds at a time, and often.

But to get back to Petite-Saens. We commenced our hike as it is was getting dark. As we swung out along the once good but now badly furrowed French road, we could see the Very lights beginning to go up far off to the left, showing where the lines were. We could distinguish between our own star lights and the German by the intensity of the flare, theirs being much superior to ours, so much so that they send them up from the second-line trenches.

===Chinese===
英军的方针是把部队频繁调动，我想美军将来也会如此。这样调来调去，为的是不让哪一个部队在某一条战壕里住得太熟、住得吊儿郎当。而这一调动，就意味着大量的徒步行军。

这时节，哪个小子要是恰好赶在调防前收到一只二十磅重的大箱好东西，那他可就难堪了：只好送人，或者扔下。所以说——寄包裹，一次寄个两三磅，勤寄。

话归正题，回到佩蒂特-桑。天将黑时，我们上了路。沿着那条昔日平整、如今被碾得沟壑纵横的法国公路开出不远，便望见左边远处维里（Very）信号弹开始升起，标出战线的所在。光凭光焰的强度，就能分清哪是自家的星光弹、哪是德国人的——他们的比我们的亮出一大截，亮到尽可以从第二线战壕往天上打。

===Original===
The sound of the guns became more distant as we swung away to the south and louder again as the road twisted back toward the front.

We began to sing the usual songs of the march and I noticed that the American ragtime was more popular among the boys than their own music. “Dixie” frequently figured in these songs.

It is always a good deal easier to march when the men sing, as it helps to keep time and puts pep into a column and makes the packs seem lighter. The officers see to it that the mouth organs get tuned up the minute a hike begins.

At the end of each hour we came to a halt for the regulation ten minutes’ rest. Troops in heavy marching order move very slowly, even with the music﻿—and the hours drag. The ten minutes’ rest though goes like a flash. The men keep an eye on the watches and “wangle” for the last second.

===Chinese===
我们折向南方时，炮声渐渐远去；公路一拐，又朝前线折回来，炮声便重新大了起来。

大家唱起了行军路上的老调子。我注意到，美国的拉格泰姆（ragtime）曲子在这些小伙子中间，比他们本国的音乐还受欢迎，《迪克西》（Dixie）就常在这些歌里露脸。

弟兄们一唱歌，行军就轻松得多：歌声帮大家踩着步点，给队伍提气，连背囊仿佛都轻了几分。所以行军令一下，军官们就张罗着让口琴先吹起来。

每走满一个钟头，队伍便停下，照章休息十分钟。全副行军装的部队走得极慢，纵有音乐助兴——时辰也磨蹭得慌；可那十分钟休息，却一晃就没了。弟兄们眼睛盯着表，把休息时间一直“磨”到最后一秒。

===Original===
We passed through two ruined villages with the battered walls sticking up like broken teeth and the gray moonlight shining through empty holes that had been windows. The people were gone from these places, but a dog howled over yonder. Several times we passed batteries of French artillery, and jokes and laughter came out of the half darkness.

Topping a little rise, the moon came out bright, and away ahead the silver ribbon of the Souchez gleamed for an instant; the bare poles that once had been Bouvigny Wood were behind us, and to the right, to the left, a pulverized ruin where houses had stood. Blofeld told me this was what was left of the village of Abalaine, which had been demolished some time before when the French held the sector.

At this point guides came out and met us to conduct us to the trenches. The order went down the line to fall in, single file, keeping touch, no smoking and no talking, and I supposed we were about to enter a communication trench. But no. We swung on to a “duck walk.” This is a slatted wooden walk built to prevent as much as possible sinking into the mud. The ground was very soft here.

===Chinese===
我们穿过两座毁掉的村庄，残破的墙壁龇出来，像一口口碎牙，灰白的月光从一个个曾是窗户的空窟窿里漏进来。这些地方的人早跑光了，只有一条狗在远处嗥叫。好几回，我们从法军炮兵中队旁边经过，半昏的夜色里传出玩笑声和笑声。

爬上一道小坡，月亮放出亮来，前头远处，苏谢河（Souchez）那条银色的绸带闪了一瞬；从前是布维尼树林（Bouvigny Wood）的那片光杆树桩，已被抛在身后；右边左边，满是被碾成齑粉的废墟，那里原本立着一座座房屋。布洛菲尔德告诉我，这就是阿巴兰（Abalaine）村剩下的全部——早先法国人守这段防线时，村子就被捣毁了。

到这里，向导出来接我们，领我们进战壕。口令沿着队列传下去：集合，一路纵队，保持联络，不许抽烟，不许说话。我估摸着这就该钻交通壕了。偏偏不。我们上了一截“木板路”（duck walk）——板条钉的木便道，为的是让人尽量少往泥里陷。这一带的地面烂透了。

===Original===
I never did know why there was no communication trench unless it was because the ground was so full of moisture. But whatever the reason, there was none, and we were right out in the open on the duck walk. The order for no talk seemed silly as we clattered along the boards, making a noise like a four-horse team on a covered bridge.

I immediately wondered whether we were near enough for the Boches to hear. I wasn’t in doubt long, for they began to send over the “Berthas” in flocks. The “Bertha” is an uncommonly ugly breed of nine-inch shell loaded with H.E. It comes sailing over with a querulous *squeeeeeee*, and explodes with an earsplitting crash and a burst of murky, dull-red flame.

If it hits you fair, you disappear. At a little distance you are ripped to fragments, and a little farther off you get a case of shell-shock. Just at the edge of the destructive area the wind of the explosion whistles by your ears, and then sucks back more slowly.

===Chinese===
我始终没弄明白，这里为什么不修交通壕，除非是地里的水汽太重。但不管什么缘故，反正没有交通壕，我们就这么走在这条木板路上，完全暴露在旷野里。板子被我们踩得哐啷哐啷响，活像四驾马车跑上了廊桥，这时候还下令不许说话，简直可笑。

我立刻琢磨：我们是不是已经近得叫德国佬听见了？这疑问没悬多久——他们开始成群结队地把“贝尔塔”（Bertha）送过来。“贝尔塔”是九英寸口径、装着高爆炸药（H.E.）的丑八怪炮弹。它呜呜咽咽拖着一声*咿——————*飘过来，炸开时一声撕裂耳朵的巨响，腾起一股昏浊的暗红色火焰。

要是正面砸中你，你就凭空消失了。隔开一点距离，你会被撕成碎片；再远一点，你会落下一场弹震症（shell-shock）。恰在杀伤圈的边上，爆炸的疾风呼啸着擦过你的耳朵，接着又慢吞吞地吸回去。

===Original===
The Boches had the range of that duck walk, and we began to run. Every now and then they would drop one near the walk, and from four to ten casualties would go down. There was no stopping for the wounded. They lay where they fell. We kept on the run, sometimes on the duck walk, sometimes in the mud, for three miles. I had reached the limit of my endurance when we came to a halt and rested for a little while at the foot of a slight incline. This was the “Pimple,” so called on account of its rounded crest.

The Pimple forms a part of the well-known Vimy Ridge﻿—is a semidetached extension of it﻿—and lies between it and the Souchez sector. After a rest here we got into the trenches skirting the Pimple and soon came out on the Quarries. This was a bowl-like depression formed by an old quarry. The place gave a natural protection and all around the edge were dugouts which had been built by the French, running back into the hill, some of them more than a hundred feet.

In the darkness we could see braziers glowing softly red at the mouth of each burrow. There was a cheerful, mouth-watering smell of cookery on the air, a garlicky smell, with now and then a whiff of spicy wood smoke.

===Chinese===
德国佬把那条木板路的射距摸得准准的，我们撒腿就跑。他们隔三差五往路边撂一发，每发总要撂倒四到十个人。没人停下来救伤号：倒在哪，就躺在哪。我们一路奔逃，有时在木板路上，有时在烂泥里，足足三英里。等队伍终于停在一道缓坡脚下稍事休息，我的耐力已经到了头。这就是“疙瘩丘”（the Pimple），因丘顶浑圆得了这么个名号。

疙瘩丘是大名鼎鼎的维米岭（Vimy Ridge）的一部分——算是它半独立的一条延伸——夹在维米岭与苏谢河地段之间。在这里歇过一气，我们进入绕着疙瘩丘边缘走的战壕，不久便到了采石场（Quarries）。这是一处碗状的洼地，由一座老采石坑形成。这地方有天然屏障，四周边缘尽是法国人修的掩蔽部，一个个往山肚子里延伸，有的深过一百英尺。

黑暗里，只见每个洞口都有一盆炭火幽幽地泛着红光。空气里飘着一股叫人垂涎的饭菜香——一股蒜香，间或夹着一缕带辛香味的柴烟。

===Original===
We were hungry and thirsty, as well as tired, and shed our packs at the dugouts assigned us and went at the grub and the char offered us by the men we were relieving, the Northumberland Fusiliers.

The dugouts here in the Quarries were the worst I saw in France. They were reasonably dry and roomy, but they had no ventilation except the tunnel entrance, and going back so far the air inside became simply stifling in a very short time.

I took one inhale of the interior atmosphere and decided right there that I would bivouac in the open. It was just getting down to “kip” when a sentry came up and said I would have to get inside. It seemed that Fritz had the range of the Quarries to an inch and was in the habit of sending over “minnies” at intervals just to let us know he wasn’t asleep.

===Chinese===
我们又累又渴又饿，在分给我们的掩蔽部里卸下行囊，扑向被换下来的弟兄——诺森伯兰燧发枪团（Northumberland Fusiliers）的人——递上来的吃食和茶（char）。

采石场这些掩蔽部，是我在法国见过最糟的。倒还算干爽，也算宽敞，可除了坑道口，再无一处通风；进深又那么长，里头的空气不多时就闷得叫人窒息。

我朝掩蔽部里的空气吸了一口，当场拍板：宁可露天宿营。刚要躺下“睡觉”（kip），一个哨兵走过来，说我必须进里面去。据说弗里茨（Fritz，指德国兵）对采石场的射距摸得一寸不差，还有个习惯：隔三差五送几发“明妮”（minnies）过来，好叫我们知道他没睡着。

===Original===
I had got settled down comfortably and was dozing off when there came a call for C company. I got the men from my platoon out as quickly as possible, and in half an hour we were in the trenches.

Number 10 platoon was assigned to the center sector, Number 11 to the left sector, and Number 12 to the right sector. Number 9 remained behind in supports in the Quarries.

Now when I speak of these various sectors, I mean that at this point there was no continuous line of front trenches, only isolated stretches of trench separated by intervals of from two hundred to three hundred yards of open ground. There were no dugouts. It was impossible to leave these trenches except under cover of darkness﻿—or to get to them or to get up rations. They were awful holes. Any raid by the Germans in large numbers at this time would have wiped us out, as there was no means of retreating or getting up reinforcements.

===Chinese===
我刚安顿舒坦、眼看着要打盹，传来了C连的集合令。我以最快的速度把自己排里的人拉出来，半小时后，我们已经进了战壕。

第10排分到中段，第11排分到左段，第12排分到右段。第9排留在采石场充作预备队。

这里得交代一句：我说到这几个地段时，意思是这一带根本没有连成一线的前沿战壕，只有一段段孤零零的壕沟，中间隔着二百到三百码的开阔地。没有掩蔽部。除非借着夜色，否则离不开这些战壕——也到不了这些战壕，口粮也送不上来。它们是可怕的坑。这时候德国人要是大举突袭一回，准能把我们全端了，因为既无路可退，也没法把增援调上来。

===Original===
The Tommies called the trenches Grouse Spots. It was a good name. We got into them in the dense darkness of just before dawn. The division we relieved gave us hardly any instruction, but beat it on the hot foot, glad to get away and anxious to go before sunup. As we settled down in our cozy danger spots I heard Rolfie, the frog-voiced baritone, humming one of his favorite coster songs:

> Oh, why did I leave my little back room in old Bloomsbury?
 

 Where I could live for a pound a week in luxury.
 

 I wanted to live higher
 

 So I married Marier,
 

 Out of the frying pan into the bloomin’ fire.

And he meant every word of it.

===Chinese===
汤米们管这些战壕叫松鸡窝（Grouse Spots）。这名字起得好。我们在拂晓前最浓的黑暗里钻了进去。被我们换下来的那个师几乎什么都没交代，拔腿就溜，乐得脱身，一心要在日出前走掉。等我们在自己这舒舒服服的危险窝里安顿下来，我听见罗尔菲（Rolfie）——那位青蛙嗓的男中音——哼起他心爱的街头小贩小调之一：

> 唉，我干吗要离开老布卢姆斯伯里那间小背屋？
>
> 在那儿，一星期一镑，日子过得赛神仙。
>
> 我偏想过高一档的日子，
>
> 便娶了马丽埃尔，
>
> 才出煎锅，又跌进该死的大火。

他这字字句句，可都是掏心窝子的实话。

===Original===
In our new positions in the Grouse Spots the orders were to patrol the open ground between at least four times a night. That first night there was one more patrol necessary before daylight. Tired as I was, I volunteered for it. I had had one patrol before, opposite Bully-Grenay, and thought I liked the game.

I went over with one man, a fellow named Bellinger. We got out and started to crawl. All we knew was that the left sector was two hundred yards away. Machine-gun bullets were squealing and snapping overhead pretty continuously, and we had to hug the dirt. It is surprising to see how flat a man can keep and still get along at a good rate of speed. We kept straight away to the left and presently got into wire. And then we heard German voices. Ow! I went cold all over.

Then some “Very” lights went up and I saw the Boche parapet not twenty feet away. Worst of all there was a little lane through their wire at that point, and there would be, no doubt, a sap head or a listening post near. I tried to lie still and burrow into the dirt at the same time. Nothing happened. Presently the lights died, and Bellinger gave me a poke in the ribs. We started to crawfish. Why we weren’t seen I don’t know, but we had gone all of one hundred feet before they spotted us. Fortunately we were on the edge of a shallow shell hole when the sentry caught our movements and Fritz cut loose with the “typewriters.” We rolled in. A perfect torrent of bullets ripped up the dirt and cascaded us with gravel and mud. The noise of the bullets “crackling” a yard above us was deafening.

===Chinese===
在松鸡窝的新防位上，命令规定：中间的开阔地一夜至少巡逻四回。头一夜，天亮前还差一趟巡逻。我累是累到了家，还是自愿报了名。先前在布利-格勒奈（Bully-Grenay）对面我巡逻过一回，自以为喜欢上了这一行。

我带一个人出去，是个叫贝林杰（Bellinger）的家伙。我们出了壕，开始匍匐。全部线索只有一条：左段在二百码外。机枪子弹在头顶上尖啸、炸响，几乎没有停过，我们只得死死贴地。一个人能趴得多么扁、却又爬得相当快，见了准叫你吃惊。我们照直朝左边去，不多时钻进了铁丝网。跟着，我们听见了德国人说话的声音。哎哟！我浑身一凉。

接着几发维里信号弹升起来，我看见德国佬的胸墙就在不到二十英尺外。最糟的是，他们铁丝网的那一处正开着一条小通道，附近八成有个支坑道口或者听音哨。我一边竭力一动不动，一边往土里钻。什么也没有发生。不多时信号弹熄了，贝林杰捅了捅我的肋骨。我们学小龙虾，倒退着往回爬。为什么没被发现，我说不上来，反正我们足足爬出一百英尺，才被他们瞧见。走运的是，哨兵察觉动静、弗里茨操起“打字机”（typewriters，指机枪）开火的时候，我们正挨着一个浅弹坑的边儿。我们滚了进去。子弹的激流把泥土犁得四下翻起，碎石烂泥瀑布似的浇在我们身上。子弹在头顶一码高处“噼啪”炸响，震得人耳朵发聋。

===Original===
The fusillade stopped after a bit. I was all for getting out and away immediately. Bellinger wanted to wait a while. We argued for as much as five minutes, I should think, and then the lights having gone out, I took matters in my own hands and we went away from there. Another piece of luck!

We weren’t more than a minute on our way when a pair of bombs went off about over the shell hole. Evidently some bold Heinie had chucked them over to make sure of the job in case the machines hadn’t. It was a close pinch﻿—two close pinches. I was in places afterwards where there was more action and more danger, but, looking back, I don’t think I was ever sicker or scareder. I would have been easy meat if they had rushed us.

We made our way back slowly, and eventually caught the gleam of steel helmets. They were British. We had stumbled upon our left sector. We found out then that the line curved and that instead of the left sector being directly to the left of ours﻿—the center﻿—it was to the left and to the rear. Also there was a telephone wire running from one to the other. We reported and made our way back to the center in about five minutes by feeling along the wire. That was our method afterwards, and the patrol was cushy for us.

===Chinese===
扫射过了一阵停了。我极力主张立刻出去赶紧走人。贝林杰想再等等。我估摸着，我俩争了足有五分钟，后来照明弹熄了，我便自作主张，带他离开了那地方。又是一桩走运的事！

上路不过一分钟，两颗手雷就在弹坑上头附近炸开了。八成是哪个胆大的海尼（Heinie）怕机枪没办利索，又扔过来两颗，务求万无一失。好险——连着两回好险。后来我也到过战斗更激烈、危险更大的地方，可回头看，我从来没有比那会儿更难受、更害怕的。他们当时要是冲过来收拾我们，我们就是送到嘴边的现成肉。

我们慢慢往回摸，终于望见钢盔的微光。是英国钢盔。我们竟一头撞上了自家的左段。这时我们才弄明白，战线是弯的：左段并不在我们这一段——中段——的正左方，而是在左后方。而且两段之间牵着一根电话线。我们报了到，顺着那根线摸索，约莫五分钟就回到了中段。这法子后来一直是我们的老路数，巡逻对我们来说，也就成了舒坦（cushy）差事。

## XII I Become a Bomber / 十二、我当上了投弹手

===Original===
When I found my battalion, the battle of High Wood had pretty well quieted down. We had taken the position we went after, and the fighting was going on to the north and beyond the Wood. The Big Push progressed very rapidly as the summer drew to a close. Our men were holding one of the captured positions in the neighborhood of the Wood.

It must have been two days after we went over the top with the tanks that Captain Green had me up and told me that I was promoted. At least that was what he called it. I differed with him, but didn’t say so.

The Captain said that as I had had a course in bombing, he thought he would put me in the Battalion Bombers.

I protested that the honor was too great and that I really didn’t think I was good enough.

After that the Captain said that he didn’t *think* I was going in the bombers. He *knew* it. I was elected!

===Chinese===
等我找回自己营里的时候，海伍德（High Wood）之战已经基本平息下来。我们要的那处阵地到手了，战斗转移到了林子以北、以外的地方。夏末将至，大攻势（Big Push）推进得飞快。我们的人守着林子附近一处新近攻占的阵地。

大约是我们跟着坦克翻出壕顶冲锋之后两天，格林上尉（Captain Green）把我叫了去，说我高升了。至少他是这么个叫法。我不这么看，不过没说出口。

上尉说，我既然上过投弹训练班，他想把我调进营投弹队（Battalion Bombers）。

我推辞说，这份荣誉太重，我实在觉得自己的斤两不够。

听了这话，上尉改了口，说他不是*认为*我要进投弹队，他是*知道*我要进。我这是“当选”了！

===Original===
I didn’t take any joy whatever in the appointment, but orders are orders and they have to be obeyed. The bombers are called the “Suicide Club” and are well named. The mortality in this branch of the service is as great if not greater than in any other.

In spite of my feelings in the matter, I accepted the decision cheerfully﻿—like a man being sentenced to be electrocuted﻿—and managed to convey the impression to Captain Green that I was greatly elated and that I looked forward to future performances with large relish. After that I went back to my shelter and made a new will.

That very night I was called upon to take charge of a bombing party of twelve men. A lieutenant, Mr. May, one of the bravest men I ever knew, was to be of the party and in direct command. I was to have the selection of the men.

Captain Green had me up along with Lieutenant May early in the evening, and as nearly as I can remember these were his instructions:

“Just beyond High Wood and to the left there is a sap or small trench leading to the sunken road that lies between the towns of Albert and Bapaume. That position commands a military point that we find necessary to hold before we can make another attack. The Germans are in the trench. They have two machine guns and will raise the devil with us unless we get them out. It will cost a good many lives if we attempt to take the position by attack, but we are under the impression that a bombing party in the night on a surprise attack will be able to take it with little loss of life. Take your twelve men out there at ten o’clock and *take that trench*! You will take only bombs with you. You and Mr. May will have revolvers. After taking the trench, consolidate it, and before morning there will be relief sent out to you. The best of luck!”

===Chinese===
这桩任命没让我沾到半点高兴，可军令如山，不服不行。投弹手被称作“自杀俱乐部”（Suicide Club），这名字起得半点不冤。干这一行的死亡率，就算不比别的兵种高，也绝不比哪一个低。

尽管心里这么想，我还是欢天喜地接受了这项决定——活像个被判了电椅的人欢天喜地接受判决——并且设法让格林上尉留下这么个印象：我得意坏了，眼巴巴盼着日后大显身手。随后我回到掩蔽处，另立了一份新遗嘱。

就在当天夜里，上头点我带队——一支十二人的投弹小队。一位中尉，梅先生（May），我认识的顶勇敢的人之一，也将随队同行，负责直接指挥。挑人的差事归我。

傍晚早些时候，格林上尉把我和梅中尉一道叫了去。凭记忆，他的命令大致是这样：

“出了海伍德再往左，有一条支坑道（sap）——也可以说是一条小壕沟——通向阿尔贝（Albert）和巴波姆（Bapaume）两镇之间那条低洼道。那处阵地俯控着一个军事要点，我们要发动下一次进攻，就得先把它攥在手里。德国人占着那条壕。他们有两挺机枪，不把他们撵出去，就要跟咱们闹个天翻地覆。强攻的话，得赔上许多条人命；不过我们估摸着，夜里派一支投弹小队搞突袭，用不了多大伤亡就能拿下。十点钟，带上你的十二个人开过去，把那条战壕给我*拿下来*！你们随身只带手雷。你和梅先生带左轮枪。拿下战壕后就地巩固，天亮前会派接防部队出去接应。祝你们好运！”

===Original===
The whole thing sounded as simple as A.B.C. All we had to do was go over there and take the place. The captain didn’t say how many Germans there would be nor what they would be doing while we were taking their comfortable little position. Indeed he seemed to quite carelessly leave the Boche out of the reckoning. I didn’t. I knew that some of us, and quite probably most of us, would never come back.

I selected my men carefully, taking only the coolest and steadiest and the best bombers. Most of them were men who had been at Dover with me. I felt like an executioner when I notified them of their selection.

At nine thirty we were ready, stripped to the lightest of necessary equipment. Each of the men was armed with a bucket of bombs. Some carried an extra supply in satchels, so we knew there would be no shortage of Millses.

Lieutenant May took us out over the top on schedule time, and we started for the position to be taken. We walked erect but in the strictest silence for about a thousand yards. At that time the distances were great on the Somme, as the Big Push was in full swing, and the advance had been fast. Trench systems had been demolished, and in many places there were only shell holes and isolated pieces of trench defended by machine guns. The whole movement had progressed so far that the lines were far apart and broken, so much so that in many cases the fighting had come back to the open work of early in the war.

===Chinese===
整件事听上去简单得跟 ABC 一样。我们只消开过去把地方占了就是。上尉没说那儿会有多少德国佬（Boche），也没说我们动手抢人家那处舒舒服服的小阵地时，他们会干些什么。说实在的，他把德国佬漫不经心地略出了账外。我没有。我知道我们中间有些人——多半是大多数人——回不来了。

我挑人挑得很仔细，只要最沉着、最稳当、投弹技术最好的。他们大半是当年跟我在多佛一起待过的人。宣布局下人选的时候，我觉得自己像个刽子手。

九点半，我们准备停当，装备精简到只留最轻的必需品。每个人的武器是一桶手雷，有的还用挎包额外多背了些，所以我们知道，“米尔斯”（Mills）手雷管保够用。

梅中尉按时带我们翻出壕顶，朝目标阵地进发。我们挺直身子走路，却保持着最严格的静默，走了约莫一千码。那时候索姆河（Somme）战场上战线隔得极宽，大攻势正进行得热火朝天，推进飞快。成体系的堑壕都给轰平了，许多地方只剩一个个弹坑，加上几段孤零零、靠机枪守着的残壕。整个战局已经推进到这个地步：两军战线隔得老远、处处断续，以至于好些地段的战斗，又回到了战争初期的野战路数。

===Original===
Poking along out there, I had the feeling that we were an awfully long way from the comparative safety of our main body﻿—too far away for comfort. We were. Any doubts on the matter disappeared before morning.

At the end of the thousand yards Lieutenant May gave the signal to lie down. We lay still half an hour or so and then crawled forward. Fortunately there was no barbed wire, as all entanglements had been destroyed by the terrific bombardment that had been going on for weeks. The Germans made no attempt to repair it nor did we.

We crawled along for about ten minutes, and the Lieutenant passed the word in whispers to get ready, as we were nearly on them. Each of us got out a bomb, pulled the pin with our teeth, and waited for the signal. It was fairly still. Away off to the rear, guns were going, but they seemed a long way off. Forward, and away off to the right beyond the Wood, there was a lot of rifle and machine-gun fire, and we could see the sharp little lavender stabs of flame like electric flashes. It was light enough so that we could see dimly.

Just ahead we could hear the murmur of the Huns as they chatted in the trench. They hadn’t seen us. Evidently they didn’t suspect and were more or less careless.

===Chinese===
在那儿深一脚浅一脚地往前蹭，我感觉到我们离大部队那点相对的安全实在远了——远得叫人心里不踏实。确实远。这事儿的任何疑问，天亮之前都烟消云散了。

一千码走完，梅中尉打手势让大家卧倒。我们一动不动趴了半个来钟头，然后匍匐前进。所幸没有铁丝网：连着几个星期的猛烈炮击，把所有障碍物都犁光了。德国人没打算修补，我们也没修。

爬了约莫十分钟，中尉用耳语传下话来：准备，我们已经快贴上他们了。我们各自掏出一颗手雷，用牙咬开保险销，等信号。四下相当安静。远远的后方有炮在响，听着隔得很远。正面，还有林子右边老远的地方，步枪和机枪打得热闹，看得见一道道淡紫色的尖细火舌，像电火花似的闪闪。天色亮得足够我们影影绰绰看见东西。

正前方，听得见德国佬们在壕里闲聊的嗡嗡低语。他们没瞧见我们。显然毫无戒心，多多少少有些大意。

===Original===
The Lieutenant waited until the sound of voices was a little louder than before, the Boches evidently being engaged in a fireside argument of some kind, and then he jumped to his feet shouting, “Now then, my lads. All together!”

We came up all standing and let ’em go. It was about fifteen yards to Fritz, and that is easy to a good bomber, as my men all were. A yell of surprise and fright went up from the trench, and they started to run. We spread out so as to get room, gave them another round of Millses, and rushed.

The trench wasn’t really a trench at all. It was the remains of a perfectly good one, but had been bashed all to pieces, and was now only five or six shell craters connected by the ruined traverses. At no point was it more than waist high and in some places only knee high. We swarmed into what was left of the trench and after the Heinies. There must have been forty of them, and it didn’t take them long to find out that we were only a dozen. Then they came back at us. We got into a crooked bit of traverse that was in relatively good shape and threw up a barricade of sandbags. There was any amount of them lying about.

The Germans gave us a bomb or two and considerable rifle fire, and we beat it around the corner of the bay. Then we had it back and forth, a regular seesaw game. We would chase them back from the barricade, and then they would rush us and back we would go. After we had lost three men and Lieutenant May had got a slight wound, we got desperate and got out of the trench and rushed them for further orders. We fairly showered them as we followed them up, regardless of danger to ourselves. All this scrap through they hadn’t done anything with the machine guns. One was in our end of the trench, and we found that the other was out of commission. They must have been short of small-arm ammunition and bombs, because on that last strafing they cleared out and stayed.

===Chinese===
中尉一直等到说话声比先前更响了些——德国佬们显然正围着火堆拌什么嘴——这才一跃而起，大喊一声：“好了，小伙子们，一齐上！”

我们全体一跃而起，把手雷一齐送了出去。到弗里茨（Fritz，指德国兵）那边约有十五码，这对好投弹手来说不算事——我的人个个都是好手。壕里爆出一阵又惊又怕的嚎叫，他们撒腿就跑。我们散开腾出位置，又送过去一轮米尔斯手雷，跟着就冲。

这条壕其实压根算不上战壕。它原是条顶呱呱的好壕，可已经被砸得七零八落，如今只剩五六个弹坑，靠残破的隔段（traverse，挡墙分隔的壕段）连着。哪儿都不及齐腰高，有的地方只到膝盖。我们蜂拥冲进残壕，追着海尼们（Heinies，指德国兵）打。他们准有四十来个，没多会儿就摸清了我们不过十二个人，于是掉头反扑过来。我们占住一段形状还相对囫囵的弯隔段，抓起沙袋垒起一道街垒。沙袋四处有的是。

德国人朝我们甩过来一两颗手雷，外加相当密的步枪火力，我们撒腿躲到壕湾的拐角后面。接着便你来我往，成了场不折不扣的拉锯战：我们把他们从街垒前赶回去，他们又反扑上来，我们再退回去。折了三个人、梅中尉也挂了轻花之后，我们急红了眼，顾不得等候什么进一步的命令，翻出壕就朝他们猛压过去，追着屁股撵，手雷简直像下雹子一样泼过去，全然不顾自身的危险。这场混战自始至终，他们那两挺机枪一枪没放。一挺在我们占的壕段这头，另一挺我们发现早就打坏了。他们准是短了轻武器弹药和手雷，因为挨过最后那顿痛揍，他们就一溜烟跑了，再没回来。

===Original===
After the row was over we counted noses and found four dead and three slightly wounded, including Lieutenant May. I detailed two men to take the wounded and the Lieutenant back. That left four of us to consolidate the position. The Lieutenant promised to return with relief, but as it turned out he was worse than he thought, and he didn’t get back.

I turned to and inspected the position. It was pretty hopeless. There really wasn’t much to consolidate. The whole works was knocked about and was only fit for a temporary defence. There were about a dozen German dead, and we searched them but found nothing of value. So we strengthened our cross-trench barricade and waited for the relief. It never came.

When it began to get light, the place looked even more discouraging. There was little or no cover. We knew that unless we got some sort of concealment, the airplanes would spot us, and that we would get a shell or two. So we got out the entrenching tools and dug into the side of the best part of the shallow traverse. We finally got a slight overhang scraped out. We didn’t dare go very far under for fear that it would cave. We got some sandbags up on the sides and three of us crawled into the shelter. The other man made a similar place for himself a little distance off.

The day dawned clear and bright and gave promise of being hot. Along about seven we began to get hungry. A Tommy is always hungry, whether he is in danger or not. When we took account of stock and found that none of us had brought along “iron rations,” we discovered that we were all nearly starved. Killing is hungry work.

===Chinese===
这场热闹收场，我们清点人数：四个阵亡，三个轻伤，梅中尉也在伤号之列。我派两个人护送伤员和中尉回去。剩下我们四个巩固阵地。中尉答应带着接防部队回来，结果他的伤比他自己估的重，终究没能回来。

我着手查看阵地。希望相当渺茫。实在没什么可巩固的：整个工事被打得稀烂，只配应付一时的防守。约莫有十来具德国兵的尸体，我们搜了一遍，没发现什么有价值的东西。于是我们加固了横壕上的街垒，等接防部队。它始终没来。

天蒙蒙亮的时候，这地方瞧着更叫人泄气。几乎没有任何遮蔽。我们知道，不弄出点伪装，飞机就会发现我们，随后就得挨上一两发炮弹。于是我们掏出挖壕铲，朝那段最完好的浅隔段的侧壁里挖。最后刮出一线浅浅的掩檐。不敢往深里掏，怕塌。我们在洞口两侧支起沙袋，三个人爬进洞里。第四个人在隔开一段距离的地方，给自己挖了个类似的窝。

这一天晴空万里，亮堂堂的，看架势会很热。七点来钟，我们开始觉得饿。汤米（Tommy，英国兵的通称）什么时候都饿，危险不危险都一样。盘点行装，发现谁都没带“铁份口粮”（iron rations，应急干粮），我们这才发觉人人都快饿瘪了。杀人是桩饿肚子的营生。

===Original===
We had only ourselves to blame. We had been told repeatedly never to go anywhere without “iron rations,” but Tommy is a good deal of a child and unless you show him the immediate reason for a thing he is likely to disregard instructions. I rather blamed myself in this case for not seeing that the men had their emergency food. In fact, it was my duty to see that they had. But I had overlooked it. And I hadn’t brought any myself.

The “iron ration” consists of a pound of “bully beef,” a small tin containing tea and sugar enough for two doses, some Oxo cubes, and a few biscuits made of reinforced concrete. They are issued for just such an emergency as we were in as we lay in our isolated dugout. The soldier is apt to get into that sort of situation almost any time, and it is folly ever to be without the ration.

Well, we didn’t have ours, and we knew we wouldn’t get any before night, if we did then. One thing we had too much of. That was rum. The night before a bunch of us had been out on a ration party, and we had come across a Brigade Dump. This is a station where rations are left for the various companies to come and draw their own, also ammo and other necessities. There was no one about, and we had gone through the outfit. We found two cases of rum, four gallons in a case, and we promptly filled our bottles, more than a pint each.

Tommy is always very keen on his rum. The brand used in the army is high proof and burns like fire going down, but it is warming. The regular ration as served after a cold sentry go is called a “tot.” It is enough to keep the cold out and make a man wish he had another. The average Tommy will steal rum whenever he can without the danger of getting caught.

===Chinese===
这只能怪我们自己。上头三令五申，出门绝不能不带“铁份口粮”，可汤米或多或少是个孩子，不让他瞧见某桩事眼下的道理，他就容易把嘱咐当耳旁风。这一次，我没盯着弟兄们带足应急口粮，责任多半在我。说实话，盯着这事本就是我的职责。可我疏忽了。我自己也一点没带。

“铁份口粮”包括：一磅“咸牛肉”（bully beef），一小罐够冲两回的茶和糖，几块“奥克索”（Oxo）牛肉精块，还有几块钢筋混凝土做的饼干。这东西就是专为我们眼下这种处境——孤零零困在一个孤零零的掩蔽部里——预备的。当兵的什么时候都可能落到这步田地，身上没有这份口粮是糊涂透顶。

唉，我们偏偏没有，而且知道天黑前——就算到了天黑——也领不到。可有一样东西我们带多了：朗姆酒。头天夜里，我们一伙人出去干领粮的差事，撞见一个旅部物资分放站（Brigade Dump）——就是把各连的口粮、弹药和其他必需品囤在那儿、由各连自己来领的站子。四下没人，我们把那摊子东西翻了个遍。翻出两箱朗姆酒，一箱四加仑，我们当场把水壶灌得满满的，一人不止一品脱。

汤米对他的朗姆酒向来热心得很。军用那种度数高，咽下去像火烧，可是暖身子。寒夜站完一班哨（sentry go）照例发的那份定量叫“一份”（tot），刚够驱寒，又叫人直惦记下一份。寻常的汤米，只要不冒被抓的风险，逮着机会就要偷朗姆酒。

===Original===
It happened that all four of us were in the looting party and had our bottles full. Also it happened that we were all normally quite temperate and hadn’t touched our supply.

So we all took a nip and tightened up our belts. Then we took another and another. We lay on our backs with our heads out of the burrow, packed in like sardines and looking up at the sky. Half a dozen airplanes came out and flew over. We had had a hard night and we all dozed off, at least I did, and I guess the others did also.

Around nine we all waked up, and Bones﻿—he was the fellow in the middle﻿—began to complain of thirst. Then we all took another nip and wished it was water. We discussed the matter of crawling down to a muddy pool at the end of the traverse and having some out of that, but passed it up as there was a dead man lying in it. Bones, who was pretty well educated﻿—he once asked me if I had visited Emerson’s home and was astounded that I hadn’t﻿—quoted from Kipling something to the effect that,

===Chinese===
赶巧，我们四个都在那趟“打劫”的队伍里，水壶都是满的。更巧的是，我们四个平常都挺有节制，一直没动这份存货。

于是我们一人抿了一口，勒紧皮带。跟着又一口，又一口。我们仰面躺着，脑袋探出洞口，挤得像罐头里的沙丁鱼，望着天。半打飞机飞出来，从头顶掠过。熬了一宿，我们全打起了盹——至少我打盹了，我猜他们几个也一样。

九点来钟，我们都醒了，“骨头”（Bones）——就是睡当中位置的那位——开始嚷嚷口渴。于是我们又各抿一口，心里盼它是水。我们商量要不要爬到隔段尽头那汪泥塘里去喝几口，终究作罢——里面躺着一个死人。骨头这人受过挺好的教育——他有一回问我去没去过爱默生（Emerson）的故居，听说没去过，惊得目瞪口呆——他引了吉卜林（Kipling）的诗句，大意是：

===Original===
> When you come to slaughter
 

 You’ll do your work on water,
 

 An’ you’ll lick the bloomin’ boots of ’im that’s got it.

===Chinese===
> 等你上阵去厮杀，
 

 活计全得靠水来干，
 

 还得舔那家伙的靴子——谁叫水攥在他手里。

===Original===
Then Bones cursed the rum and took another nip. So did the rest of us.

There was a considerable bombardment going on all the forenoon, but few shells came anywhere near us. Some shrapnel burst over us a little way off to the right, and some of the fragments fell in the trench, but on the whole the morning was uncomfortable but not dangerous.

Around half-past ten we saw an airplane fight that was almost worth the forenoon’s discomfort. A lot of them had been circling around ever since daybreak. When the fight started, two of our planes were nearly over us. Suddenly we saw three Boche planes volplaning down from away up above. They grew bigger and bigger and opened with their guns when they were nearly on top of our fellows. No hits. Then all five started circling for top position. One of the Boches started to fall and came down spinning, but righted himself not more than a thousand feet up. Our anti aircraft guns opened on him, and we could see the shells bursting with little cottony puffs all around. Some of the shrapnel struck near us. They missed him, and up he went again. Presently all five came circling lower and lower, jockeying for position and spitting away with their guns. As they all got to the lower levels, the anti aircraft guns stopped firing, fearing to get our men.

===Chinese===
接着骨头咒骂了一句朗姆酒，又抿了一口。我们几个也照办。

整个上午，外头的炮击一直闹得不小，可没几发炮弹落到我们附近。一些榴霰弹在右边不远处的上空炸开，几块碎片落进壕里。不过总的说来，这个上午难受归难受，倒不危险。

十点半前后，我们看到一场空战，几乎抵得过我们受了一上午的罪。天一亮，飞机就在这一带兜圈子，多得很。开打的时候，我们有两架飞机差不多正在头顶上。突然，我们看见三架德国佬飞机从老高的天上滑旋而下，个头越变越大，眼看快压到我们那两架头顶上时开了火。没打中。随后五架一齐兜起圈子抢高位。一架德国佬飞机打着旋往下掉，跌到不足一千英尺才重新稳住。我们的高射炮朝它开火，看得见炮弹在它四周炸出一团团棉絮似的白烟，有些榴霰弹片就落在我们近旁。没打中它，它又蹿了上去。不多时，五架飞机越兜越低，抢着位置，机枪喷个不停。等它们全都降入低空，高射炮便停了火，生怕打着自己人。

===Original===
Suddenly one of the Huns burst into flames and came toppling down behind his lines, his gas tank ablaze. Almost immediately one of ours dropped, also burning and behind the Boche lines.

After that it was two to one, and the fight lasted more than ten minutes. Then down went a Hun, not afire but tumbling end over end behind our lines. I learned afterwards that this fellow was unhurt and was taken prisoner. That left it an even thing. We could see half a dozen planes rushing to attack the lone Boche. He saw them too. For he turned tail and skedaddled for home.

Bonesie began to philosophize on the cold-bloodedness of air fighting and really worked himself up into an almost optimistic frame of mind. He was right in the midst of a flowery oration on our comparative safety, “nestling on the bosom of Mother Earth,” when, without any warning whatever, there came a perfect avalanche of shell all around us.

I knew perfectly well that we were caught. The shells, as near as we could see, were coming from our side. Doubtless our people thought that the trench was still manned by Germans, and they were shelling for the big noon attack. Such an attack was made, as I learned afterwards, but I never saw it.

===Chinese===
突然，一架德国佬飞机腾地烧了起来，连滚带栽地落到自家防线后面，油箱烧成一团火。几乎同时，我们的一架也栽了下去，同样烧着，同样落在德国佬防线后头。

此后是二对一，斗了十多分钟。跟着又一架德国佬飞机栽下去，没起火，翻着跟头摔在我们防线后面。后来我听说，这家伙毫发无伤，还当了俘虏。这一下双方扯平。我们看见半打飞机蜂拥着去围攻那架落单的德国佬。他也瞧见了，掉转尾巴，一溜烟朝家跑了。

骨头仔（Bonesie）开始就空战的冷酷发表哲学宏论，还当真把自己说得几乎乐观起来。他正讲到最热闹处，大发花团锦簇的演说，说什么我们相对安全，“偎在大地母亲的怀抱里”——话音未落，一场炮弹的雪崩没有半点先兆，劈头盖脸砸到了我们四周。

我心里透亮：我们着了道儿。那些炮弹，看来看去都是从我们这边飞来的。不用问，自己人以为那条壕还在德国人手里，正为正午大进攻实施炮击。那一仗后来确实打了——这是我后来才知道的——可我没见着。

===Original===
At eleven o’clock I looked at my watch. Somehow I didn’t fear death, although I felt it was near. Maybe the rum was working. I turned to Bonesie and said, “What about that safety stuff, old top?”

“Cheer, cheer, Darby,” said he. “We may pull through yet.”

“Don’t think so,” I insisted. “It’s us for pushing up the daisies. Good luck if we don’t meet again!”

I put my hand in and patted Dinky on the back, and sent up another little prayer for luck. Then there was a terrific shock, and everything went black.

===Chinese===
十一点钟，我看了一眼表。不知怎么，我并不怕死，虽然觉出它就近在身边。也许是朗姆酒劲上来了。我扭头对骨头仔说：“老兄，你那套安全的高论还算不算数？”

“乐观点儿，乐观点儿，达比（Darby），”他说，“咱们兴许还能挺过去。”

“不这么想，”我坚持道，“咱们这回是铁定要进土里顶雏菊了。要是往后碰不着了，祝你好运！”

我把手伸进怀里，拍了拍丁奇（Dinky）的背，又偷偷做了个小祷告求运气。接着天崩地裂一震，眼前一黑。

===Original===
When I came out of it, I had the sensation of struggling up out of water. I thought for an instant that I was drowning. And in effect that was almost what was happening to me. I was buried, all but one side of my face. A tremendous weight pressed down on me, and I could only breathe in little gasps.

I tried to move my legs and arms and couldn’t. Then I wiggled my fingers and toes to see if any bones were broken. They wiggled all right. My right nostril and eye were full of dirt; also my mouth. I spit out the dirt and moved my head until my nose and eye were clear. I ached all over.

It was along toward sundown. Up aloft a single airplane was winging toward our lines. I remember that I wondered vaguely if he was the same fellow who had been fighting just before the world fell in on me.

I tried to sing out to the rest of the men, but the best I could do was a kind of loud gurgle. There was no answer. My head was humming, and the blood seemed to be bursting my ears. I was terribly sorry for myself and tried to pull my strength together for a big try at throwing the weight off my chest, but I was absolutely helpless. Then again I slid out of consciousness.

===Chinese===
缓过来的时候，我觉得自己正从水里往上挣扎。有一瞬间，我以为自己要淹死了。实际上，差一点就是这么回事。我被埋住了，只露出半边脸。千钧的分量压在身上，我只能一口一小口地倒气。

我想动动腿脚胳膊，动不了。又扭了扭手指脚趾，看断了骨头没有。都扭得动。右鼻孔和右眼全是土，嘴里也是。我把土啐出去，一点点挪动脑袋，直到鼻孔和眼睛透开。浑身没有一处不疼。

天色已近黄昏。高高处，一架飞机正朝我们防线飞去。我记得自己迷迷糊糊地琢磨：它是不是就是天塌下来之前打架的那一位。

我试着朝其他人喊，可竭尽全力，也只发出一种很响的咕噜声。没人应。脑袋嗡嗡直响，血仿佛要把耳朵胀破。我把自己怜惜得不行，拼命攒力气，想大干一场、把压在胸口的分量掀掉，可我实在一丝力气也使不上。随后，我又滑出了知觉。

===Original===
It was dark when I struggled up through the imaginary water again. I was still breathing in gasps, and I could feel my heart going in great thumps that hurt and seemed to shake the ground. My tongue was curled up and dry, and fever was simply burning me up. My mind was clear, and I wished that I hadn’t drunk that rum. Finding I could raise my head a little, I cocked it up, squinting over my cheek bones﻿—I was on my back﻿—and could catch the far-off flicker of the silver-green flare lights. There was a rattle of musketry off in the direction where the Boche lines ought to be. From behind came the constant boom of big guns. I lay back and watched the stars, which were bright and uncommonly low. Then a shell burst near by﻿—not near enough to hurt﻿—but buried as I was the whole earth seemed to shake. My heart stopped beating, and I went out again.

When I came to the next time, it was still dark, and somebody was lifting me on to a stretcher. My first impression was of getting a long breath. I gulped it down, and with every grateful inhalation I felt my ribs painfully snapping back into place. Oh, Lady! Didn’t I just eat that air up.

And then, having gotten filled up with the long-denied oxygen, I asked, “Where’s the others?”

===Chinese===
等我再度从那片想象中的水里挣扎上来，天已经黑透。我还在一口口倒气，觉得出心脏大咚大咚地撞，每撞一下都疼，仿佛震得地面直颤。舌头蜷着，干得像柴，热度简直要把人烧起来。脑子倒还清楚，直后悔喝了那朗姆酒。发觉脑袋能抬起一点了，我便——我仰面躺着——支起头，眯眼从颧骨上瞟出去，望得见远处银绿色信号弹的微光闪烁。德国佬防线该在的那个方向，传来步枪的噼啪声。背后大炮轰隆不断。我躺回去看星星，星星又亮，又低得出奇。后来一发炮弹在近旁炸了——没近到伤人——可我埋在土里，整个大地仿佛都晃了一晃。心跳停了，我又昏死过去。

再一回醒过来，天还黑着，有人正把我抬上担架。我的第一感觉是终于吸上了一口长气。我大口吞下去，每感激地吸进一口，都疼得觉得肋骨咔地一声弹回原处。哦，老天爷！那空气，我简直是一口一口吃下去的。

然后，等憋了许久才盼来的氧气灌满了胸腔，我问：“其他人在哪儿？”

===Original===
“Ayen’t no hothers,” was the brief reply.

And there weren’t. Later I reconstructed the occurrences of the night from what I was told by the rescuing party.

A big shell had slammed down on us, drilling Bonesie, the man in the middle, from end to end. He was demolished. The shell was a “dud,” that is, it didn’t explode. If it had, there wouldn’t have been anything whatever left of any of us. As it was our overhang caved in, letting sandbags and earth down on the remaining man and myself. The other man was buried clean under. He had life in him still when he was dug out but “went west” in about ten minutes.

The fourth man was found dead from shrapnel. I found, too, that the two unwounded men who had gone back with Lieutenant May had both been killed on the way in. So out of the twelve men who started on the “suicide club” stunt I was the only one left. Dinky was still inside my tunic, and I laid the luck all to him.

===Chinese===
“没有别的人喽，”回答简短。

确实没有了。后来，我根据救援队的人的讲述，把那天夜里的事一桩桩拼凑了出来。

一发大口径炮弹砸在我们头上，把睡当中的骨头仔从头到脚打了个对穿，整个人砸得稀烂。那发炮弹是颗“哑弹”（dud），就是说，没有炸。它要是炸了，我们几个连一根骨头渣都剩不下。饶是如此，我们的掩檐塌了，沙袋和泥土一股脑压在剩下那位弟兄和我身上。那位弟兄被埋了个严实，挖出来时还有口气，可约莫十分钟后就“上西天”了。

第四个人被发现死于榴霰弹片。我还得知，先前护送梅中尉回去的那两个没挂花的，也在回程路上双双毙命。当初干这桩“自杀俱乐部”行动的一共十二个人，到头来只剩我一个。丁奇还揣在我军上衣里，我把这份运气统统记在了他账上。

===Original===
Back in hospital I was found to be suffering from shell shock. Also my heart was pushed out of place. There were no bones broken, though I was sore all over, and several ribs were pulled around so that it was like a knife thrust at every breath. Besides that, my nerves were shattered. I jumped a foot at the slightest noise and twitched a good deal.

At the end of a week I asked the M.O. if I would get Blighty and he said he didn’t think so, not directly. He rather thought that they would keep me in hospital for a month or two and see how I came out. The officer was a Canadian and had a sense of humor and was most affable. I told him if this jamming wasn’t going to get me Blighty, I wanted to go back to duty and get a real one. He laughed and tagged me for a beach resort at Ault-Onival on the northern coast of France.

I was there a week and had a bully time. The place had been a fashionable watering place before the war, and when I was there the transient population was largely wealthy Belgians. They entertained a good deal and did all they could for the pleasure of the four thousand boys who were at the camp. The Y.M.C.A. had a huge tent and spread themselves in taking care of the soldiers. There were entertainments almost every night, moving pictures, and music. The food was awfully good and the beds comfortable, and that pretty nearly spells heaven to a man down from the front.

===Chinese===
送回医院一查，我得了弹震症。心脏也震离了位。骨头倒一根没断，虽然浑身酸痛，几根肋骨被拽得错了位，每吸一口气都像挨一刀。此外，我的神经垮了。最轻的响动都能吓得我蹦起一尺高，身上还一阵阵地抽搐。

满一星期的时候，我问军医（M.O.）我能不能捞到回布莱蒂（Blighty，英国兵对本土的叫法）的资格，他说看样子不行，至少不会直接回。他倒是觉得，医院会留我观察一两个月，看看结果怎样。这位军官是个加拿大人，有幽默感，和气得很。我告诉他，要是这回的“磕碰”换不来布莱蒂，我宁可回部队去，挣一处真格的。他哈哈大笑，提笔把我批到了法国北部海岸奥尔特-奥尼瓦尔（Ault-Onival）的一处海滨疗养地。

我在那儿待了一个星期，快活极了。这地方战前是个时髦的海滨浴场，我去的时候，暂住的人口大半是有钱的比利时人。他们三天两头设宴请客，想方设法让营地四千个小伙子开心。基督教青年会（Y.M.C.A.）支起一座大帐篷，使出浑身解数照料大兵。几乎夜夜有演出、电影和音乐。伙食好得不得了，床铺舒舒服服——对一个刚从前线下来的人来说，这差不多就拼出“天堂”两个字了。

===Original===
Best of all, the bathing was fine, and it was possible to keep the cooties under control﻿—more or less. I went in bathing two and three times daily as the sloping shore made it just as good at low tide as at high.

I think that glorious week at the beach made the hardships of the front just left behind almost worth while. My chum, Corporal Wells, who had a quaint Cockney philosophy, used to say that he liked to have the stomach ache because it felt so good when it stopped. On the same theory I became nearly convinced that a month in the trenches was good fun because it felt so good to get out.

At the end of the week I was better but still shaky. I started pestering the M.O. to tag me for Blighty. He wouldn’t, so I sprung the same proposition on him that I had on the doctor at the base﻿—to send me back to duty if he couldn’t send me to England. The brute took me at my word and sent me back to the battalion.

===Chinese===
最要紧的是，海水浴痛快，还能把虱子（cooties）控制住——多少控制住。海岸是缓坡，退潮下水和涨潮一样好，我一天下去泡两三回。

我想，海滩上那光辉的一星期，险些儿把刚刚抛在身后的前线苦日子都变成值当的了。我的挚友威尔斯下士有一套古怪的伦敦佬哲学，他常说，他喜欢闹胃疼，因为疼停下来的时候实在太舒坦了。本着同一条道理，我几乎相信，在战壕里蹲上一个月也是桩乐事——因为爬出来的那一刻实在太舒坦了。

星期满了我见好，可还是发虚。我开始缠着军医，求他批我去布莱蒂。他不批，我便把对后方医院那位大夫说过的一套原话搬出来——送不了英国，就送我回部队。这狠心货竟拿我的话当了真，把我打发回了营。

===Original===
I rejoined on the Somme again just as they were going back for the second time in that most awful part of the line. Many of the old faces were gone. Some had got the wooden cross, and some had gone to Blighty.

I sure was glad when old Wellsie hopped out and grabbed me.

“Gawd lumme, Darby,” he said. “Hi sye, an’ me thinkin’ as ’ow you was back in Blighty. An’ ’ere ye are yer blinkin’ old self. Or is it yer bloomin’ ghost. I awsks ye. Strike me pink, Yank. I’m glad.”

And he was. At that I did feel more or less ghostly. I seemed to have lost some of my confidence. I expected to “go west” on the next time in. And that’s a bad way to feel out there.

===Chinese===
我回索姆河归队的时候，他们正要第二回开进那段全线最吓人的地段。许多老面孔不见了。有的领了木十字架，有的去了布莱蒂。

老威尔西（Wellsie）蹦出来一把攥住我时，我是打心眼里高兴。

“我的老天爷，达比，”他说，“我跟你说，我还寻思你回布莱蒂享福去了呢。瞧你站在这儿，还是你那副老德行——要不，就是你那该死的鬼魂回来了？我问你。真是活见鬼，美国佬。我太高兴啦。”

他确实高兴。话说回来，我自己也确实觉得有几分像鬼魂。我好像丢了些底气。我料定下一回进壕就得“上西天”。在前线那种地方，抱着这路心气儿可不是好事。

## I Joining the British Army / 一、投效英军

===Original===
Once, on the Somme in the fall of 1916, when I had been over the top and was being carried back somewhat disfigured but still in the ring, a cockney stretcher bearer shot this question at me:

“Hi sye, Yank. Wot th’ bloody ’ell are you in this bloomin’ row for? Ayen’t there no trouble t’ ’ome?”

And for the life of me I couldn’t answer. After more than a year in the British service I could not, on the spur of the moment, say exactly why I was there.

To be perfectly frank with myself and with the reader I had no very lofty motives when I took the King’s shilling. When the great war broke out, I was mildly sympathetic with England, and mighty sorry in an indefinite way for France and Belgium; but my sympathies were not strong enough in any direction to get me into uniform with a chance of being killed. Nor, at first, was I able to work up any compelling hate for Germany. The abstract idea of democracy did not figure in my calculations at all.

===Chinese===
1916年秋天，在索姆河（Somme）战场上，有一次我翻出壕顶冲锋（going over the top）之后，正被人往回抬——模样破了点相，好歹还没出局——一个伦敦佬（cockney）担架员冲我劈头扔过来一个问题：

“喂，我说，美国佬（Yank）。你到底为啥跑来掺和这场该死的混仗？家里就寻不着一点儿麻烦事儿么？”

可我打死也答不上来。在英军里当了一年多的差，一时之间，我竟说不准自己究竟为什么会在这里。

对自己也对读者掏句实话：当初领“国王的先令”（King's shilling，应征入伍的代称）时，我压根没有什么崇高的动机。大战爆发那会儿，我对英国怀着淡淡的同情，对法国和比利时也隐隐约约地难过，可这点同情无论朝哪个方向，都还不够浓烈到让我穿上军装、把性命押上去。起初，我对德国也恨不出个所以然。“民主”这么个抽象念头，更是压根没进过我的盘算。

===Original===
However, as the war went on, it became apparent to me, as I suppose it must have to everybody, that the world was going through one of its epochal upheavals; and I figured that with so much history in the making, any unattached young man would be missing it if he did not take a part in the big game.

I had the fondness for adventure usual in young men. I liked to see the wheels go round. And so it happened that, when the war was about a year and a half old, I decided to get in before it was too late.

On second thought I won’t say that it was purely love for adventure that took me across. There may have been in the back of my head a sneaking extra fondness for France, perhaps instinctive, for I was born in Paris, although my parents were American and I was brought to Boston as a baby and have lived here since.

Whatever my motives for joining the British army, they didn’t have time to crystallize until I had been wounded and sent to Blighty, which is trench slang for England. While recuperating in one of the pleasant places of the English countryside, I had time to acquire a perspective and to discover that I had been fighting for democracy and the future safety of the world. I think that my experience in this respect is like that of most of the young Americans who have volunteered for service under a foreign flag.

===Chinese===
不过，随着战事推进，我——想必所有人也一样——越来越看得明白：世界正经历一场划时代的大动荡。我盘算着，这么多历史正在眼皮子底下写就，一个无牵无挂的年轻人要是不到这场大博弈里插一脚，可就白白错过了。

我身上带着年轻人常有的那股子冒险劲，爱瞧着大轮子转、凑那份热闹。于是，开战大约一年半的时候，我决定趁还来得及，赶紧入场。

不过再一想，我又不肯说，把我拽过洋去的是纯粹的冒险瘾。我脑子背后兴许还藏着一丝对法国说不清的偏爱，也许是天性使然——因为我生在巴黎，虽说父母都是美国人，我还是个奶娃娃的时候就被带到了波士顿，打那以后一直住在这儿。

不管我投英军的动机究竟是什么，它们都来不及凝成个样子——直到我负了伤、被送回“布莱蒂”（Blighty，战壕俚语，指英国本土），才算有了工夫。在英格兰乡间一处怡人的地方养伤时，我有了从容的视角，这才发现：原来我一直在为民主、为世界未来的安全而战。我想，在这一层上，我的经历跟大多数志愿到外国旗帜下服役的美国青年并无二致。

===Original===
I decided to get into the big war game early in 1916. My first thought was to go into the ambulance service, as I knew several men in that work. One of them described the driver’s life about as follows. He said:

“The *blessés* curse you because you jolt them. The doctors curse you because you don’t get the *blessés* in fast enough. The Transport Service curse you because you get in the way. You eat standing up and don’t sleep at all. You’re as likely as anybody to get killed, and all the glory you get is the War Cross, if you’re lucky, and you don’t get a single chance to kill a Hun.”

That settled the ambulance for me. I hadn’t wanted particularly to kill a Hun until it was suggested that I mightn’t. Then I wanted to slaughter a whole division.

So I decided on something where there would be fighting. And having decided, I thought I would “go the whole hog” and work my way across to England on a horse transport.

===Chinese===
1916年初，我打定主意投身这场大战局。头一个念头是进救护队，因为那一行里我认识好几个人。其中一个把司机的生活大致描绘成这样。他说：

“伤兵（blessés，法语）骂你，嫌你把他们颠着了。医生骂你，嫌你把伤兵送得不够快。运输队骂你，嫌你挡道。你吃饭站着吃，睡觉压根儿没得睡。挨枪子儿的概率跟谁都一样，到头来能捞到的全部荣耀，运气好不过一枚战争十字勋章（War Cross），而杀德国佬（Hun）的机会，一个都轮不着你。”

这一下，救护队对我来说就算了结了。本来我也没怎么特别想杀德国佬，可一听人家说我兴许没机会杀，我顿时就想屠他一个整师。

于是我选了个有仗打的行当。既然定了，索性“一竿子干到底”，在一条运马船上谋了个差事，一路打工渡到英国去。

===Original===
One day in the first part of February I went, at what seemed an early hour, to an office on Commercial Street, Boston, where they were advertising for horse tenders for England. About three hundred men were earlier than I. It seemed as though every beachcomber and patriot in New England was trying to get across. I didn’t get the job, but filed my application and was lucky enough to be signed on for a sailing on February 22 on the steamship *Cambrian*, bound for London.

We spent the morning of Washington’s Birthday loading the horses. These government animals were selected stock and full of ginger. They seemed to know that they were going to France and resented it keenly. Those in my care seemed to regard my attentions as a personal affront.

We had a strenuous forenoon getting the horses aboard, and sailed at noon. After we had herded in the livestock, some of the officers herded up the herders. I drew a pink slip with two numbers on it, one showing the compartment where I was supposed to sleep, the other indicating my bunk.

===Chinese===
二月初的一天，我赶了个自以为很早的钟点，跑到波士顿商业街（Commercial Street）一处正在招募赴英押马员的办公室。比我更早的已经有三百来号人。新英格兰的海边游民和爱国志士，那阵子像是全在削尖脑袋往对岸钻。当天的活儿没轮上我，不过我递了申请，运气不错，被签上了2月22日启航、开往伦敦的“坎布里亚号”（Cambrian）轮船。

华盛顿诞辰日那天上午，我们全用来装马。这些政府牲口都是精选的好种，满身劲头。它们仿佛知道自己要去法国，为此愤懑不已。归我照管的那几匹，似乎把我的殷勤伺候当成了对它们的人身冒犯。

把马弄上船折腾了整整一个上午，晌午开船。牲口都圈进舱之后，几位军官又来圈拢我们这群管牲口的。我抽到一张粉红色纸条，上头印着两个号码：一个标明我该睡哪间舱，一个指着我的铺位。

===Original===
That compartment certainly was a glory-hole. Most of the men had been drunk the night before, and the place had the rich, balmy fragrance of a waterfront saloon. Incidentally there was a good deal of unauthorized and undomesticated livestock. I made a limited acquaintance with that pretty, playful little creature, the “cootie,” who was to become so familiar in the trenches later on. He wasn’t called a cootie aboard ship, but he was the same bird.

Perhaps the less said about that trip across the better. It lasted twenty-one days. We fed the animals three times a day and cleaned the stalls once on the trip. I got chewed up some and stepped on a few times. Altogether the experience was good intensive training for the trench life to come; especially the bunks. Those sleeping quarters sure were close and crawly.

We landed in London on Saturday night about nine thirty. The immigration inspectors gave us a quick examination and we were turned back to the shipping people, who paid us off﻿—two pounds, equal to about ten dollars real change.

===Chinese===
那间舱房可真是个藏污纳垢的杂物窝。头天夜里大多数人都喝得烂醉，满舱飘着码头酒馆那种醇厚的“香风”。顺带一提，舱里还活跃着相当一批未经批准、未加驯化的“牲口”。我跟那种漂亮又顽皮的小生灵——“虱子”（cootie）打了个照面——日后在战壕里，它还要跟我亲上加亲。在船上它不叫虱子这个名号，可反正是同一路货。

这趟跨洋航程，恐怕还是少说为妙。整整走了二十一天。我们一天喂三遍牲口，全程打扫了一次畜栏。我被啃了几口，也挨了几蹄子。总的说来，这番经历算是为日后的战壕生活做了不错的强化训练，尤其是那些铺位——那睡觉的地方，真心又挤又“会爬”。

星期六晚上九点半前后，我们在伦敦靠了岸。移民检查官草草查了一遍，把我们退回给船公司的人，由他们结清工钱——两英镑，折合真金白银差不多十美元。

===Original===
After that we rode on the train half an hour and then marched through the streets, darkened to fool the Zeps. Around one o’clock we brought up at Thrawl Street, at the lodgings where we were supposed to stop until we were started for home.

The place where we were quartered was a typical London doss house. There were forty beds in the room with mine, all of them occupied. All hands were snoring, and the fellow in the next cot was going it with the cutout wide open, breaking all records. Most of the beds sagged like a hammock. Mine humped up in the middle like a pile of bricks.

I was up early and was directed to the place across the way where we were to eat. It was labeled “Mother Wolf’s. The Universal Provider.” She provided just one meal of weak tea, moldy bread, and rancid bacon for me. After that I went to a hotel. I may remark in passing that horse tenders, going or coming or in between whiles, do not live on the fat of the land.

I spent the day﻿—it was Sunday﻿—seeing the sights of Whitechapel, Middlesex Street or Petticoat Lane, and some of the slums. Next morning it was pretty clear to me that two pounds don’t go far in the big town. I promptly boarded the first bus for Trafalgar Square. The recruiting office was just down the road in Whitehall at the old Scotland Yard office.

===Chinese===
随后我们坐了半小时火车，又列队穿过一条条街道——满城灯火管制，为的是糊弄齐柏林飞艇（Zeps）。凌晨一点上下，我们到了思罗尔街（Thrawl Street）的宿处，按安排要一直住到启程回家。

我们落脚的地方是家地道的伦敦下等客栈（doss house）。我那间屋里摆了四十张床，张张有人。满屋鼾声齐鸣，隔壁铺那位老兄更是鼾声全开，像把消音器的旁通阀推到了底，一举打破全部纪录。大多数床中间塌陷得像吊床，我那张偏偏中间鼓起，活像一堆砖头。

我起了个大早，被人指到马路对面我们该用饭的地方。招牌上写着“狼妈妈，万有供应商”。她供给我统共一顿饭：寡淡的茶、发霉的面包、哈喇了的熏肉。那顿之后我就投宿旅馆去了。顺带说一句，干押马这一行的，来也好，去也好，来去之间也好，都吃不上什么好的。

这一天是星期天，我拿它逛了白教堂区（Whitechapel）、米德尔塞克斯街（Middlesex Street，又叫佩蒂科特巷 Petticoat Lane），还看了几处贫民窟。第二天一早我就看明白了：两英镑在大城市撑不了几天。我当机立断跳上头一班公共汽车，直奔特拉法加广场（Trafalgar Square）。征兵办公室就在白厅（Whitehall）那条街往前一点，用的是苏格兰场（Scotland Yard）的旧办公室。

===Original===
I had an idea when I entered that recruiting office that the sergeant would receive me with open arms. He didn’t. Instead he looked me over with unqualified scorn and spat out, “Yank, ayen’t ye?”

And I in my innocence briefly answered, “Yep.”

“We ayen’t tykin’ no nootrals,” he said, with a sneer. And then: “Better go back to Hamerika and ’elp Wilson write ’is blinkin’ notes.”

Well, I was mad enough to poke that sergeant in the eye. But I didn’t. I retired gracefully and with dignity.

===Chinese===
走进那间征兵办公室之前，我还当那位军士会张开双臂欢迎我。他才不呢。他把我从头到脚打量了一遍，满眼不加掩饰的鄙夷，啐出一句：“美国佬，是不是？”

而我天真无知，简短利落地答了声：“是。”

“咱不收中立国的，”他冷笑一声，又补一句：“趁早回你的美国去，帮威尔逊（Wilson）写他那些倒霉照会吧。”

唉，我当时气得真想往那军士眼睛上擂一拳。可我到底没动手，而是体体面面、不失尊严地退了出去。

===Original===
At the door another sergeant hailed me, whispering behind his hand, “Hi sye, mytie. Come around in the mornin’. Hi’ll get ye in.” And so it happened.

Next day my man was waiting and marched me boldly up to the same chap who had refused me the day before.

“ ’Ere’s a recroot for ye, Jim,” says my friend.

===Chinese===
门口另一位军士招呼住我，掩着嘴悄声说：“喂，伙计，明儿早上再过来，我给你弄进去。”后来事情果然如此。

第二天，我那位贵人果然候着，大摇大摆把我领到前一天拒收过我的那位老兄跟前。

“吉姆（Jim），给你招来个新兵，”我那朋友说。

===Original===
Jim never batted an eye. He began to “awsk” questions and to fill out a blank. When he got to the birthplace, my guide cut in and said, “Canada.”

The only place I knew in Canada was Campobello Island, a place where we camped one summer, and I gave that. I don’t think that anything but rabbits was ever born on Campobello, but it went. For that matter anything went. I discovered afterward that the sergeant who had captured me on the street got five bob (shillings) for me.

The physical examination upstairs was elaborate. They told me to strip, weighed me, and said I was fit. After that I was taken in to an officer﻿—a real officer this time﻿—who made me put my hand on a Bible and say yes to an oath he rattled off. Then he told me I was a member of the Royal Fusiliers, gave me two shillings, sixpence and ordered me to report at the Horse Guards Parade next day. I was in the British army﻿—just like that!

I spent the balance of the day seeing the sights of London, and incidentally spending my coin. When I went around to the Horse Guards next morning, two hundred others, new rookies like myself, were waiting. An officer gave me another two shillings, sixpence. I began to think that if the money kept coming along at that rate the British army might turn out a good investment. It didn’t.

===Chinese===
吉姆眼皮都没眨一下。他开始“阿斯克”（awsk，伦敦腔的 ask）起问题来，往一张空白表格上填。填到出生地一栏，我那位引路人插了句嘴：“加拿大。”

加拿大我唯一叫得上名的地方是坎波贝洛岛（Campobello Island），有一年夏天我们全家去那儿露过营，我就报了它。依我看，坎波贝洛岛上除了兔子，从没出生过别的活物，可这一条居然也过了关。说起来，那阵子报什么都过得关。后来我才知道，在街上把我“俘获”的那位军士，凭我领了五 bob（先令）的赏钱。

楼上的体格检查办得挺考究。他们叫我脱光衣服，过了秤，说我合格。随后我被带去见一位军官——这回是位如假包换的军官——他让我把手按在《圣经》上，对他一口气念完的誓词答“是”。然后他告诉我，我已经是皇家燧发枪团（Royal Fusiliers）的一员，发给我两先令六便士，命令我第二天去王室骑兵卫队阅兵场（Horse Guards Parade）报到。我这就进了英国陆军——就这么回事！

这一天剩下的工夫，我都拿去逛伦敦的名胜，顺带花我的钱。第二天早上我转到王室骑兵卫队时，另外两百个跟我一样的新兵蛋子已经在等着了。一位军官又发给我两先令六便士。我开始琢磨，要是钱照这个势头进账，投效英国陆军说不定是笔好买卖。结果不是。

===Original===
That morning I was sent out to Hounslow Barracks, and three days later was transferred to Dover with twenty others. I was at Dover a little more than two months and completed my training there.

Our barracks at Dover was on the heights of the cliffs, and on clear days we could look across the Channel and see the dim outlines of France. It was a fascination for all of us to look away over there and to wonder what fortunes were to come to us on the battle fields of Europe. It was perhaps as well that none of us had imagination enough to visualize the things that were ahead.

I found the rookies at Dover a jolly, companionable lot, and I never found the routine irksome. We were up at five thirty, had cocoa and biscuits, and then an hour of physical drill or bayonet practice. At eight came breakfast of tea, bacon, and bread, and then we drilled until twelve. Dinner. Out again on the parade ground until three thirty. After that we were free.

Nights we would go into Dover and sit around the “pubs” drinking ale, or “ayle” as the cockney says it.

===Chinese===
那天上午我被发往霍恩斯洛兵营（Hounslow Barracks），三天后跟另外二十个人一道调去了多佛（Dover）。我在多佛待了两个多月，在那儿完成了全部训练。

我们在多佛的兵营坐落在崖顶高处，天晴的日子，隔海能望见法兰西朦胧的轮廓。遥遥眺望对岸，揣度欧洲战场将带给我们什么样的命运，对我们每个人都有种莫名的魔力。好在我们谁也没那份想象力，能把前头的光景看得真真切切。

我觉得多佛的新兵是一伙乐呵呵、好相处的家伙，而操练的日常我从不觉得难熬。五点半起床，可可就饼干，然后一小时体操或刺杀训练。八点开早饭：茶、熏肉、面包，随后操练到十二点。吃正餐。下午再上操场，练到三点半。之后就自由了。

到了夜里，我们就进多佛城，围着“酒馆”坐坐，灌麦芽酒——照伦敦佬的叫法，是“艾尔”（ayle）。

===Original===
After a few weeks, when we were hardened somewhat, they began to inflict us with the torture known as “night ’ops.” That means going out at ten o’clock under full pack, hiking several miles, and then “manning” the trenches around the town and returning to barracks at three a.m.

This wouldn’t have been so bad if we had been excused parades the following day. But no. We had the same old drills except the early one, but were allowed to “kip” until seven.

In the two months I completed the musketry course, was a good bayonet man, and was well grounded in bombing practice. Besides that I was as hard as nails and had learned thoroughly the system of British discipline.

===Chinese===
过了几个星期，等我们筋骨磨硬了些，他们开始对我们施加一种号称“夜间演习”（night 'ops）的酷刑。具体是：十点钟全副行装出动，行军好几英里，然后“据守”城郊的堑壕，凌晨三点返回兵营。

这本不算太难受，只要第二天免了操就行。可没门儿。除了早操，一应操练照旧，仅仅恩准我们“补觉”（kip）睡到七点。

这两个月里，我修完了步枪射击课程，练成一手好刺刀，投弹训练也打下了扎实的底子。除此之外，我浑身筋骨硬得像铁打的，还把英式纪律那一套学了个通透。

===Original===
I had supposed that it took at least six months to make a soldier﻿—in fact had been told that one could not be turned out who would be ten percent efficient in less than that time. That old theory is all wrong. Modern warfare changes so fast that the only thing that can be taught a man is the basic principles of discipline, bombing, trench warfare, and musketry. Give him those things, a well-conditioned body, and a baptism of fire, and he will be right there with the veterans, doing his bit.

Two months was all our crowd got at any rate, and they were as good as the best, if I do say it.

My training ended abruptly with a furlough of five days for Embarkation Leave, that is, leave before going to France. This is a sort of goodbye vacation. Most fellows realize fully that it may be their last look at Blighty, and they take it rather solemnly. To a stranger without friends in England I can imagine that this Embarkation Leave would be either a mighty lonesome, dismal affair, or a stretch of desperate, homesick dissipation. A chap does want to say goodbye to someone before he goes away, perhaps to die. He wants to be loved and to have someone sorry that he is going.

===Chinese===
我原以为造就一个士兵至少得六个月——实际上还有人告诉过我，不出这个年头，练出来的兵连一成的战斗力都谈不上。那套老理论完全不对。现代战争变化太快，能教给人的只有纪律、投弹、堑壕战和枪法的基本功。把这些教给他，再给他一副结实的身板、一场战火的洗礼，他就能跟老兵们并肩而立，出自己的一份力。

反正我们这批人统共只得了两个月的训练，而恕我自夸一句，他们跟最拔尖的比也不逊色。

我的训练戛然而止，换来五天“登船假”（Embarkation Leave），也就是开赴法国前的假期。这是一种告别性质的休假。多数弟兄心里透亮：这也许是他们最后一眼看看布莱蒂，所以都过得相当郑重。对一个在英格兰举目无亲的外乡人，我可以想象，这登船假要么是一段冷清凄惶的日子，要么是一段想家想得发疯、破罐破摔的放纵岁月。一个人远行之前——也许是赴死之前——总归想跟什么人道个别。他盼着有人爱他，盼着有人为他的离去而难过。

===Original===
I was invited by one of my chums to spend the leave with him at his home in Southall, Middlesex. His father, mother and sister welcomed me in a way that made me know it was my home from the minute I entered the door. They took me into their hearts with a simple hospitality and whole-souled kindness that I can never forget. I was a stranger in a strange land and they made me one of their own. I shall never be able to repay all the loving thoughts and deeds of that family and shall remember them while I live. My chum’s mother I call Mother too. It is to her that I have dedicated this book.

After my delightful few days of leave, things moved fast. I was back in Dover just two days when I, with two hundred other men, was sent to Winchester. Here we were notified that we were transferred to the Queen’s Royal West Surrey Regiment.

This news brought a wild howl from the men. They wanted to stop with the Fusiliers. It is part of the British system that every man is taught the traditions and history of his regiment and to *know* that his is absolutely the best in the whole army. In a surprisingly short time they get so they swear by their own regiment and by their officers, and they protest bitterly at a transfer.

===Chinese===
一位好友邀我去他家共度假期——他家在米德尔塞克斯郡（Middlesex）的绍索尔（Southall）。他的父亲、母亲和妹妹迎接我的那副热络劲儿，让我一进门就知道：这里就是我的家。他们以朴实的殷勤和全心全意的善意把我接纳进心里，叫我终生难忘。我身在异乡为异客，他们却把我当成了自家人。那一家人所有的关爱，我这辈子都报答不尽，只要活着，就会记着他们。战友的母亲，我也唤她妈妈。这本书，就是献给她的。

愉快的几天假期过后，事情骤然提速。我回到多佛才两天，就跟另外两百人一道被送往温彻斯特（Winchester）。在那儿我们接到通知：全员转调女王皇家西萨里团（Queen's Royal West Surrey Regiment）。

这消息在弟兄们中间激起一片狂嚎。他们想留在燧发枪团。英军的制度里有这么一条：每个兵都要学习本团的传统和历史，并且*确信*自己这个团绝对是全军之冠。用不了多久——快得惊人——他们就会把自己的团、把团里的军官奉若神明，于是一听说要调动，就怨气冲天。

===Original===
Personally I didn’t care a rap. I had early made up my mind that I was a very small pebble on the beach and that it was up to me to obey orders and keep my mouth shut.

On June 17, some eighteen hundred of us were moved down to Southampton and put aboard the transport for Havre. The next day we were in France, at Harfleur, the central training camp outside Havre.

We were supposed to undergo an intensive training at Harfleur in the various forms of gas and protection from it, barbed wire and methods of construction of entanglements, musketry, bombing, and bayonet fighting.

Harfleur was a miserable place. They refused to let us go in town after drill. Also I managed to let myself in for something that would have kept me in camp if town leave had been allowed.

===Chinese===
就我自己来说，半点儿不在乎。我早想明白了：自己不过是滩上一颗顶小的石子，本分就是服从命令、闭上嘴巴。

6月17日，我们大约一千八百人被送到南安普敦（Southampton），登上开赴勒阿弗尔（Havre）的运输船。第二天我们已到了法国，落脚在勒阿弗尔城外的中枢训练营——阿夫勒尔（Harfleur）。

按计划，我们要在阿夫勒尔接受强化训练，科目包括：各式毒气及其防护，铁丝网与障碍物构筑的种种方法，枪法、投弹和拼刺。

阿夫勒尔是个糟糕透顶的地方。操练之余也不放我们进城。另外，我还自己招揽了一桩差事——倘若那阵子真准了进城假，这桩差事本会把我拴在营里。

===Original===
The first day there was a call for a volunteer for musketry instructor. I had qualified and jumped at it. When I reported, an old Scotch sergeant told me to go to the quartermaster for equipment. I said I already had full equipment. Whereupon the sergeant laughed a rumbling Scotch laugh and told me I had to go into kilts, as I was assigned to a Highland contingent.

I protested with violence and enthusiasm, but it didn’t do any good. They gave me a dinky little pleated petticoat, and when I demanded breeks to wear underneath, I got the merry ha ha. Breeks on a Scotchman? Never!

Well, I got into the fool things, and I felt as though I was naked from ankle to wishbone. I couldn’t get used to the outfit. I am naturally a modest man. Besides, my architecture was never intended for bare-leg effects. I have no dimples in my knees.

So I began an immediate campaign for transfer back to the Surreys. I got it at the end of ten days, and with it came a hurry call from somewhere at the front for more troops.

===Chinese===
头一天，贴出告示招募一名志愿担任步枪射击教官的人。我正够格，一口应承。前去报到时，一位苏格兰老军士打发我去找军需官领装备。我说我装备已然齐整。那位军士闻言发出一阵滚雷似的苏格兰式大笑，说我必须改穿苏格兰裙（kilts），因为我被分到了一支高地部队。

我又激烈又起劲地抗议了一通，毫无用处。他们发给我一条皱皱巴巴的百褶小裙子，我要求再配一条衬里穿的裤子（breeks），换来一阵幸灾乐祸的哈哈大笑。苏格兰人穿裤子？门儿都没有！

唉，我还是套上了那件劳什子，顿觉自己从脚脖子到锁骨窝寸缕不着。这身行头我怎么也习惯不了。我天生是个腼腆人。何况我这副骨架子压根不是玩光腿造型的料——我膝盖上可长不出酒窝。

于是我立刻发动攻势，争取调回萨里团（the Surreys）。第十天头上，调令批了下来——随调令一道来的，还有前线某处催要补充兵员的十万火急令。

## List of Illustrations / 插图目录

===Original===
1. Corporal Holmes in the uniform of the 22nd London Battalion, Queen’s Royal West Surrey Regiment, H.M. Imperial Army.
2. Reduced facsimile of discharge certificate of character.
3. A heavy howitzer, under camouflage.
4. Over the top on a raid.
5. Cooking under difficulties.
6. Head-on view of a British tank.
7. Corporal Holmes with staff nurse and another patient, at Fulham Military Hospital, London, S.W.
8. Corporal Holmes with company office force, at Winchester, England, a week prior to discharge.

===Chinese===
1. 霍尔姆斯下士，身着英王陛下帝国陆军女王皇家西萨里团第22伦敦营制服。
2. 退役品格证明书缩小摹本。
3. 一门重型榴弹炮，加着伪装。
4. 翻出壕顶执行突袭。
5. 在艰难条件下做饭。
6. 一辆英军坦克的正面图。
7. 霍尔姆斯下士与一位科班护士及另一位伤病员，摄于伦敦西南区富勒姆军医院。
8. 霍尔姆斯下士与连部文书班同仁，摄于英国温彻斯特，退役前一周。

## VIII On the Go / 八、转战途中

===Original===
> Marching, marching, marching,
 

 Always ruddy well marching.
 

 Marching all the morning,
 

 And marching all the night.
 

 Marching, marching, marching,
 

 Always ruddy well marching,
 

 Roll on till my time is up
 

 And I shall march no more.

We sung it to the tune of “Holy, Holy, Holy,” the whole blooming battalion. As we swung down the Boulevard Alsace-Lorraine in Amiens and passed the great cathedral up there to the left, on its little rise of ground, the chant lifted and lilted and throbbed up from near a thousand throats, much as the unisoned devotions of the olden monks must have done in other days.

Ours was a holy cause, but despite the association of the tune the song was far from being a holy song. It was, rather, a chanted remonstrance against all hiking and against this one in particular.

After our service at Vimy Ridge someone in authority somewhere decided that the 22nd Battalion and two others were not quite good enough for really smart work. We were, indeed, hard. But not hard enough. So some superior intellect squatting somewhere in the safety of the rear, with a finger on the pulse of the army, decreed that we were to get not only hard but tough; and to that end we were to hike. Hike we did.
===Chinese===
> 行军，行军，行军，
 

 老是没完没了地行军。
 

 一早上都在行军，
 

 一整夜还在行军。
 

 行军，行军，行军，
 

 老是没完没了地行军，
 

 熬啊，熬到服役期满，
 

 我从此再不用行军。

我们全营这帮小子，拿《圣哉，圣哉，圣哉》（“Holy, Holy, Holy”）的调子唱它。当我们甩开步子走下亚眠（Amiens）的阿尔萨斯-洛林大道，打左边那座高踞小土冈的大教堂前经过时，这支歌从近千条喉咙里升腾起来，抑扬顿挫、嗡嗡震颤，活像旧时修士齐声祷告——当年的虔诚，想必就是这般光景。

我们的事业是神圣的，可尽管调子沾着圣诗的边，这首歌却半点也不神圣。毋宁说，它是一曲对一切行军的诵唱抗议——尤其针对眼下这一趟。

维米岭的差事打完之后，不知哪儿的哪位权威人士断定，第22营（22nd Battalion）和另外两个营还不够利索，干不了真正漂亮的活儿。我们确实够硬了。但还不够硬。于是，某位蹲在安稳后方、一根手指搭着全军脉搏的高级脑袋发下谕旨：我们不只要硬，还要韧；为达此目的，得行军。行军，我们真是行了。

===Original===
For more than three weeks we went from place to place with no apparent destination, wandering aimlessly up and down the countryside of Northern France, imposing ourselves upon the people of little villages, shamming battle over their cultivated fields, and sleeping in their hen coops.

I kept a diary on that hike. It was a thing forbidden, but I managed it. One manages many things out there. I have just read over that diary. There isn’t much to it but a succession of town names﻿—Villiers du Bois, Maisincourt, Barly, Oneaux, Canchy, Amiens, Bourdon, Villiers Bocage, Agenvilliers, Behencourt, and others that I failed to set down and have forgotten. We swept across that country, sweating under our packs, hardening our muscles, stopping here for a day, there for five days for extended-order drills and bayonet and musketry practice, and somewhere else for a sham battle. We were getting ready to go into the Somme.

The weather, by some perversity of fate, was fair during all of that hiking time. Whenever I was in the trenches it always rained, whether the season warranted it or not. Except on days when we were scheduled to go over the top. Then, probably because rain will sometimes hold up a planned-for attack, it was always fair.

On the hike, with good roads under foot, the soldier does not mind a little wet and welcomes a lot of clouds. No such luck for us. It was clear all the time. Not only clear but blazing hot August weather.
===Chinese===
三个多星期里，我们一处又一处地转，看不出个明确的目的地，在法兰西北部的乡野间漫无目的地荡来荡去，硬闯进小村庄的百姓家中，在他们耕种的田地里假打仗，在他们的鸡窝里过夜。

那趟行军我记了日记。这是犯禁的事，可我还是办到了。在前线，人总能办成许多事。我刚把那本日记重读了一遍。里面没多少东西，只有一长串地名——维利耶迪布瓦（Villiers du Bois）、梅桑库尔（Maisincourt）、巴尔利（Barly）、奥诺（Oneaux）、康希（Canchy）、亚眠、布尔东（Bourdon）、维利耶波卡日（Villiers Bocage）、阿让维利耶（Agenvillers）、贝昂库尔（Behencourt），还有些我没记下、如今忘了的。我们扫过那片乡野，背着背囊流汗，把筋肉练得更硬，在这儿停一天，在那儿停五天搞疏开队形操练、刺杀和射击练习，再换个地方打一场假仗。我们这是在为开进索姆河做准备。

也不知是命运存心别扭，那趟行军从头到尾天气晴好。而每逢我住战壕，天必定下雨，不管时节合不合。只有定好翻出壕顶冲锋的日子例外——大概是老天也知道，下雨有时会搅黄一场筹划好的进攻，那些日子天总晴着。

行军路上，只要脚下是好路，当兵的不在乎下点小雨，反倒巴望天上多堆云彩。我们没这福气。天一直晴着。不但晴，还是八月里毒日当空的酷热天。

===Original===
On our first march out of the Cabaret Rouge communication trench we covered a matter of ten miles to a place called Villiers du Bois. Before that I had never fully realized just what it meant to go it in full heavy equipment.

Often on the march I compared my lot with that of the medieval soldier who had done his fighting over these same fields of Northern France.

The knight of the Middle Ages was all dressed up like a hardware store with, I should judge, about a hundred pounds of armor. But he rode a horse and had a squire or some such striker trailing along in the rear with the things to make him comfortable, when the fighting was over.

The modern soldier gets very little help in his war making. He is, in fact, more likely to be helping somebody else than asking for assistance for himself. The soldier has two basic functions: first, to keep himself whole and healthy; second, to kill the other fellow. To the end that he may do these two perfectly simple things, he has to carry about eighty pounds of weight all the time.
===Chinese===
从卡巴雷·鲁日（Cabaret Rouge）交通壕出来头一性行军，我们足足走了十英里光景，到一个叫维利耶迪布瓦的地方。在那之前，我从来没能真正体会，全副重装备上路到底意味着什么。

行军路上，我常拿自己的命，同那些中世纪就在法兰西北部这些田地里打过仗的士兵比。

中世纪的骑士从头到脚裹得像个五金铺子，我估摸着，那一身盔甲少说有百把磅。可人家骑着马，身后还缀着个侍从之类的勤务角色，驮着让他舒坦受用的物什，专等仗打完了好伺候他。

现代士兵打仗，几乎没人帮衬。说实在的，与其说他求人相助，倒不如说他更常在帮别人的忙。士兵有两项基本职能：第一，保全自己，健健康康；第二，干掉对方。为了干好这两件再简单不过的事，他得时时刻刻背着八十磅上下的分量。

===Original===
He has a blanket, a waterproof sheet, a greatcoat, extra boots, extra underwear, a haversack with iron rations, entrenching tools, a bayonet, a water bottle, a mess kit, a rifle, two hundred fifty rounds of ammo, a tin hat, two gas helmets, and a lot of miscellaneous small junk. All this is draped, hung, and otherwise disposed over his figure by means of a web harness having more hooks than a hatrack. He parallels the old-time knight only in the matter of the steel helmet and the rifle, which, with the bayonet, corresponds to the lance, sword, and battle-ax, three in one.

The modern soldier carries all his worldly goods with him all the time. He hates to hike. But he has to.

I remember very vividly that first day. The temperature was around 90°, and some fool officers had arranged that we start at one﻿—the very worst time of the day.
